The caution that it brings forth only the concept's form, the content being drawn from intuition, agrees with Kant on empirical concepts. It leaves open the a priori content of the categories, which §§11–12 have to face.

**¶13, 30.** In producing the form of the concept the understanding helps "set forth" the content of the object; in this "setting" the "re-presenting [*Vor-stellen*] of thinking reveals itself"; the essence of the understanding is "co-determined" by its character of "from out of itself" (spontaneity), "but without getting to the heart of the matter". Heidegger here contests a definition Kant gives repeatedly. For Kant spontaneity is what the understanding is: "the spontaneity of cognition" (A 51/B 75), "spontaneity of concepts" (A 50/B 74), and the "I think" is "an act of spontaneity" (B 132). Heidegger grants it as true and secondary. The core will be the understanding's dependence on the imagination and on time (§§29, 34). The hyphenated *Vor-stellen* prepares that answer: representing as setting-before becomes the letting-stand-against of §16. Kant's chief defining mark of the understanding is demoted in a subordinate clause.

**¶14–¶15, 30–31.** From the structure of knowing to the knowable: if finite knowledge takes things in stride, the knowable must show itself, as "appearance"; "only for finite knowledge is there anything at all like an object"; infinite knowing lets the being stand forth and has it "as a thing which stands forth [*als Ent-stand*]", "the being as being in itself, i.e., not as object". The coinage *Ent-stand* sets against Kant's *Gegenstand* a correlate of infinite intuition for which Kant has no name. Kant's usage runs the other way: he speaks of the object of an intellectual intuition. A noumenon "in a **positive** sense" would be "an object of a non-sensible intuition" (B 307), and the divine understanding is one "through whose representation the objects would themselves at the same time be given, or produced" (B 145). Heidegger corrects Kant's vocabulary, as he says outright three paragraphs later: "Strictly speaking, one should not speak of 'Object'". The correction presents itself as clarification. It removes from Kant the possibility that the thing in itself is a determinate object we lack the means to know. A marginal note writes "Objectivity is Being [*Seyn*]! in the empirical sense"; it belongs to the same retrospective voice as the 1930s note before the book.

**¶16, 31.** The being "in the appearance" is "the same being as the being in itself, and this alone", revealed according to the manner and scope of finite taking-in and determining. A later marginal note narrows the claim: the sameness is that of "the That of the X", not of the What. No Kant locus is cited. Kant's text supports and resists the claim in roughly equal measure. B xxvii supports it (unit ¶19). Elsewhere Kant speaks of a "non-sensible cause" of appearances (A 494/B 522) and of a "transcendental object ... which might be the ground of this appearance that we call matter" (A 277/B 333). Those formulas suggest a relation of ground between two terms, and Heidegger reads them all in the light of B xxvii.

**¶17, 31–32.** "Appearance" has a wider sense, phenomena as a kind of object (A 235 heading, A 249), and a narrower one, the content of affection stripped of determination: "The undetermined object of an empirical intuition is called appearance" (A 20/B 34); to appear is "to be an Object of empirical intuition" (A 89/B 121). The quotations are exact. The A 89/B 121 phrase stands in Kant's argument that "an object can appear to us only by means of such pure forms of sensibility, i.e., be an object of empirical intuition", a sentence about the forms of sensibility, not about finitude. A 249 belongs to a part of the chapter on phenomena and noumena that Kant cut in B. Heidegger cites it without saying so, and it gives him the wider sense he wants.

**¶18, 32.** Appearances "are not mere illusion [*Schein*]" but the being itself, not something other than the thing in itself; the double characterization corresponds to the two relations, "the being in the standing-forth [*Entstand*] and the same being as object [*Gegenstand*]". The first half is Kant's: to say that objects appear "is not to say that these objects would be a mere **illusion**", and "this object as **appearance** is to be distinguished from itself as object **in itself**" (B 69, a passage added in B). The second clause also gives Heidegger his "same being". The second half grounds the distinction in two kinds of intuition. Kant grounds it in the ideality of space and time as forms of our sensibility, from which the restriction of cognition to appearances follows (A 26/B 42; B xxv–xxvi). For Kant the infinite intuition is the illustration of B 72; for Heidegger it is the ground. That is the most consequential re-inscription in §5, and it explains why the Aesthetic's own proof of ideality plays no part in Heidegger's account of the thing in itself.

**¶19, 32–33.** Kant says the *Critique* teaches that "the Object is to be taken in a *twofold sense*, namely, as appearance and as thing in itself" (B xxvii); strictly, absolute knowledge has no objects; the *Opus postumum* says the thing in itself "is not another Object, but is rather another aspect (*respectus*) of the representation of *the same Object*" (Adickes's edition, not in the corpus; Heidegger's emphasis). B xxvii is quoted correctly. Kant's sentence goes on to apply the double meaning to the freedom of the will (B xxvii–xxviii), which is its purpose in the B preface. The *Opus postumum* is the late Kant of the 1790s, and Heidegger uses it to fix the sense of the *Critique*: Kant's development is treated as one position, and a late formula decides a disputed point. A marginal note quoting another *Opus postumum* passage, found through Jancke's critique of Heidegger, reinforces the reading; it is later than the text. The one-being reading has since become a standard position in Kant scholarship, and B xxvii supports it. What is not Kant's is the ground Heidegger gives it, the two intuitions of the previous unit.

**¶20, 33–34.** "Behind the appearance" means that finite knowledge "necessarily conceals at the same time", in advance, so that the thing in itself is "absolutely inaccessible to such knowledge by its very essence"; "According to the *Critique*, everything in an appearance is itself again appearance" (reply to Eberhard, not in the corpus). Concealment is Heidegger's term, and it anticipates the doctrine of untruth (§35). Kant's corresponding claim is epistemic and has no vocabulary of concealing: we have "nothing to do with anything except appearances anywhere (in the world of sense), even in the deepest research into its objects" (A 45/B 62–63), and the thing in itself is "uncognized by us" (B xx). Heidegger turns Kant's restriction of cognition into a structure of finite knowing that hides as it reveals.

**¶21, 34.** To prove by "positivist critique" that things in themselves are unknowable misunderstands the concept, since such proofs treat the thing in itself as an object of finite knowledge that happens to be inaccessible; "mere appearance" denies only that the being is known infinitely; Kant is quoted from A 45/B 62–63, exactly. Kant's own sentence supports the reading: we believe we cognize things in themselves only when "our transcendental distinction is lost" (B 62–63), so the inaccessibility is not a fact to be shown by experience. Heidegger reconstructs faithfully here. The target, "positivist critique", is Kant's nineteenth-century reception, not Kant.

**¶22, 34–35.** The double meaning of "outside us" (A 373) shows the difference: as thing in itself the being is outside us because we lack infinite intuition, as appearance because we are not it and yet have access to it; appearance and thing in itself are not "two classifications of objects arranged one behind the other". The citation is to the fourth paralogism of the first edition, which Kant replaced in B by the Refutation of Idealism (B 274–279). Heidegger cites the A Paralogisms as Kant's view without noting that B dropped them. In Kant the double meaning serves an argument against the empirical idealist, who confuses things outside us in the transcendental sense with things found in space (A 373). Heidegger reads a distinction made to refute the idealist as a distinction between two relations to one being.

**¶23, 35.** The characterization of finitude has indicated the dimension of the laying of the ground and the direction of the regress. A transition. In §5 the finitude of §4's thesis has absorbed Kant's distinction of appearance and thing in itself, which now rests on finitude rather than on the forms of sensibility.

##### §6. The Source-Ground of the Laying of the Ground for Metaphysics

**¶1, 35–36.** The understanding depends on intuition: "For we can understand nothing except what one of our words brings with it corresponding to something in intuition" (A 277/B 333). When Kant says "Neither of these qualities {sensibility and understanding} is to be preferred to the other" (A 51/B 75), this seems to contradict the primacy of intuition; but the belonging-together "does not exclude but rather includes an order of precedence in the structural grounding of thinking in intuition", which must not be levelled into "an indifferent correlation of content and form". Heidegger confronts the passage that tells against him and reads it so that his primacy stands. Kant's reasons for parity are both claims of indispensability: "Without sensibility no object would be given to us, and without understanding none would be thought. Thoughts without content are empty, intuitions without concepts are blind" (A 51/B 75). Commentary R judges parity of indispensability and precedence in grounding compatible, and on Kant's stated reasons they are. The A 277/B 333 sentence is used out of its setting: Kant says it of the "transcendental object" behind matter, "a mere something, about which we would not understand what it is even if someone could tell us". It is a sentence about the limits of understanding, enlisted here for the understanding's dependence on intuition, which it also implies.

**¶2, 36.** One might stop at the duality of elements, the more so since Kant orients the "springing forth" of cognition on "two basic sources of the mind", the receptivity of impressions and the spontaneity of concepts (A 50/B 74), and says that "other than these two sources of knowledge" we have "no others" (A 294/B 350). Both quotations are accurate. The second stands in Kant's theory of error: "because we have no other sources of cognition besides these two, it follows that error is effected only through the unnoticed influence of sensibility on understanding" (A 294/B 350). The Third Section will add a third source, the imagination, which Kant himself counts among the sources in the A-Deduction (A 94, A 115). A 294/B 350 is therefore among the strongest Kantian texts against the book's later thesis, and Heidegger quotes it here, before the thesis is in play.

**¶3, 36.** The duality is no juxtaposition: "Only through their union can knowledge spring forth" (A 51/B 75–76); the unity of their union is no later result, but "what unites them, this 'synthesis,' must let the elements in their belonging-together and their oneness spring forth"; hence naming the two sources already points to their source-ground, "an original unity". The quotation stops at the end of Kant's sentence, and the next sentence goes the other way: "But on this account one must not mix up their roles, rather one has great cause to separate them carefully from each other and distinguish them" (A 51–52/B 76). From the necessity of union Kant draws the duty of separation, and on it he rests the division of aesthetic from logic. Heidegger draws from it the priority of a unifying ground. The inference that what unites must precede and generate what it unites is Heidegger's; at this point Kant's text supports only that cognition needs both. The omitted sentence is the conclusion Kant drew from the premise Heidegger quotes.

**¶4, 37.** At the beginning and the end of the *Critique* Kant goes beyond enumeration: the "two stems of human knowledge ... which perhaps spring forth from a common, but to us unknown, root" (A 15/B 29), and the point "where the general root of our cognitive power divides and branches out into two stems, one of which is **reason**", reason meaning "the entire higher faculty of cognition", so that "I therefore contrast the rational to the empirical" (A 835/B 863). The empirical here, Heidegger adds, is "the experiencing taking of things in stride: receptivity, sensibility as such". Both quotations are exact in Heidegger's German; Taft's copy renders the second as "common root", Kemp Smith's phrase, where Kant wrote "general root" (*allgemeine Wurzel*), and Heidegger's next paragraph turns on the difference. The gloss on "the empirical" is Heidegger's. Kant's next paragraph explains his contrast as one between kinds of cognition, "historical" cognition *ex datis* and "rational" cognition *ex principiis* (A 836/B 864). The architectonic's two stems are kinds of cognition, not sensibility and understanding as faculties. The two pairs line up loosely, and Heidegger's identification of the empirical with receptivity makes them line up exactly.

**¶5, 37.** In the first passage the root is qualified by "perhaps", in the second it "is reputed to exist"; in both it is only pointed to; Kant does not pursue it and calls it "unknown to us"; hence his laying of the ground "goes into and points consciously toward the unknown. It is a philosophizing laying of the ground for Philosophy." The observation about "perhaps" is exact: A 835 has no "perhaps". The inference changes the role of the unknown. Kant mentions the root in the Introduction to justify an order of exposition, sensibility before understanding, "since the conditions under which alone the objects of human cognition are given precede those under which those objects are thought" (A 15–16/B 29–30). "Unknown to us" marks a limit Kant does not propose to cross. Heidegger turns the limit into a direction. That is an extension in form and a contestation in substance, since the Third Section will name what Kant called unknown: Kant's "unknown" becomes Heidegger's "not yet unveiled".

#### II. The Manner of the Unveiling of the Origin

##### §7. The Outline of the Stages in the Laying of the Ground for Ontology

**¶1, 38.** The grounding of metaphysics is the projection of the inner possibility of a priori synthesis; the account of finite knowledge and of the basic sources has given the question "more precision" and made it "more complex". A transition. "Projection of the inner possibility" now stands where Kant's question of how synthetic judgments a priori are possible stood in §3.

**¶2, 38.** Since knowledge of beings rests on a prior knowledge of their constitution of being, and finite knowledge is receptive, finite knowledge "requires a knowing which does not take things in stride (and which is apparently nonfinite), such as a 'creative' intuiting". The paradox is Heidegger's formulation of a problem Kant poses in other terms. Kant asks how an intuition can precede its objects: "how can an outer intuition inhabit the mind that precedes the objects themselves, and in which the concept of the latter can be determined *a priori*?", and answers, "Obviously not otherwise than insofar as it has its seat merely in the subject, as its formal constitution for being affected by objects" (B 41). Kant's answer makes the a priori a form of receptivity, and no creative intuition is needed. Heidegger's statement of the problem brings back the creative intuition Kant's answer had made unnecessary, so that §§28–33 can show how a finite being is formative without being creative.

**¶3, 38–39.** "How can a finite creature ... know, i.e., intuit, prior to all [instances of] taking the being in stride, without being its 'creator?' In other words: how must this finite creature be with respect to the constitution of its own Being so that such a bringing-forward of the constitution of the Being of beings which is free from experience, i.e., an ontological synthesis, is possible?" The second question rewrites Kant's answer at B 41. Kant's "formal constitution" of the subject becomes "the constitution of its own Being" (*Seinsverfassung*), and the question is no longer what forms the subject has but what kind of being the subject is. This is the hinge from Kant's question to fundamental ontology, and it is set as a question.

**¶4, 39.** A complication: since all finite knowledge is itself a synthesis, the a priori synthesis "is not identical with the above-named veritative synthesis, which is concerned solely with ontic knowledge". No Kant locus. The distinctions of §5 are now applied to Kant's a priori synthesis, which must be a synthesis of pure elements.

**¶5, 39.** Hence the order: the pure elements, pure intuition and pure thinking; their essential unity, "the pure veritative synthesis", which must also determine pure intuition a priori, so that the concepts belonging to it spring forth prior to all experience in content as well as form; hence "the question concerning the essence of the 'ontological predicate' must shift to the center". The order follows the *Critique*'s: Aesthetic, then the clue to the categories, then their deduction. "Ontological predicate" is Heidegger's name for the category; Kant reserves "predicables" for the pure derivative concepts (A 82/B 108). The requirement that the categories' content be a priori is Kant's: the understanding "brings a transcendental content into its representations", which are therefore called pure concepts "that pertain to objects *a priori*; this can never be accomplished by general logic" (A 79/B 105).

**¶6, 39.** The question presses further back to the ground of the pure synthesis; only then does it appear how ontological knowledge conditions ontic knowledge, and "the full essence of ontological truth is circumscribed". No Kant locus. The "full essence of ontological truth" will be §24's reading of the highest principle of synthetic judgments (A 154–158/B 193–197) together with transcendental truth (A 146/B 185).

**¶7, 39–40.** Five stages: the essential elements of pure knowledge; their essential unity; the inner possibility of that unity, the ontological synthesis; the ground of that possibility; the full determination of the essence of ontological knowledge. The five stages are not Kant's divisions; they redistribute the Analytic. The first takes the Aesthetic and the clue to the categories, the second §10 of the clue (A 76–79/B 102–105), the third the transcendental deduction, chiefly in A, the fourth the Schematism, and the fifth the highest principle of synthetic judgments. The Analytic of Principles beyond its highest principle, the chapter on phenomena and noumena and the whole Dialectic fall outside them. Kant's architectonic puts the Schematism at the threshold of the "transcendental doctrine of the power of judgment" (A 136–137/B 175–176). Heidegger makes it the ground. The selection is announced: the stages are introduced as the "inner movement" behind Kant's arrangement (43). It is the book's largest editorial act on Kant's text.

##### §8. The Method for Revealing the Origin

**¶1, 40.** The preliminary account showed a wealth of syntheses; the pure veritative synthesis contains the idea of a knowledge that "appears to be nonfinite"; and the indication of the source-ground "leads to the unknown". A recapitulation. "Leads to the unknown" restates §6's reading of Kant's "unknown to us" as a direction, and the method is built on it.

**¶2, 40.** The method remains undetermined at first; the region is the human "mind" (*mens sive animus*), whose opening would fall to "psychology" and, since knowledge is taken as judging, to "logic"; the two divide the task or fight for preeminence. *Gemüt*, mind, is Kant's word (A 19/B 33; A 50/B 74). The division of labour between logic and psychology is the dispute of Kant's neo-Kantian reception, and Kant himself banishes empirical psychology from metaphysics (A 848–849/B 876–877). Heidegger states a problem of the reception in Kant's vocabulary.

**¶3, 40–41.** Given the originality of what Kant sought and the unfitness of the traditional disciplines, it is "hopeless" to grasp the essential by a "logical" or "psychological" questioning; "'Transcendental Psychology' merely expresses a perplexity". Kant's own use of the label is pointed. In the first-edition Paralogisms "transcendental psychology" is one of three "putative sciences of pure reason", with cosmology and theology (A 396–397, first edition only): the illusory rational psychology the Dialectic takes apart. Heidegger turns the label against interpreters who read the *Critique*'s analysis of the faculties as transcendental psychology. Kant also distinguishes his deduction from Locke's "physiological derivation" (A 86–87/B 118–119) and says that the subjective side of the deduction, though "of great importance in respect of my chief end", "does not belong essentially to it" (A xvi–xvii). That last passage matters in §31, where Heidegger argues that the subjective deduction is the essential one. Here he joins Kant in refusing psychology; in §31 he will refuse the reason Kant gave for keeping the subjective side subordinate.

**¶4, 41.** The method is left open, mindful of what Kant wrote to Marcus Herz after finishing the *Critique*: "This sort of inquiry will always remain difficult" (letter of 1781, not in the corpus). Taft's note supplies the rest of Kant's sentence: "for it includes the metaphysics of metaphysics". Heidegger stops before the clause that would have given his reading its strongest Kantian support, the *Critique* as a metaphysics of metaphysics. For once the cut removes support rather than resistance.

**¶5, 41.** The procedure can be called "analytic" in the broadest sense; Kant calls the *Critique* "a 'study of our inner nature'" (A 703/B 731), and "This revealing of the essence of human Dasein is 'to the philosophers, however, even a duty.'" Kant's sentence says what the study is: "the resolution of all our transcendental cognition into its elements (as a study of our inner nature) not only has in itself no small value, but is even a duty for a philosopher" (A 703/B 731; the German of both editions in the corpus reads "transcendent", where Guyer and Wood print "transcendental"). Three shifts occur. Kant's study of inner nature is a resolution into elements, which Heidegger's next paragraph denies analytic is. It stands at the end of the Dialectic, where it serves to expose the illusions of reason, not to ground ontology. And "our inner nature" becomes "the essence of human Dasein". A later marginal note points the method toward transcendental reflection (A 262–263/B 319), the Amphiboly once more.

**¶6, 41–42.** "Analytic" does not mean breaking finite pure reason into elements, "but rather the reverse: an 'unknotting' as a freeing which loosens the seeds [*Keime*] of ontology"; in Kant's words, it brings "to light through reason" "what reason brings forth entirely from out of itself" (A xx); analytic becomes "a letting-be-seen [*Sehenlassen*] of the genesis of the essence of finite pure reason from its proper ground". The word in scare quotes, *Auflösen*, is Kant's word from the sentence just quoted, *Auflösung ... in ihre Elemente* (A 703/B 731), and Heidegger reverses its sense: a resolution into elements becomes a loosening that lets a genesis be seen. The A xx quotation comes from Kant's claim of completeness: the *Critique* is "nothing but the **inventory** of all we possess through **pure reason,** ordered systematically", from which nothing can escape, "because what reason brings forth entirely out of itself cannot be hidden" (A xx). Kant's sentence guarantees an inventory; Heidegger's makes it a genesis. Kant does describe his Analytic as "the much less frequently attempted **analysis of the faculty of understanding** itself" rather than of concepts (A 65/B 90), the best support Heidegger has for an analytic that does not break things into elements. He quotes the phrase only later, at the end of §11 (55), and not here, where it would count against Kant's own word *Auflösung*. "Letting-be-seen" is *Being and Time*'s definition of phenomenology (§7). The *Critique*'s method has been renamed phenomenology.

**¶7, 42.** Such analytic is "the projecting anticipation of the entire inner essence of finite pure reason", a construction of its foundations that brings metaphysics to the ground in which it is rooted as "a 'haunting' of human nature" (*Heimsuchung*, from B xv). Kant's verb at B xv is *heimgesucht*: "Why then has nature afflicted our reason with the restless striving for such a path, as if it were one of reason's most important occupations?" Kant asks it as a question about metaphysics' failure to become a science. Heidegger makes the affliction the soil in which metaphysics is rooted. "Construction" names his own method, which §43 defends against the charge of arbitrariness. Kant's own answer to the B xv question is the doctrine of the ideas of reason in the Dialectic, and it plays no part here.

The first part of the Second Section makes three Kantian texts load-bearing: the first sentence of the Aesthetic (A 19/B 33), the contrast of derivative and original intuition (B 71–72, B 135–145), and the root of the two stems (A 15/B 29, A 835/B 863). The reading holds against Kant's text on four points: the receptive definition of sensibility (A 19/B 33; B 146–147), the mediateness of judgment (A 68/B 93), the inaccessibility of things in themselves as a consequence of the transcendental distinction rather than an empirical fact (B 62–63), and the one-being reading of appearance and thing in itself (B xxvii). It does not hold where Heidegger turns Kant's illustration into a ground. The intuitus originarius, which Kant counts "only as an illustration ... and not as a ground of its proof" (B 72), becomes the measure of finitude and the ground of the distinction between appearance and thing in itself. Kant's contrast of an intuiting understanding (B 135, B 145) becomes a difference located "primarily" in intuition. The selection of the First Section continues and sharpens. Heidegger omits the sentence after "Only from their unification can cognition arise", which commands separation (A 51–52/B 76). He omits the definition of the "study of our inner nature" as resolution into elements (A 703/B 731) and then inverts its word. He restates Kant's ground for the a priori, the formal constitution of the subject (B 41), as the constitution of being of a finite creature. And he reads the B text two ways, as clarifying A at B 33 and, as §31 will show, as Kant's retreat. Much here is announced as Heidegger's own: the thesis of finitude is a summary of "presuppositions", the five stages an "inner movement" behind Kant's arrangement, the unknown root a direction. The quotations are again exact.

### B. The Stages of the Execution of the Projection of the Inner Possibility of Ontology

**¶1, 42–43.** The guiding problem is restated: "How can finite human Dasein pass beyond (transcend) the being in advance when this being is not only something it did not create itself, but something at which it must be directed in order to exist as Dasein?" The problem of the transcendental is "the synthesis which constitutes transcendence"; put otherwise, "How must the finite being that we call 'human being' be according to its innermost essence so that in general it can be open to a being that it itself is not and that therefore must be able to show itself from itself?" The two questions finish the substitution begun in §3. Kant's "transcendental" is now defined as "the synthesis which constitutes transcendence", and transcendence is Dasein's passing beyond beings toward them. Kant reserves passing beyond for the transcendent, the illegitimate use of principles beyond experience (A 296/B 352–353). In Kant's text the a priori synthesis concerns objects of possible experience and stops at their limit. Here it is the movement by which a finite being is open to beings at all. "Dasein" enters the question at the point where Kant's subject, the "formal constitution" of the mind (B 41), is asked about its "innermost essence".

**¶2, 43.** The stages will be run through without exhausting everything equally: "We thereby follow the inner movement of the Kantian ground-laying, but without holding to his particular arrangement and the formulation therein", in order to assess "the appropriateness, the validity, and the limits of the external architectonic". The licence is announced, and it is the reverse of Kant's own claim about his architectonic. Kant holds that a system ordered by contingent aims has only "**technical** unity", while one ordered from an idea has architectonic unity (A 833/B 861), and that the *Critique*'s plan is drawn "architectonically, i.e., from principles" (A 13/B 27). For Kant the arrangement is the principle. Heidegger separates the "inner movement" from the "external architectonic", and the separation lets him judge Kant's arrangement by a movement he himself reads. Kant's own warning against judging a science by its founder's description (A 834/B 862) supports the separation, though Heidegger does not cite it here.

#### The First Stage of the Laying of the Ground: The Essential Elements of Pure Knowledge

**¶1, 43.** "As knowing, the transcendental synthesis must be an intuition and, as *a priori* knowing, it must be a pure intuition. As pure knowing which belongs to human finitude, pure intuition must necessarily be determined through a pure thinking." The order is Kant's: "the conditions under which alone the objects of human cognition are given precede those under which those objects are thought" (A 15–16/B 29–30). The ground of the order is Heidegger's: transcendental synthesis is first an intuition because §4 made knowing "primarily intuiting". Kant's reason is that objects must be given before they are thought. Heidegger's is that intuition is the essence of knowing.

##### a) Pure Intuition in Finite Knowing

###### §9. The Elucidation of Space and Time as Pure Intuitions

**¶1, 44.** Is there pure intuiting in finite knowing? What is sought is "an immediate, although experience-free, allowing of an individual to be encountered"; since what is taken in stride in knowing being is no being at hand, "the pure representing which takes things in stride must give itself something capable of being represented. Pure intuition, therefore, must be in a certain sense 'creative.'" The scare quotes concede what Kant's B 72 had said: our intuition, pure intuition included, is "derived ... not original", and creative intuition "seems to pertain only to the original being". Kant has his own answer to how a receptive intuition can precede its objects: it is the subject's "formal constitution for being affected by objects" (B 41), a form of receptivity that gives nothing. Heidegger's "must give itself something" makes pure intuition give a content, a "something represented". Kant's pure intuition is a form in which a manifold is given. Heidegger's is a giving of the form itself. The difference is slight here and will decide §28, where pure intuition becomes an act of the imagination.

**¶2, 44.** What pure intuition represents is "no being (no object [*Gegenstand*], i.e., no appearing being), but at the same time it is plainly not nothing". Kant says the same of pure space and time in his table of the concept of nothing: "The mere form of intuition, without substance, is in itself not an object, but the merely formal condition of one (as appearance), like pure space and pure time, which are to be sure something, as the forms for intuiting, but are not in themselves objects that are intuited (*ens imaginarium*)" (A 291/B 347). Heidegger's "not nothing" restates Kant's "to be sure something" without citing it here. He cites the passage in §28 (143), where Kant's name for the status, *ens imaginarium*, imaginary being, becomes his evidence that pure intuition is "pure imagination".

**¶3, 44.** Kant posits space and time as pure intuitions; space first, how it manifests itself in finite knowledge of beings. A transition. "Manifests itself" (*bekundet*) puts space in the vocabulary of showing; Kant's question in the metaphysical exposition is what belongs to the concept of space (A 22–23/B 37–38).

**¶4–¶5, 44–45.** Kant gives each phenomenon a negative and then a positive characterization; the negative beginning is no accident, since what is to be grasped positively is "already known—and known essentially—in advance, even though it is not yet recognized but rather is misunderstood"; space is not found "here" or "there", is not a thing, not an "empirical representation", but must be represented in advance as that "within which" what is at hand is encountered. The positive claims are Kant's: "Space is not an empirical concept that has been drawn from outer experiences", and "Space is a necessary representation, *a priori*, which is the ground of all outer intuitions" (A 23–24/B 38–39). The account of the negative form is Heidegger's. Kant's negations answer rival theories: that space is drawn from experience, and the Newtonian and Leibnizian views he names when he draws his conclusions (A 39–40/B 56–57). Heidegger's negations answer a phenomenon too familiar to be noticed, the pattern of *Being and Time*'s treatment of world. The marginal notes (later) register dissatisfaction with Kant's second argument while accepting its conclusion.

**¶6, 45–46.** Since space "applies to every" spatial relation it looks like a concept; but it is no "discursive" representation; its unity is that of "a unique one"; "The many spaces are only limitations of the one, unique space", and "even the limiting limits themselves" are spatial; representing space is "immediate representing of a unified particular, i.e., intuition". A faithful reconstruction of Kant's third argument: "one can only represent a single space, and if one speaks of many spaces, one understands by that only parts of one and the same unique space", and "It is essentially single; the manifold in it ... rests merely on limitations" (A 24–25/B 39). The closing equation of intuition with *repraesentatio singularis* is Kant's from the ladder of representations (A 320/B 377). The point that limits are themselves spatial is Heidegger's addition, and it is consistent with Kant.

**¶7, 46–47.** Pure intuition gives its object "immediately as a whole": "Space is represented as an infinite given magnitude" (A 25, B 39); magnitude means "extensiveness" (*Großheit*), which first makes quantities possible (Reflexion 5846, not in the corpus); space does not have the particulars "under itself", like a concept, but "in itself"; such representing "allows what is representable in it to spring forth, and in this sense it is called an 'original' representing" (A 32/B 48; B 40). Two things happen. Heidegger quotes the first-edition wording, *unendliche Größe gegeben*, and argues with the second edition's reasoning: only B contrasts the concept, which "contains these **under itself**", with space, which contains an infinite set of representations "**within itself**" (B 40). The A argument is different: a general concept of space "can determine nothing in respect to magnitude" (A 25). The citation "A 25 (B 39)" covers a blend. And "original" changes sense. Kant calls the representations of space and time "original" because they are not reached by limiting something prior: "The original representation, time, must therefore be given as unlimited" (A 32/B 48); "the original representation of space is an *a priori* intuition, not a concept" (B 40). The word means underived. Kant uses the same word at B 72 for the intuition our minds do not have, *intuitus originarius*. Heidegger glosses "original" as "allows ... to spring forth", which joins the two Kantian senses Kant kept apart. Our pure intuition is underived in the first sense and, Kant insists, not original in the second.

**¶8, 47.** Pure intuition has its intuited, but gives it only in intuiting; the intuited is not a being at hand and not thematically grasped; "In a preliminary glimpse [*Vorblick*], what is intuited in pure intuition stands without reference to a particular object and is unthematic"; what is intuited in this "way of intuiting" is "not simply nothing". The unthematic "preliminary glimpse" is Heidegger's phenomenology of space as it is lived in "being busy with the things" (the circumspection of *Being and Time* §§22–24). Kant's pure intuition is not described as a glimpse at all, but as a form "in the mind *a priori*" (A 20/B 34) and as the ground of geometry: in A, "The apodictic certainty of all geometrical principles" is "grounded in this *a priori* necessity" (A 24); in B, "geometrical propositions are all apodictic" (B 41). Heidegger's account says nothing of mathematics, which in Kant's transcendental exposition is the proof that space is a pure intuition (B 40–41). This is selection: the pure intuition without its science.

**¶9, 47.** Further elucidation requires showing "in which sense pure intuition is 'original,' i.e., how it allows what is intuited in it to spring forth". A deferral to §28. The question is posed in Heidegger's sense of "original"; in Kant's sense it has been answered: the representation is not derived from limitation (A 32/B 48).

###### §10. Time as the Universal Pure Intuition

**¶1, 48.** Space gives only the relations of outer sense; the givens of "inner sense" show no spatial shape but a "succession of states of our mind (representations, drives, moods)"; hence time is "the form of inner sense, i.e., of the intuition of our self and our inner state" (A 33/B 49); it determines "the relation of representations in our inner state"; and "time cannot be a determination of outer appearances; it belongs neither to a shape or a position, etc." (A 33/B 49–50). Working rendering for the paraphrase, which the corpus copy of Taft garbles; the Kant quotations are exact. "Drives, moods" are Heidegger's additions to Kant's "inner state". The addition foreshadows the Fourth Section, where moods belong to Dasein's disclosedness.

**¶2, 48–49.** Next to the assignment of space and time to two regions stands the thesis "Time is the *a priori* formal condition of all appearances in general" (A 34/B 50); time therefore has "a priority over space" and must become the leading element of the pure knowledge that "forms transcendence". The thesis is Kant's. The priority is Kant's in one respect: time is the "immediate condition" of inner and the "mediate condition of outer appearances" (A 34/B 50–51). It is not a priority in the order of the *Critique*, which treats space first and whose Analogies and Refutation of Idealism need space for the determination of time (B 274–279, B 291–292). Heidegger draws from a priority of scope a priority of ground. The Kant of the second edition, who argues that the persistent thing time-determination presupposes is perceivable "only through a thing outside me" (B 275), points the other way. That text is B-only and Heidegger does not engage it.

**¶3, 49.** Time will move more and more to the centre through the stages and reveal its essence "in a more original way" than the Aesthetic's provisional characterization can. A promise. It contests the Aesthetic's own account of time as complete for its purpose, and the Schematism chapter supports the promise: there time takes on a role the Aesthetic did not give it, as the medium of the schemata (A 138–139/B 177–178).

**¶4–¶5, 49.** Kant denies time-determination to outer appearances, though everyday experience finds time in the stars and in growth and decay; yet he does not deny it outright if time is the condition of all appearances; "One thesis denies within-time-ness to physical beings which are at hand, the other grants it"; the restriction of time to inner sense is in fact an extension, since among representations are those "which, as representations, also allow beings to be encountered". "Within-time-ness" is the term of *Being and Time* §§80–81 for the ordinary concept of time. The appeal to the heavens recalls Aristotle and Plato on time, not Kant. Heidegger presents an apparent contradiction in Kant's text and resolves it as Kant does: time is the immediate condition of inner and the mediate condition of outer appearances (A 34/B 50–51). The reconstruction is faithful in result.

**¶6, 49–50.** Kant's argument runs from the within-time-ness of representing to that of the represented; it "becomes essentially facilitated for Kant through the ambiguity of the expression 'intuition,' or rather 'representation,'" which means both states of mind and what those states have as objects. This is contestation, and Kant's text partly meets it. Kant's sentence marks the distinction Heidegger says he exploits: "all representations, whether or not they have outer things as their object, nevertheless as determinations of the mind themselves belong to the inner state" (A 34/B 50). Kant draws only the conclusion that time is "the mediate condition of outer appearances". The step Heidegger finds facile is the step from the representation's being in time to the represented's being in time. Kant's word "mediate" names that step without arguing for it. Heidegger's objection has force, but it does not catch Kant in an unnoticed ambiguity; it catches him in an unargued inference he labels.

**¶7, 50.** Whether this grounding of time's universality is valid, and whether space is thereby displaced from a central ontological function, "must here remain open". The question returns in §35, where Heidegger concedes that space too belongs to transcendence and leaves the matter. The concession here keeps Kant's argument from carrying the weight it will later be given.

**¶8, 50.** If time's universality can be grounded at all, then because time "dwells in the subject in a more original way than space"; and time is ontologically more universal only "if the subjectivity of the subject exists in the openness for the being. The more subjective time is, the more original and extensive is the expansiveness [*Entschränkung*] of the subject." The thesis is Heidegger's. Kant's time is subjective as the form of inner sense, and he draws from its subjectivity the opposite conclusion: "Time is therefore merely a subjective condition of our (human) intuition ... and in itself, outside the subject, is nothing. Nonetheless it is necessarily objective in regard to all appearances" (A 35/B 51). For Kant subjectivity restricts. Heidegger reverses the direction: the more subjective, the less restricted. The reversal depends on redefining subjectivity as openness to beings, which is *Being and Time*'s being-in-the-world, not Kant's mind.

**¶9, 50–51.** The universal function Kant gives time can be justified only if time itself "forces us to determine the essence of subjectivity in a more original way". The demand looks ahead to §34. Kant does not accept it; for him the subjectivity of time is settled in the Aesthetic and refined in the B-Deduction's doctrine of self-affection (B 67–68, B 153–156), which Heidegger will read as the more original determination.

**¶10, 51.** The Transcendental Aesthetic's task is "to set forth the ontological *αἴσθησις* which makes it possible 'to disclose a priori' the Being of beings"; "one of the pieces required for the solution of the general problem of transcendental philosophy" (B 73) "(Ontology)" has been attained. Two quotations are bent. Kant's sentence at B 73 continues with its own apposition: "**how are synthetic *a priori* propositions possible?**" Heidegger replaces Kant's statement of the general problem with "(Ontology)". The phrase "to disclose a priori" comes from the same sentence. There it says that in a priori judgments we encounter in the intuition "that which is to be discovered *a priori* not in the concept but in the intuition that corresponds to it". What is discovered a priori is a predicate going beyond a concept, not "the Being of beings". B 73 is a B-only passage.

**¶11–¶12, 51.** Pure intuition must not be dissipated, nor can isolating one element show its function; the goal must be "the preservation and refining" of the Aesthetic's problem, not its elimination; next, pure thinking. The polemic is against the Marburg absorption of the Aesthetic into the Logic, which Cohen pursued. Here Heidegger defends a Kantian part of the *Critique* against Kant's reception, and his own later reading will transform the Aesthetic as thoroughly as the Marburg reading did, by making pure intuition an act of the imagination (§28).

##### b) Pure Thinking in Finite Knowing

###### §11. The Pure Concept of Understanding (Notion)

**¶1, 51–52.** Thinking, determining representing directed toward the intuited, determines the object as "such and such" in a "universal representation", the concept; "pure knowing is pure intuition through pure concepts". A reconstruction. Kant: cognition through the understanding is "cognition through concepts, not intuitive but discursive" (A 68/B 93), and pure cognition requires pure concepts that relate to objects a priori (A 79/B 105). "Pure intuition through pure concepts" compresses Kant's three pieces (A 78–79/B 104) into two, leaving the imagination's synthesis for §14.

**¶2, 52.** In representing a linden, beech or fir as a tree, the intuited is determined from what "applies for many"; but this applicability is derivative, grounded in the one in which several objects agree; the basic act of forming a concept is what Kant calls "Reflection", "the deliberation whereby various representations can be grasped in one consciousness"; comparison and abstraction follow; the concept is "a representation *insofar as it can be embodied in various [things]*", and "It is a mere tautology to speak of universal or common concepts" (Jäsche *Logic* §§1, 6, not in the corpus). The doctrine of concept-formation is taken from the *Logic* that Jäsche compiled from Kant's lecture notes in 1800, not from the *Critique*, which has none of comparison, reflection and abstraction in this sense. The *Critique*'s own term for the act that grounds concepts is "function": "By a function, however, I understand the unity of the action of ordering different representations under a common one" (A 68/B 93). Heidegger begins from the *Logic*'s reflection rather than the *Critique*'s function, and the choice shapes what follows: a reflection looks ahead to a unity, while a function orders representations under one. The first lends itself to the horizon of §§16–25, the second to the forms of judgment.

**¶3, 53.** Concepts are "reflected" representations; their conceptual form always springs from reflection, but their content mostly comes from empirical comparison, so "The origin of the content of such empirical concepts is hence no problem". Faithful to the *Logic*'s distinction between the form and the matter of concepts. The *Critique* agrees in substance: empirical concepts need no deduction "because we always have experience ready at hand to prove their objective reality" (A 84/B 116–117).

**¶4, 53.** A pure concept is a reflected representation whose content "essentially cannot be read from appearances"; concepts given a priori in content Kant calls "notions, *conceptus dati a priori*" (Jäsche *Logic* §4; A 320/B 377). The *Critique* passage defines the notion differently: "the pure concept, insofar as it has its origin solely in the understanding (not in a pure image of sensibility), is called *notio*" (A 320/B 377). Kant defines the notion by its origin in the understanding and by excluding the "pure image of sensibility". Both points matter later. The Third Section will deny that the understanding is the sole origin of the pure concept, and the Fourth Stage will make time the "pure image" (*reines Bild*) in which the schemata of the categories are formed (A 142/B 182). The *Critique*'s definition of the notion is the very contrast Heidegger's doctrine of the schema is built to undo.

**¶5, 53.** Are there such concepts? How can the understanding, "only an empty function of binding-together", give a content? If at all, then in "the basic act of concept-formation as such, in reflection". The objection states Kant's problem fairly. Kant answers it with the clue: the understanding brings "a transcendental content into its representations by means of the synthetic unity of the manifold in intuition in general" (A 79/B 105), through the same functions that give judgments their form. Heidegger's answer will place the content in reflection's look ahead to unity. Kant places it in the synthetic unity of an intuitive manifold, and so in the very passage (§10) that the Second Stage reads.

**¶6, 54.** Every judging includes the "unity of the act of ordering various representations under a common one" (A 68/B 93); this act is guided in advance by a look toward a unity; hence "representing of unity belongs to the essential structure of the basic act of understanding". Heidegger quotes Kant's definition of "function" and treats it as the definition of reflection. The quotation is exact, and in Kant it defines function, the ground of concepts in "the spontaneity of thinking" (A 68/B 93). Heidegger's inference, that unity is represented in advance in the act, has Kantian support he does not cite here. Kant writes that the representation of unity "cannot ... arise from the combination; rather, by being added to the representation of the manifold, it first makes the concept of combination possible", and that this unity "is not the former category of unity" but lies higher, in apperception (B 131). Kant puts the prior unity in apperception, Heidegger in the understanding's reflection. In the Third Stage he will put it in the imagination.

**¶7, 54.** "The essence of understanding is original comprehending or grasping"; the represented unities are the content of the pure concepts; the pure concept "is itself this form in an original sense". The thesis is Heidegger's construction, and it answers a question Kant answers otherwise. For Kant the categories are "concepts of an object in general, by means of which its intuition is regarded as **determined** with regard to one of the **logical functions** for judgments" (B 128). Their content is the determination of an intuition with respect to a function of judgment. Heidegger's "represented unities" have no reference to intuition yet, which is why the next section calls the notion a fragment.

**¶8, 54–55.** Pure concepts are not reflected but "reflecting concepts", "representations which act in, with, and for reflection"; a Kant note is quoted: there are concepts "the entire sense of which is to be nothing other than one reflection or another ... concepts of reflection (*conceptus reflectentes*)", grounds of the possibility of judging (Reflexion 5051, not in the corpus). The *Critique* uses the term "concepts of reflection" for something else and sets it against the categories. In the Amphiboly the concepts of reflection, identity and difference, agreement and opposition, inner and outer, matter and form, "are distinguished from categories by the fact that what is exhibited through them is not the object in accordance with what constitutes its concept (magnitude, reality), but rather only the comparison of representations ... which precedes the concepts of things" (A 269/B 325). Heidegger names the categories "reflecting concepts" on the strength of an unpublished note, against the published terminology. The marginal notes in his copy repeatedly point to the Amphiboly (above, Preface ¶1; §8 ¶5), as if the later Heidegger had seen where the collision lay.

**¶9, 55.** Hence there are pure concepts in the understanding, and "the 'analysis of the faculty of understanding itself'" (A 65/B 90) must bring them to light. Kant's phrase is quoted correctly (Guyer and Wood: "analysis of the faculty of understanding itself"). Kant's programme continues: to seek the a priori concepts "only in the understanding as their birthplace" (A 65–66/B 90–91). Heidegger takes the method and, by §12, withdraws the birthplace: the understanding alone cannot yield the categories.

###### §12. Notions as Ontological Predicates (Categories)

**¶1, 55.** The pure understanding yields a manifold of pure unities; if the ways of unification (judgments) form a closed whole, the understanding hides a system of pure concepts, the predicates that "state something about the Being of beings", the "categories"; "The Table of Judgments is thus the origin of the categories and their table." This is a reconstruction of Kant's claim, and Kant makes it in so many words. The division of the categories "is systematically generated from a common principle, namely the faculty for judging (which is the same as the faculty for thinking)" (A 80/B 106), and in the second edition "In the **metaphysical deduction** the origin of the *a priori* categories in general was established through their complete coincidence with the universal logical functions of thinking" (B 159). The one non-Kantian word is "ontological", and Heidegger's later marginal note asks of the identification "how so?".

**¶2, 55–56.** This origin is disputed: Kant does not develop the functions of judgment from the essence of the understanding but presents a finished table under four "primary moments" (*Logic* §20), and "whether and the extent to which these four moments are grounded in the essence of the understanding is likewise not indicated". Kant concedes the point in the B-Deduction: for why our understanding achieves the unity of apperception "only through precisely this kind and number" of categories, "a further ground may be offered just as little as one can be offered for why we have precisely these and no other functions for judgment" (B 145–146). Heidegger's objection has Kant's own admission behind it, though he does not cite it. A later marginal note points to Klaus Reich's 1932 defence of the table's completeness and to Heidegger's courses of 1929–32; it is retrospective.

**¶3, 56.** "Kant himself vacillates, calling it at times a 'transcendental table' (A 73/B 98), and at other times a 'logical table of judgments' (*Prolegomena* §21)", and the charge Kant brought against Aristotle's categories falls back on his own table. The "vacillation" is a distinction Kant draws and explains. His table differs from the logicians' by including singular and infinite judgments, which general logic may treat as universal and affirmative, and it includes them because "the function of understanding that is hereby exercised may perhaps be important in the field of its pure *a priori* cognition"; hence they "must not be omitted from the transcendental table of all moments of thinking in judgments" (A 71–73/B 96–98). "Transcendental" marks the table's departures from the logical one, for transcendental purposes. Heidegger reads a deliberate distinction as wavering. The Aristotle charge is fairer. Kant faulted Aristotle because "he had no principle" and "rounded them up as he stumbled on them" (A 81/B 107), and B 145–146 admits that the functions of judgment have no further ground.

**¶4, 56.** The critique of the table misses the problem: the categories "are not deduced factically from the Table of Judgments, but in no way can they have conduced to it" (Taft; the copy has "tactically"), because at this stage "the essence and the idea of the category in general has not yet been determined". This contests Kant's self-description at B 159, which says the origin of the categories was established in the metaphysical deduction. Heidegger's reason has support in Kant's Schematism: "Without schemata, therefore, the categories are only functions of the understanding for concepts, but do not represent any object" (A 147/B 187). If a category in the full sense includes its schema, the table of judgments yields only part of it. Heidegger generalizes Kant's Schematism back onto the metaphysical deduction, and Kant's later text corrects his earlier claim.

**¶5, 56.** If the question of the origin cannot yet arise, the table must have another function in preparing the question. A transition.

**¶6, 57.** The isolation of the elements must not forget that finite pure knowledge is the problem: pure thinking essentially serves intuition, so the pure concept as notion has been "shorn of the decisive, essential moment, namely, the inner reference to intuition. The pure concept as notion is therefore only a fragment of the second element." Kant's definition of the notion is exactly this "shorn" concept: a concept with its origin "solely in the understanding (not in a pure image of sensibility)" (A 320/B 377). What Kant presents as a definition, Heidegger calls a fragment. The judgment has Kant's Schematism behind it (A 147/B 187), and it is aimed at Kant's own way of introducing the categories in the clue.

**¶7, 57.** "Hence the Table of Judgments is not also the 'origin of the categories,' but rather is merely the 'guiding text for the discovery of all the concepts of the understanding.'" Kant's chapter title is "On the clue to the discovery of all pure concepts of the understanding" (A 66/B 91), so Heidegger sets Kant's title against Kant's B 159, where the same chapter is said to have established their origin. Heidegger takes the modest title over the strong summary. Whether the table can perform even that modest function, he says, "remains open here".

**¶8, 57–58.** The more radically one isolates the elements, the more their isolation proves impossible and the dependence of pure thinking on intuition obtrudes; "the artificiality of the first point of departure" is revealed; pure concepts can be determined as ontological predicates only from "the *essential unity* of finite, pure knowledge". Kant's method of isolation is announced at the start of the Logic, where the understanding is isolated "as we did above with sensibility in the transcendental aesthetic" (A 62/B 87), and the separation is commanded by the sentence after "Only from their unification can cognition arise" (A 51–52/B 76). Heidegger turns Kant's method into its own refutation: isolation shows itself artificial in being carried out. Kant counts the isolation as necessary for the critique and the unity as a later achievement of the deduction and the Schematism. For Heidegger, the failure of isolation shows that unity comes first.

#### The Second Stage of the Laying of the Ground: The Essential Unity of Pure Knowledge

**¶1, 58.** The isolated elements are time and the notions; if isolation cannot even grasp them fully, their unity cannot come from a later linking of the pieces. No Kant locus. The premise that the unity is not "a bond stretching between the elements which is merely an afterthought" will have to meet Kant's account of synthesis as an action that combines what is given (B 129–130).

**¶2, 58.** The pull of the elements toward each other means their unity cannot be "later" than they but must be "earlier"; it unites them so that "even at first in the uniting, the elements as such spring forth". Kant also has a unity before combination: "The representation of this unity cannot, therefore, arise from the combination; rather, by being added to the representation of the manifold, it first makes the concept of combination possible" (B 131). Kant's prior unity is the synthetic unity of apperception (B 131–132), and it does not let the elements spring forth. The manifold is "given" to it "prior to the synthesis of understanding and independently from it" (B 145). Heidegger's unity is prior in a stronger sense than Kant's: Kant's makes combination possible; Heidegger's would make the combined elements possible. The question that closes the paragraph, how far Kant made this unity visible, measures Kant by the stronger sense.

**¶3, 58–59.** Kant gives the first characterization of this unity in the third section of the clue, "On the Pure Concepts of the Understanding, or Categories" (A 76–80/B 102–105; §10 in B), and understanding it "is the key to understanding the *Critique of Pure Reason* as a laying of the ground for metaphysics". The choice of key is significant. The passage stands, nearly unchanged, in both editions, so the dispute over A and B does not touch it. It is the first place where Kant names the imagination as the source of synthesis (A 78/B 103), and in Kant it introduces the table of categories, which it serves to derive. Heidegger makes a transitional passage of the metaphysical deduction the centre of the *Critique*. Kant's own centre was the deduction, whose investigations "have cost me the most, but I hope not unrewarded, effort" (A xvi).

**¶4, 59.** Since the notions relate essentially to pure intuition, the essential delimitation of the category is at once the elucidation of the inner possibility of the unity of ontological knowledge. A transition. The equation of "category" with "unity of ontological knowledge" is the Second Stage's thesis in one sentence. It makes the category, which Kant defines as a concept (B 128), the name of a unity of concept and intuition, which is Kant's schematized category (A 147/B 187).

###### §13. The Question Concerning the Essential Unity of Pure Knowledge

**¶1, 59.** If the elements depend on one another, their unity cannot be stuck on afterwards; the preceding isolation has concealed how the unity underlies them; and given the rigour of the isolation, it may never be fully undone, "so that in the end the unity will not be expressly developed on the basis of its own most origin". The prediction of Kant's failure is made before the deduction is read, and it has a methodological cause: an analysis that begins from isolated elements cannot fully recover their unity. Kant's own method begins there because critique requires it (A 62/B 87; A 51–52/B 76). Heidegger's prediction is a verdict on that requirement.

**¶2, 60.** That the unity is itself the original unifying "is announced by naming it 'synthesis'". Kant's definition of synthesis "in the most general sense" is "the action of putting different representations together with each other and comprehending their manifoldness in one cognition" (A 77/B 103). It is an action on representations already given. The name does not announce an original unifying in Heidegger's sense. Heidegger's gloss lies in the word's prefix, "syn-", and the gloss comes from him.

**¶3–¶4, 60.** Of the veritative, predicative and apophantic syntheses, the veritative is meant, the unity of intuition and thinking, and it concentrates the problem; in ontological knowledge it is the pure veritative synthesis of time and the notions; pure intuition is already a unifying, and "Kant speaks with justification of a 'Synopsis' in intuition" (A 94); pure thinking, as reflecting concept, is already "synthetic". The A 94 citation is from the opening of the A-Deduction, a passage without B text, and the sentence Heidegger uses stands next to one that tells against his whole Third Section: "There are, however, three original sources (capacities or faculties of the soul), which contain the conditions of the possibility of all experience, and cannot themselves be derived from any other faculty of the mind, namely **sense, imagination,** and **apperception**", which ground "1) the **synopsis** of the manifold *a priori* through sense; 2) the **synthesis** of this manifold through the imagination; finally 3) the **unity** of this synthesis through original apperception" (A 94). Kant ranks the imagination as one of three underivable sources, not as the root of the other two. Heidegger takes "synopsis" from the sentence and leaves its frame for now. He quotes the frame in §27 (136), to establish the imagination as a third fundamental faculty, and then derives the other two from it (§§28–29), against the clause he quoted.

**¶5, 60–61.** The problem becomes: what is the original synthesis of the pure synopsis and the pure reflecting synthesis? The sought synthesis "must from the first already have been on a par with the forms of 'Synthesis' and 'Synopsis' which are to be unified; it must itself form these originally in the course of unifying them". The demand, a synthesis that forms what it unifies, is Heidegger's, and its form anticipates the imagination as root. Kant's three-part sequence at A 94 (synopsis, synthesis, unity) is a sequence of three sources, each contributing what the others cannot. Heidegger's demand turns the middle term into the ground of the other two.

###### §14. The Ontological Synthesis

**¶1, 61.** The elements show "seams [*Fugen*] which point in advance to a having-been-joined together", and the veritative synthesis is what "fits" them together in the first place. No Kant locus. The image of the joint, which forms the pieces so that they meet, is the formal argument for the imagination as root, given before the imagination is named.

**¶2, 61–62.** Kant introduces the unity thus: transcendental logic "has lying before it a manifold of a priori sensibility which the transcendental aesthetic offered to it", space and time belong to "the conditions for the receptivity of our mind ... which therefore must also always affect the concept of these objects", and "the spontaneity of our thought requires that this manifold first be gone through in a certain way, taken up, and bound together in order to produce knowledge. This act I name synthesis" (A 76–77/B 102, quoted exactly). The dependence is introduced "in a remarkably superficial form": it is not transcendental logic that has the manifold before it, but pure thinking, and not the Aesthetic that offers it, but pure intuition. The re-description replaces disciplines by structures and is fair to Kant's meaning. One feature of the quotation is lost in it. Kant gives spontaneity, "the spontaneity of our thought", as what requires the manifold to be gone through, taken up and combined. Spontaneity, which Heidegger set aside as missing the heart of the understanding (§5, 30), is the agent in the sentence he calls the key.

**¶3, 62.** "This pure offering is introduced even more precisely as an 'affecting'": since this affection "always" belongs to pure knowledge, "our pure thinking always stands before the time which approaches it. How this is possible remains unclear for the present." This is a re-inscription of one verb, and the rest of the book draws on it. In Kant's sentence the subject of "affect" is space and time as conditions of receptivity, and its object is "the concept of these objects": the forms of sensibility "must always also affect the concept of these objects" (A 77/B 102). Kant means that the forms of intuition condition the concepts of objects that can be given in them. Heidegger makes time affect pure thinking. Kant's verb has become the first appearance of the pure self-affection of §34, and Heidegger marks the step himself: "How this is possible remains unclear."

**¶4, 62.** The finitude of thinking "demands" that the manifold fit with thinking; to be determinable by concepts, the manifold "must have been gathered from dispersion, i.e., it must be gone through and assembled"; this "reciprocal preparing-themselves-for-each-other" is what Kant calls synthesis, which "joins together the seams" and constitutes the unity of pure knowledge. Kant's text says "gone through, taken up, and combined" (A 77/B 102), and the A-Deduction will analyse the three acts as apprehension, reproduction and recognition (A 98–110). "Gathered from dispersion" is Heidegger's gloss, which anticipates the "gathering" of the categories as unities (§11, marginal note). In Kant the synthesis is required by spontaneity. Heidegger makes it a mutual preparation of the two elements, and a synthesis of both is not Kant's synthesis of a manifold of intuition.

**¶5, 62–63.** "This synthesis is neither a matter of intuition nor of thinking. Mediating 'between' both, so to speak, it is related to both", and must be a representing: "Synthesis in general, as we shall hereafter see, is the mere result of the power of imagination, a blind but indispensable function of the soul without which we would have no knowledge whatever, but of which we are seldom conscious even once" (A 78/B 103, Heidegger's spacing). The sentence is quoted exactly from both editions' printed text. Two facts frame it. Kant's next sentence divides the labour: "Yet to bring this synthesis **to concepts** is a function that pertains to the understanding, and by means of which it first provides cognition in the proper sense" (A 78/B 103). And in his own copy of the first edition Kant replaced "a blind though indispensable function of the soul" with "a function of the understanding" (Guyer and Wood's note to A 78, from Erdmann's *Nachträge*). Heidegger knows the correction and holds it back for §31, where it becomes evidence of Kant's retreat. Here the uncorrected sentence carries the claim that synthesis is "neither a matter of intuition nor of thinking", while Kant's corrected sentence makes it the understanding's. The "between" is Heidegger's. In Kant's three pieces the imagination's synthesis comes second in an enumeration, as Heidegger himself observes before calling the position structural.

**¶6, 63.** Everything synthetic in knowledge is apparently "brought about through the power of imagination"; pure synthesis is called pure "if the manifold ... is given a priori" (A 77/B 103). The second claim is exact. The first generalizes from the A 78 sentence ("Synthesis in general ... the mere effect of the imagination") and is Kant's in A. In the B-Deduction Kant also calls all combination "an action of the understanding" (B 130), which is the claim the Handexemplar correction already makes.

**¶7, 63–64.** Pure synthesis needs a guiding unity; it represents this unity in general, which means "with respect to the unity that is represented in it, pure synthesis is brought to the concept which itself gives it unity" (Taft; the German says the pure synthesis *brings itself* to the concept); hence three pieces: "What must first be given to us—with a view to the *a priori* knowledge of all objects—is the *manifold* of pure intuition; the synthesis of this manifold by means of the power of the imagination is the second, but even this does not yet yield knowledge. The concepts which give unity to this pure synthesis ... furnish the third ... and they rest on the understanding" (A 78–79/B 104). The re-inscription is in the reflexive verb. Kant says "to bring this synthesis to concepts is a function that pertains to the understanding" (A 78/B 103), and of the third piece that it "rest[s] on the understanding". Heidegger's sentence makes the synthesis bring itself to the concept. The agent Kant names, the understanding, has disappeared from the sentence, and Taft's passive half restores it. The Third Section's thesis is already in this verb.

**¶8, 64.** "In this triad, the pure synthesis of the power of imagination holds the central position", and the centre "is a structural one" in which pure synopsis and pure reflecting synthesis join; Kant expresses this joining in establishing "the sameness of pure synthesis" in intuition and understanding. Heidegger concedes that the centre might seem merely enumerative and then denies it. Kant's three pieces are a sequence of conditions, each of which "does not yet yield" cognition without the next (A 78–79/B 104). The structural centre is Heidegger's reading, and the next quotation is his evidence for it.

**¶9, 64.** "The same function which gives unity to the various representations in a single judgment also gives unity to the mere synthesis of various representations in a single intuition which, expressed generally, is called the pure concept of the understanding" (A 79/B 104–105, quoted exactly); this sameness is not "the empty identity" of a formal connecting but "the original, rich wholeness" of a unifying that works as intuiting and thinking at once; "Here sameness means: an essential, structural belonging-together." In Kant the "function" is the understanding's, by his own definition two pages earlier (A 68/B 93), and the sentence says that one function of the understanding unifies both judgment and intuition. The sameness Kant asserts is that of a single source, the understanding. Heidegger reads it as a belonging-together of two sources in a third. The re-inscription reverses the direction of Kant's claim, which extends the understanding's function into intuition.

**¶10, 64–65.** "Therefore the same understanding—namely, through exactly the same actions by means of which it achieves the logical form of a judgment in concepts through analytical unity—also brings a transcendental content into its representations by means of the synthetic unity of the manifold in intuition in general ..." (A 79/B 105); the unity of pure knowledge is "a multiform action which remains obscure", and the ground-laying must trace pure synthesis "in its springing-forth". The quotation names its subject: "the same understanding". Heidegger quotes the sentence whose subject is the understanding and concludes that the unity is an obscure action whose origin must still be found. The understanding Kant names as the author of the transcendental content is, for Heidegger, one of the things whose origin is in question. The ellipsis cuts Kant's sentence before "this can never be accomplished by general logic", which is harmless. The dispute is in the subject Heidegger kept.

**¶11, 65.** The ground-laying has reached the point where "the matter itself is deeply veiled" (A 88/B 121), and a methodological pause is needed. Kant's sentence warns the reader of the deduction "that he will not complain of obscurity where the subject-matter itself is deeply veiled" (A 88/B 121). The citation is accurate and well placed: Heidegger is about to enter the deduction, and he borrows Kant's warning for it.

###### §15. The Problem of the Categories and the Role of Transcendental Logic

**¶1, 65–66.** If a category is to be not only "a mode of 'assertion,' σχῆμα τοῦ λόγου" but "σχῆμα τοῦ ὄντος", it may not function as an "element" (notion); the known being of beings must lie in it, and since the knowing of being is the unity of pure intuition and thought, "the pure intuitability of the notions" becomes decisive. The Greek formulas are Heidegger's frame from Aristotle, not Kant's. On the substance, Kant agrees that a category without intuition represents no object: "Without schemata, therefore, the categories are only functions of the understanding for concepts, but do not represent any object. This significance comes to them from sensibility" (A 147/B 187). Kant's category, though, is still a concept whose application is restricted. Heidegger makes the category the being of beings as known. Kant's "concept of an object in general" (B 128) has become a figure of being, and the marginal note's "unclear" (later) registers some hesitation.

**¶2, 66.** The Aesthetic expounded pure intuition, the Analytic of Concepts pure thinking; the pure synthesis belongs to neither; so the elucidation of its origin "can be neither a transcendental-aesthetic nor a transcendental-logical one", and the category is a problem of neither discipline. No Kant locus. The conclusion contradicts Kant's own placement of the problem, which the next paragraph names.

**¶3, 66.** Which discipline then? "This question remains foreign to Kant." He assigns the unity of pure knowledge to the Analytic of Concepts, giving logic "an incomparable priority over the aesthetic", although intuition is primary. Kant does have a place for the mediating problem, and Heidegger does not name it. The Schematism opens the "transcendental doctrine of the power of judgment", which "will contain two chapters: the first, which deals with the sensible condition under which alone pure concepts of the understanding can be employed, i.e., with the schematism of the pure understanding; and the second" with the principles (A 136–137/B 175–176). Kant gave the unity of category and intuition its own discipline, the doctrine of judgment, inside the Transcendental Logic. Heidegger's "foreign to Kant" holds if the discipline must stand outside the Logic. It does not hold if what is asked is whether Kant saw that the unity needs a treatment of its own.

**¶4–¶5, 66–67.** Interpretation keeps succumbing to reading the *Critique* as a "logic of pure knowledge", even where intuition is granted a relative right; yet the priority of transcendental logic "remains, in a certain sense, valid", and "precisely for this reason, the interpretation must free itself from the Kantian architectonic, and it must make the idea of transcendental logic problematic". The target is Cohen's *Logic of Pure Cognition* and its school. Kant's own definition of transcendental logic includes the condition Heidegger wants it to remember: its use "depends on this as its condition: that objects are given to us in intuition, to which it can be applied" (A 62/B 87). A later marginal note grounds the priority of logic in the ancient question of being as onto-*logo*-logy; it is retrospective and belongs to the later history of being.

**¶6, 67.** If thinking essentially serves intuition, a properly understood analytic of pure thinking must include that relation, and that Kant does so "shows on its surface that the finitude of thinking is contained in the theme"; with insight into the grounds of the priority of logic, that priority "is superseded", in favour of a question that takes the unity of ontological knowledge back to "a more original basis". The first step reconstructs Kant's reason for treating the unity in the Analytic, and A 62/B 87 supports it. The second step, priority justified and superseded at once, is Heidegger's. It removes the problem from Kant's discipline at the moment it explains why Kant put it there.

**¶7, 67–68.** Under the heading of the "use" of the pure concepts, the relation to intuition enters the theme, but always from the side of thinking, and the category keeps presenting itself as a notion, a "pure concept of the understanding". Kant's terminology does what Heidegger says. The Schematism is "the sensible condition under which alone pure concepts of the understanding can be employed" (A 136/B 175): the concept comes first and its condition of use second.

**¶8–¶9, 68.** Oriented on thinking, Kant must draw on traditional formal logic, so the transcendental question takes on a "logical, albeit a transcendental-logical" character; and the orientation toward *logos* and *ratio* has a priority from the start in Western metaphysics, expressed in the title *Critique of Pure Reason*. The first claim describes Kant's procedure in the clue, where the forms of judgment from general logic guide the table of categories (A 70/B 95). The second is Heidegger's history of metaphysics. Kant's title names reason as the faculty of principles a priori (A 11/B 24). Heidegger hears in it the inherited primacy of *logos*.
