**¶10–¶11, 68.** Kant needed a schoolish framework, which a new logic of pure knowledge could borrow from formal logic, to master "this 'very complicated web of human knowledge'" (A 85/B 117) first disclosed by his analytic; the interpretation must "break through the architectonic of the extrinsic succession and pattern of problems". Kant's phrase, "the very mixed fabric of human cognition" (A 85/B 117), introduces the need for a deduction of the concepts "destined for pure use", not the need for a framework. Heidegger borrows the phrase for a different purpose, without distorting its sense. The programme of breaking through the architectonic restates the licence of the introduction to Part B (43).

The first two stages make three Kantian texts load-bearing: the metaphysical exposition of space and time (A 22–25/B 37–40, A 30–34/B 46–51), the notion and the table of judgments (A 320/B 377; A 70–80/B 95–106), and §10 of the clue (A 76–80/B 102–105), declared "the key" to the *Critique*. The reading holds where it reconstructs: the singularity and non-discursiveness of space, the universal scope of time as mediate condition of outer appearances, the impossibility of grounding the functions of judgment further, which Kant concedes (B 145–146), and the dependence of the full category on the schema (A 147/B 187). It shifts where single words carry *Being and Time* into Kant's sentences. "Original" moves from underived (A 32/B 48) to letting-spring-forth. "Affect" passes from the forms of intuition's conditioning of concepts (A 77/B 102) to the affection of thinking by time. The understanding's "function" (A 68/B 93) becomes reflection, and "the same function" becomes a structural belonging-together. The synthesis brings itself to the concept where Kant has the understanding bring it. The quotations stop before Kant's limits or keep them out of view: A 94's three underivable sources, Kant's correction at A 78, the "doctrine of the power of judgment" as Kant's place for the unity, and the Amphiboly's distinction of concepts of reflection from categories. The pattern of the earlier divisions holds. The texts are quoted exactly, and the decisive moves fall in the gloss after the quotation. The Second Stage ends with Kant's own sentence making the understanding the author of the unity (A 79/B 105). Heidegger declares that unity still obscure and turns to the deduction to find its origin.

#### The Third Stage of the Laying of the Ground: The Inner Possibility of the Essential Unity of the Ontological Synthesis

**¶1–¶2, 69.** The apparently firm answer about the unity of ontological knowledge "progressively dissolves" into the problem of the possibility of such unification; the pure synthesis must be shown uniting time and notion, and this "exhibition of the original self-forming of the essential unity of ontological knowledge" is "the sense and task of what Kant calls the 'Transcendental Deduction of the Categories.'" The definition is Heidegger's. Kant states the deduction's task as "the explanation of the way in which concepts can relate to objects *a priori*" (A 85/B 117) and as showing that the categories "must be recognized as *a priori* conditions of the possibility of experiences" (A 94/B 126). "Self-forming" makes the unity an act that forms itself. In Kant the deduction proves an entitlement.

**¶3, 69.** Since the deduction's intention lies in "the analytical opening-up of the basic structure of pure synthesis", its content cannot be expressed as a "*quaestio juris*", and the *quaestio juris* "may not be taken as a guide". This is contestation of Kant's own framing, announced as such. Kant introduces the deduction as a *quaestio juris*: "Jurists, when they speak of entitlements and claims, distinguish in a legal matter between the questions about what is lawful (*quid juris*) and that which concerns the fact (*quid facti*) ... they call the first, that which is to establish the entitlement or the legal claim, the **deduction**" (A 84/B 116). Heidegger concedes the frame in §18 and subordinates it there. He reads the deduction by what it does and sets aside what Kant says it is for. The text he relies on is A 115–128, where the analysis of synthesis does occupy most of the execution, so the reading has a basis in the execution even as it overrides the stated purpose.

**¶4, 69–70.** The interpretation "will consider exclusively the working out of the Transcendental Deduction in the first edition", for reasons given in §31; Kant stressed its "difficulty" and sought to "remedy" its "obscurity"; its many approaches show Kant "struggling"; what the analysis should first yield is announced in a "Preliminary Remark"; and the difficulty leads Kant to overemphasize pure thinking. The decision to read only A is the book's most consequential philological choice, and Kant's own account of his revision tells against the reason Heidegger will give for it. In the B preface Kant says of the second edition: "I have found nothing to alter either in the propositions themselves or in their grounds of proof, or in the form and completeness of the book's plan", and his revisions concern "the mode of presentation" (B xxxvii–xxxviii). On Kant's account B is a restatement of A. On Heidegger's (§31), B retreats from A's discovery. The choice of A is announced and deferred, but its ground, the thesis of shrinking-back, is the thing the A-reading is meant to establish, so the argument runs in a circle the book acknowledges. The "Preliminary reminder" is the heading of the A text's second section (before A 98). The charge of overemphasis on pure thinking anticipates the verdict that the understanding's priority is a symptom of the retreat.

**¶5, 70.** The interpretation will not walk every twisted path of the deduction but lay bare "the original impetus"; first the deduction's goal must be clarified. No Kant locus. "Original impetus" places the interpreter's selection above Kant's own route through the text.

##### §16. The Elucidation of the Transcendence of Finite Reason as Basic Intention of the Transcendental Deduction

**¶1, 70–71.** A finite knower can relate to a being it did not create only if that being can be encountered from itself, and for that it must be "recognized" in advance in its constitution of being; ontological knowledge, "which here is always pre-ontological", is the condition of "something like a being" standing against a finite creature; the finite creature needs "a turning-toward ... which lets-{something}-stand-in-opposition", which forms a "space for play [*Spielraum*]" in which something can "correspond"; holding oneself in this space "is none other than the transcendence which marks all finite comportment to beings". The vocabulary is entirely Heidegger's: pre-ontological understanding, turning-toward, letting-stand-against, play-space, transcendence. The Kantian anchor is the word "correspond", which Heidegger puts in quotes and which comes from A 104 ("we have nothing that we could set over against this cognition as corresponding to it"). Kant's question at that point is what can make cognition agree with an object if nothing outside cognition is available to compare it with. Heidegger's answer, a pre-formed play-space, replaces Kant's answer at A 104–105, the formal unity of consciousness. Henry's *objectivation* translates "letting-stand-against" (Commentary R notes the link). For the present comparison the point is that Kant's *Gegenstand* is not derived from a letting. Kant's object of representations is "something in general = X" (A 104), thought through the unity of the synthesis.

**¶2–¶3, 71.** Finite knowledge, as receptive intuition, needs determining thought, so pure thinking has "a central significance", precisely because of intuition's preeminence; "To what essential service is pure thinking called in its serving appointment?" No Kant locus. The question frames the understanding of the A-Deduction as a servant whose service is to be determined. Kant's A text frames it as the source of the unity that makes objects possible (A 119, A 126–127, below).

**¶4, 71–72.** In the "Transition to the Transcendental Deduction" (A 92–93/B 124–125) Kant points to the finitude of our representing, "for we are not here speaking of its causality by means of the will"; "representation in itself" "cannot bring forth its object so far as its existence is concerned"; and at A 104, "outside our knowledge we have nothing which we could ever set over against this knowledge as corresponding to it". Both quotations are exact. The first stops where Kant's sentence turns positive: representation in itself does not produce its object "as far as its **existence** is concerned", but "the representation is still determinant of the object *a priori* if it is possible through it alone to **cognize something as an object**" (A 92/B 125). Heidegger reads the transition as an admission of finitude. Kant's sentence makes it the opening of his positive thesis, that representation determines the object a priori as object. Heidegger's letting-stand-against, two pages on, restates that positive thesis in his own terms. The cut keeps finitude in front and moves Kant's claim of a priori determination into Heidegger's vocabulary.

**¶5–¶6, 72.** What belongs to the possibility of taking beings in stride? That beings can be encountered "as that which stands-against"; since we do not control their being at hand, they must be given "in advance and at all times the possibility of standing-against". No Kant locus. The step is Heidegger's: from Kant's "the object is given to us" (A 19/B 33) to a prior possibility of standing-against that the finite knower must supply. Kant locates the a priori conditions of givenness in the forms of intuition and of thought, not in a supplied possibility of encounter.

**¶7, 72.** Taking-in-stride can occur only in a faculty of letting-stand-against; what we let stand against "cannot be a being. But if not a being, then just a nothing"; only if letting-stand-against is "a holding oneself in the nothing" can a "not-nothing", a being, be encountered; "this nothing is not the *nihil absolutum*". Kant's name for what stands over against cognition here is the opposite word: the object "must be thought of only as something in general = *X*" (A 104), the "transcendental object = *X*" that is "always one and the same" (A 109). Kant says something, Heidegger says nothing. The difference is not simply verbal. Kant's X is the thought of an object in general, the correlate of the unity of apperception. Heidegger's nothing is the horizon, which is not a being. Kant's own table of nothing would place the pure forms of intuition, not the transcendental object, among the "something" that is not an object (A 291/B 347). Heidegger draws "nothing" from *What is Metaphysics?* of the same year. It is contestation of Kant's word, made without naming the word it replaces.

**¶8, 73.** With finitude placed as clearly as in Kant at the start of transcendence, there is no need for the "turn to the Object" praised "all too noisily" today; finitude rather forces the question of the conditions of a prior being-turned toward the object; and Kant poses the decisive question in the deduction, "the first one" to do so. The polemic is against contemporary realism (Commentary R names Nicolai Hartmann). The claim that Kant was first to ask about the turning-toward the object in general is a reading of A 104, which follows.

**¶9, 73.** Kant: "And here, then, it is necessary that we make clear to ourselves what we mean by the expression an object of representations"; "Now we find that our thought of the relation of all knowledge to its object carries with it an element of necessity, where indeed this [the object] is viewed as that which opposes so that our knowledge is not haphazard or arbitrary, but is instead determined a priori in a certain way" (A 104); in the letting-stand-against "something reveals itself as 'that which opposes.'" The quotation is exact and Taft's rendering of Kant's *dawider ist, daß* ("is against [our cognitions'] being") is close. Guyer and Wood: the object "is regarded as that which is opposed to our cognitions being determined at pleasure or arbitrarily". Kant's *dawider* is an adverb in a construction: the object is what prevents arbitrariness in our cognitions. Heidegger turns the construction into a noun, *das Dawider*, "that which opposes", and from the next paragraph a character of being. Kant's sentence goes on to say what the opposition consists in: "since insofar as they are to relate to an object our cognitions must also necessarily agree with each other in relation to it, i.e., they must have that unity that constitutes the concept of an object" (A 104). On the next page: "the unity that the object makes necessary can be nothing other than the formal unity of consciousness in the synthesis of the manifold of representations" (A 105). Kant reduces the "against" to a unity of consciousness, and Heidegger's next step follows the reduction.

**¶10, 73–74.** The resistance is not of beings or sensations but "the previous resistance of Being"; it carries a constraint ("necessity") that forces all encounter into "a concordance"; holding unity before oneself is the essence of the concept, "a consciousness" (A 103–104); letting-stand-against is the "primal concept" and "the primal activity of the understanding"; the understanding is "the faculty of letting-stand-against"; "the representation of a universal condition according to which a certain manifold (thus, in uniform fashion) can be posited is called a rule" (A 113); the concept, "however imperfect or obscure", is in form "always something that is universal and that serves as the rule" (A 106). The sequence follows Kant closely from A 104 to A 106 and A 113, and the quotations are exact (Guyer and Wood render *mithin auf einerlei Art* as "of whatever kind", Taft as "in uniform fashion"). The one re-inscription is the first: "resistance of Being". Kant's necessity is the necessity of unity in the synthesis, the formal unity of consciousness (A 105). Heidegger's is a constraint exercised by being on what is encountered. With that word the A-Deduction's theory of the object becomes ontology. The rest is Kant's, set in the vocabulary of letting-stand-against.

**¶11, 74–75.** The pure concepts (*conceptus reflectentes*) have such ruling unities as their sole content and give something rule-like in advance; so "in conjunction with the elucidation of the letting-stand-against, Kant first attains the more original concept of understanding: 'We may now characterize it as the *faculty of rules*. This characterizing is more fruitful and approximates its essence more closely'" (A 126). The quotation is exact and the gloss stretches it. Kant's sentence begins by listing earlier explanations of the understanding, "through a spontaneity of cognition ..., through a faculty for thinking, or a faculty of concepts, or also of judgments", which, "if one looks at them properly, come down to the same thing" (A 126). Kant calls the faculty of rules more fruitful, not more original, and he places it alongside spontaneity, not above it. Heidegger, who set spontaneity aside as missing the heart of the understanding (§5, 30), takes from Kant a ranking that Kant's sentence denies. A marginal note points to the Duisburg *Nachlass* of the 1770s on rules; it is later than the text.

**¶12–¶13, 75.** If the understanding regulates in advance all that intuition brings, "Has the servant not changed into the master?" Has Kant "forgotten the finitude of the understanding"? Or is its mastery "fundamentally a serving"? The questions are Heidegger's, and they state the tension between his §4 (thinking serves intuition) and Kant's A 126–127, where the understanding is "the legislation for nature". Kant's text does not experience the tension Heidegger names, since Kant never made the understanding a servant. The serving position was Heidegger's reading of A 19/B 33 ("all thought as a means is directed").

**¶14–¶15, 75–76.** "In fact, the understanding is the supreme faculty—in finitude, i.e., [it is] finite to the highest degree"; its dependence on intuition must show most sharply in letting-stand-against, on pure intuition; "Only insofar as the pure understanding, as understanding, is the servant of pure intuition can it remain master of empirical intuition." The formula reconciles Heidegger's two theses and has partial Kantian warrant. Kant's understanding prescribes laws to nature only as the nature of appearances in space and time. The categories "have significance only in relation to the unity of intuitions in space and time" (B 308), and the A-Deduction makes the understanding lawgiver of a nature that is the "synthetic unity of the manifold of appearances in accordance with rules; for appearances, as such, cannot occur outside us, but exist only in our sensibility" (A 127). What Kant expresses as the restriction of the understanding's legislation to appearances, Heidegger expresses as service to pure intuition. "Servant of pure intuition" is not Kant's relation. In Kant, pure intuition supplies a manifold that the understanding's synthesis determines (B 150–151; A 77/B 102).

**¶16, 76.** Pure intuition too is finite; their structural unity sinks both into "their full finitude, which reveals itself as transcendence"; the unveiling of the full structure of pure synthesis leads to the goal of the deduction, "the elucidation of transcendence". No Kant locus. The goal is stated in Heidegger's terms, and Kant's goal, the objective validity of the categories (A 89/B 122; A 128), will be redescribed in §18 to fit it.

##### §17. The Two Ways of the Transcendental Deduction

**¶1, 76.** The deduction is "the analytical unveiling of the structural whole of pure synthesis"; this "hardly corresponds to its lexical concept" and "even appears to contradict Kant's own explicit explanation of what deduction means"; the interpretation keeps to the "Third Section" (A 115–128), where Kant presents the deduction "in its interconnectedness" (A 115). Heidegger names his contradiction of Kant's definition and defers the reckoning to §18. The choice of A 115–128 is a choice within A. Kant's A-Deduction has a "subjective" second section, the threefold synthesis (A 95–114), and an "objective" third section. Heidegger takes the third section as the deduction and reserves the second for §33, where its threefold synthesis is read as time.

**¶2, 76–77.** The heading, "On the relationship of the understanding to objects in general, and the possibility of knowing them a priori" (A 115), shows that the problem of the possibility of ontological knowledge is "nothing other than the unveiling of transcendence". The heading is quoted exactly. Kant's "relation to objects" is the relation of a representation to an object. "Transcendence" interprets it as Dasein's stepping-over to beings. The heading names the understanding as one term of the relation, and in Heidegger's account the understanding is about to give up its primacy within it.

**¶3–¶4, 77.** For a finite creature beings are accessible only through a prior letting-stand-against that takes them in advance "into the unified horizon of a possible belonging-together"; what is encountered is already embraced by "the horizon of time" set forth in pure intuition; so the unity of the understanding must already be united with pure intuition; this whole "'forms' the play-space for the letting-stand-against". "Horizon" and "play-space" are Heidegger's. The claim that the understanding's unity must already be united with pure intuition has Kantian support in the B-Deduction, where the unity of apperception is shown to apply to the pure manifold of space and time through the figurative synthesis (B 150–152, B 160–161). Heidegger does not use the B text, which makes that synthesis the understanding's "effect" on sensibility (B 152). The scare quotes on "forms" (*bildet*) mark the word that will connect the imagination (*Einbildungskraft*) to the forming of the horizon.

**¶5–¶7, 77.** Two ways: "from above", from the pure understanding down to intuition (A 116–120), and "from below" (A 119), from intuition up to the pure understanding (A 120–128); both unveil "both extreme endpoints, namely, sensibility and understanding", which "must necessarily be interconnected" (A 124). The citations are accurate. Kant's full sentence names the connecting term: "Both extremes, namely sensibility and understanding, must necessarily be connected by means of this transcendental function of the imagination" (A 124). Kant's figure is that of extremes and a middle term, the imagination mediating between two sources that remain distinct. Heidegger's next paragraph turns the middle into "that in which they can be interconnected in general", which is a step toward the root.

**¶8, 77–78.** What matters is not a linear connection of two faculties but "the structural elucidation of their essential unity"; "that in which they can be interconnected"; the unifying middle is run through from both ends, and the pure synthesis is unveiled in this going back and forth. Commentary R calls the two ways "two passages through one middle". In Kant's text they are two orders of exposition of one deduction, from apperception downward and from appearance upward (A 116, A 119). The reading of the two ways as one movement through a centre is faithful to the A text's structure. What Heidegger adds is the claim that the middle is "that in which" the extremes cohere, a ground rather than a link.

###### a) The First Way

**¶1–¶2, 78.** The first way must clarify the pure understanding until its dependence on pure synthesis, hence on pure intuition, becomes visible; the "Deduction" is "wholly different from a deductive, logical developing" of relations; it holds the whole of pure finite knowledge in view from the start; "Without the lasting premonition of the finitude of transcendence, every statement of the Transcendental Deduction remains incomprehensible." The disclaimer about logical deduction agrees with Kant, whose deduction is a juridical proof of entitlement, not a syllogistic derivation (A 84/B 116). The last sentence makes Heidegger's frame, the finitude of transcendence, the condition of understanding Kant's text. It is the hermeneutic circle stated as a requirement.

**¶3, 78–79.** The "Being-in-opposition" (*Dawider*) shows itself in an "anticipatory holding of the unity"; in representing unity, the representing is revealed to itself as "the selfsame which maintains itself" (A 108); only in such "turning-oneself-toward" can what is encountered be "something which matters to us" (A 116). The citations fit Kant's sentences. At A 108 the unity of consciousness would be impossible "if in the cognition of the manifold the mind could not become conscious of the identity of the function"; at A 116 intuitions "are nothing for us and do not in the least concern us if they cannot be taken up into consciousness". "Turning-oneself-toward" (*Selbstzuwendung*) is Heidegger's word for Kant's identity of the function. Kant's point is that unity of consciousness requires consciousness of the identity of the act. Heidegger's is that the self turns toward itself in holding unity before it. The second will become self-affection in §34.

**¶4, 79.** Representing unity as pure thinking has the character of the "I think"; it must always be possible, "an 'I am able'"; "This pure, original, unchangeable consciousness I will now name *Transcendental Apperception*" (A 107); letting-stand-against is grounded in apperception "as a power" (A 117 n., Kant's emphasis); "Only as the constant, free 'I can'" can the "I think" let the "against" of unity stand against itself, "if in fact linking remains possible only with reference to an essentially free comporting". The quotations are exact. Kant's "as a faculty" (*als einem Vermögen*) means that the logical form of cognition rests on the relation to apperception as a capacity, "whether this representation be clear ... or obscure, even whether it be actual" (A 117 n.). Heidegger reads *Vermögen*, faculty, as *vermögen*, to be able, and the capacity as freedom. Freedom is not in Kant's sentence. It comes from *Vom Wesen des Grundes*, where freedom is the ground of grounds. Kant has his own word for the possibility: in B, "The **I think** must **be able** to accompany all my representations" (B 131). Heidegger reaches the "I can" through A 117 n. and not through B 131, consistent with his decision to read only A.

**¶5, 79–80.** What does the unity represent? Not "the universe of beings" as a *totum simul* such as the *intuitus originarius* intuits; finite thinking cannot set beings before itself; "The represented unity first awaits the encountered being"; as nonontic it tends toward unifying what is not yet unified; hence Kant says it "presupposes a synthesis, or includes one" (A 118). The quotation is exact: "This synthetic unity, however, presupposes a synthesis, or includes it, and if the former is to be necessary *a priori* then the latter must also be a synthesis *a priori*" (A 118). The "awaiting" unity is Heidegger's gloss and prepares the futural recognition of §33. Kant's sentence states a dependence of unity on synthesis, which Heidegger takes up next.

**¶6, 80.** "Characteristically, Kant wavers here" between "presupposes" and "includes"; in any case synthesis belongs to unity; "Kant is not afraid to say that Transcendental Apperception 'presupposes' the synthesis." Heidegger is right that the alternative is unresolved in Kant's sentence. The weight Commentary R places on it ("the supreme principle of the *Critique* is here said by Kant himself to presuppose the imagination") goes beyond the sentence, which speaks of "a synthesis", and beyond its continuation: "Thus the transcendental unity of apperception is related to the pure synthesis of the imagination, as an *a priori* condition of the possibility of all composition of the manifold in a cognition" (A 118). Kant's "as an *a priori* condition" is ambiguous between unity and synthesis as its subject, so Kant leaves the order open in both sentences. Heidegger takes the half of the alternative that suits him and calls the other half wavering.

**¶7, 80.** All synthesis is the imagination's (§14); so apperception is related essentially to pure imagination, which as pure is "necessarily formative [*bildend*] a priori, i.e., purely productive"; Kant calls it "transcendental"; "Thus the principle of the necessary unity of the pure (productive) synthesis of the power of imagination, prior to apperception, is the ground for the possibility of all knowledge, especially of experience" (A 118). The quotation is exact. Kant distinguishes the productive from the reproductive synthesis because only the first "can take place *a priori*" (A 118). Heidegger glosses "productive" as "formative", from *bilden* in *Einbildungskraft*, and that etymology carries the rest of the book. In Kant "productive" means not dependent on prior empirical intuition. The gloss "forming" adds the image (*Bild*) that §20 will need.

**¶8–¶9, 80–81.** What does "prior to apperception" (*vor der Apperzeption*) mean? Prior in the order of grounding, matching "presupposes"? Or, as in Kant's phrase "of an object prior to [*vor*] a wholly other intuition" (A 287/B 343–344), where changing "vor" to "für" would weaken the sense, and as in "*coram intuitu intellectuali*" (A 249): "before", in view of; so the representing of unity "has essentially before itself, in view, a unifying unity". This is a philological argument, and its evidence cuts both ways. In eighteenth-century German *vor* often stands where modern German has *für*, "for". The corpus texts show the problem. At A 287/B 343 the 1781 text has *vor* (garbled in the corpus OCR), the 1787 text in the corpus reads *für eine ganz andere Anschauung*, and Guyer and Wood translate "an object for an entirely different intuition". Kant's sentence there concerns the noumenon as the "problematic concept of an object for an entirely different intuition and an entirely different understanding than our own". "For" fits it; "before the eyes of" fits less well. The Latin *coram intuitu intellectuali* is from A 249, a passage cut in B, and it supports "before" only for that phrase. At A 118, "prior to" (Guyer and Wood, Kemp Smith) and "for" both make sense: the principle of the unity of the imagination's synthesis, for apperception, is the ground of cognition. Heidegger's *coram* reading is a third, and it makes apperception the viewer of a unity the imagination has already formed. He says the reading "includes" the first. In effect it reverses Kant's ordinary sense, in which the unity of apperception is what the imagination's synthesis must conform to (A 118–119).

**¶10, 81.** Pure synthesis must unify a priori what is given a priori, which is time; so pure imagination must relate essentially to time and "is unveiled as the mediator between Transcendental Apperception and time". The conclusion is Kant's in substance: the imagination's synthesis, related to apperception, has the manifold of inner sense in time as its material (A 99, A 118–119). "Mediator between apperception and time" is a fair compression, and it is the formula §§32–34 will radicalize, until the mediator becomes time itself.

**¶11, 81–82.** Kant prefaces the deduction with "a general observation which must serve as the ground for what follows", that all "modifications of the mind ... are subject to time ... as that in which they must all be ordered, connected, and brought into relation with one another" (A 99); yet neither way discusses the relation of imagination to time explicitly; the analysis concentrates on the understanding's relation to the imagination, which expresses its finitude; the understanding is understanding only insofar as it "presupposes or includes" the imagination; "The unity of apperception in relation to the synthesis of the power of imagination is the understanding; and this same unity, with reference to the transcendental synthesis of the power of imagination, [is] the pure understanding" (A 119). The A 99 remark is quoted exactly. Heidegger's observation that Kant does not work out the imagination's relation to time in the third section is accurate, and the Schematism is where Kant does so (A 138–142/B 177–181). The A 119 definition is exact and needs its next sentence: "In the understanding there are therefore pure *a priori* cognitions that contain the necessary unity of the pure synthesis of the imagination in regard to all possible appearances. These, however, are the **categories**" (A 119). Kant locates the unity of the imagination's synthesis "in the understanding". Heidegger reads the definition as making the understanding dependent on the imagination. Kant's next sentence makes the understanding the possessor of that synthesis's unity. "Presupposes or includes" is Heidegger's paraphrase, transferring A 118's phrase about apperception and synthesis to understanding and imagination.

###### b) The Second Way

**¶1, 82.** The second way begins: "we want now to start from below, that is, with the empirical, in order to bring out the necessary connection in which understanding, by means of the categories, stands to appearances" (A 119). The quotation is exact (Guyer and Wood: "by beginning from beneath, namely with what is empirical"). Kant's stated aim is the connection of understanding with appearances "by means of the categories". Heidegger's aim on this way is the dependence of pure intuition on pure understanding through the pure synthesis.

**¶2, 82.** Kant does not dwell on pure intuition but shows that "sense" takes in yet "has nothing" like a connectedness, since what is encountered is found "scattered and individually" (A 120); connection must be understood in advance, which means forming relation in general, and "this power—which first and foremost 'forms' relations—is the pure power of imagination". Kant's sentence is quoted correctly: "different perceptions by themselves are encountered dispersed and separate in the mind, a combination of them, which they cannot have in sense itself, is therefore necessary. There is thus an active faculty of the synthesis of this manifold in us, which we call imagination" (A 120). The next sentence would have suited Heidegger better than his own gloss, and he does not quote it: "For the imagination is to bring the manifold of intuition into an **image**" (A 120). Kant's imagination forms images. Heidegger's forms "relations", a horizon. The "image" waits for §20.

**¶3, 82–83.** Time, by the "general observation", is where connection can be formed; letting beings be encountered "must be grounded in the pure power of imagination that is essentially related to time"; its pure forming of relations gives "a normative unification" opposed to haphazard taking-in; "This horizon of normative connection contains the pure 'affinity' of appearances"; "That the affinity of appearances ... only becomes possible by means of this transcendental function of the power of imagination is indeed strange" (A 123). The quotation is exact. Kant's sentence continues: affinity, "and with it the association and through the latter finally reproduction in accordance with laws, and consequently experience itself, become possible" (A 123). Kant's "strange" registers the surprise of grounding empirical association in a transcendental function. "Horizon" re-describes Kant's "transcendental affinity", which Kant defines through laws: appearances "stand in a thoroughgoing connection according to necessary laws, and hence in a transcendental affinity" (A 114). Kant's affinity is lawfulness. Heidegger's horizon is the play-space of encounter. The two meet in the idea of a prior order.

**¶4, 83.** All connecting includes a prior representing of unity, which must be a priori and "invariably one and the same"; this "'fixed and lasting' self ... is the I of transcendental apperception"; pure apperception must be added if taking-in is to be sustained by a pure turning-toward (A 124). Kant: "For the standing and lasting I (of pure apperception) constitutes the correlate of all of our representations" (A 123). The quotation is exact. Kant's "standing and lasting I" will matter in §34, where Heidegger reads it as time's own standing. Here it is the correlate of all representations, as Kant has it.

**¶5, 83–84.** Apperception is not isolated and does not merely join the imagination; it must have before it a unity forming itself in unifying; so the second way too brings forth the imagination as mediator: "We thus have a pure power of imagination as a fundamental faculty of the human soul which serves as a basis for all knowledge a priori. By means of this, we bring the manifold of intuition {into connection} on the one hand, and we bring {this} into connection with the condition of the necessary unity of pure apperception on the other" (A 124). Heidegger's note defends the "and" against Erdmann's and Riehl's deletion: without it the sentence loses the sense that the imagination unites pure intuition in itself and unites it with apperception. Kant's German in the corpus has the "and" (*das Mannigfaltige der Anschauung einerseits, und mit der Bedingung ... andererseits in Verbindung*). Guyer and Wood follow the deletion's sense: "By its means we bring into combination the manifold of intuition on the one side and the condition of the necessary unity of apperception on the other." Heidegger's two bracketed insertions supply the grammar his reading requires. The "and" makes the sentence irregular, and two readings are possible. On the editors' reading the imagination brings two terms into combination with each other. On Heidegger's it performs two unifications. The textual point is defensible, and its weight is large: the first unification, of intuition "in itself", is what will make pure intuition a product of the imagination (§28). "Fundamental faculty of the human soul" (*Grundvermögen*) is Kant's phrase, and §27 will build on it. A 94 had made the imagination one of three underivable sources, and the phrase is consistent with that.

**¶6, 84.** The triad of pure intuition, pure imagination and pure apperception "is no longer a juxtaposition of faculties"; the deduction has shown the inner possibility of the unity of pure knowledge, which "forms the pure letting-something-stand-against" and makes manifest "an horizon of objectivity in general"; since pure knowledge thus opens the space "in which 'all relation of Being or Not-Being takes place'" (A 110), it must be called ontological. The A 110 phrase has another subject in Kant: "just as there is only one space and time, in which all forms of appearance and all relation of being or non-being take place" (A 110). Kant says this of space and time. Heidegger says it of the play-space formed by pure knowledge. The transfer anticipates §28, where pure intuition (space and time) is shown to be formed by the imagination, but at this point it is a change of subject in a quoted clause.

**¶7, 84.** Since finitude is marked by the understanding, it plays a special role in the deduction; but on the two ways "the understanding gives up its preeminence", and its essence lies in having to be grounded in the time-related pure synthesis of the imagination. This is the stage's conclusion, and Kant's A text ends the other way. After the passage Heidegger has just quoted, Kant draws his own conclusion from both ways: the understanding is "the **faculty of rules**", "it is itself the legislation for nature, i.e., without understanding there would not be any nature at all", and "as exaggerated and contradictory as it may sound to say that the understanding is itself the source of the laws of nature ... such an assertion is nevertheless correct" (A 126–127). The A-Deduction's last pages give the understanding a preeminence stronger than anything in its earlier pages. Heidegger quotes A 126 for the "faculty of rules" in §16 and does not report what A 126–127 says next. On his reading the understanding gives up its preeminence. In the text he chose over B, it claims to legislate for nature.

##### §18. The External Form of the Transcendental Deduction

**¶1, 85.** Why does the deduction, "a 'laying before the eyes' of transcendence", take the form of a "*quaestio juris*"? What are the right and the limits of this juridical posing, which "obtrudes only in the first introduction to the Transcendental Deduction and not in the course of its being carried out"? The second claim is accurate for A. The legal frame is set out in the first section (A 84–92/B 116–124) and returns at the end of the A text only as a reference to the deduction's "objective validity" (A 128). Its claim to settle what the deduction is about is the issue.

**¶2, 85.** Kant uses "deduction" not in the philosophical sense opposed to *intuitus* (Descartes's *Regulae*) but as the "professor of law" uses it: establishing the facts (*quid facti*) and exhibiting the legal entitlement (*quid juris*), which jurists call a "Deduction". A reconstruction of A 84/B 116, and accurate. The Cartesian contrast is Heidegger's addition, apt since Descartes's *deductio* is the inferential sense Kant sets aside.

**¶3–¶4, 85.** Why the juridical form? Does a "legal action" underlie the problem? The question of metaphysica generalis arose from that of metaphysica specialis, which seeks rational knowledge of the supersensible; "The claim to a priori ontic knowledge lies in the pure concepts (categories)." Kant's concern at the head of the deduction includes the categories' use in experience. Empirical concepts need no deduction, but "there are also concepts that have been usurped, such as **fortune** and **fate**", and the categories, "destined for pure use *a priori*", need one (A 84–85/B 116–117). The legal action in Kant concerns the right of a priori concepts to relate to objects at all, including objects of experience, not only the supersensible. Heidegger connects the deduction to metaphysica specialis, and Kant's own text connects it to a priori concepts in general. The connection to the supersensible is real in the Dialectic, where reason's claims are tried. Here Heidegger moves it forward.

**¶5, 85–86.** The dispute with traditional metaphysics "regarding 'its ultimate purpose'" becomes a legal action; pure reason must "open the trial", "witnesses" are interrogated, Kant speaks of a "tribunal" (A 669/B 697; A 703/B 731); the pure concepts "always [demand] the Deduction" (A 85/B 117). The courtroom vocabulary is Kant's, and its main locus is not cited. The A preface calls the critique "a court of justice, by which reason may secure its rightful claims while dismissing all its groundless pretensions" (A xi–xii), and the Doctrine of Method calls it "the true court of justice for all controversies of pure reason" (A 751/B 779). The A 703/B 731 passage speaks of the "dialectical witnesses which a transcendent reason brings forward". The witnesses Kant hears are dialectical, the claims of reason beyond experience, not the categories in their use for experience. Heidegger's account of the trial is accurate for the Dialectic and the critique as a whole, and he applies it to the deduction.

**¶6, 86.** The categories' authority is decided by their essence: as representations of unity in a finite representing, they depend on pure synthesis and pure intuition; the solution to the *quaestio juris* is the unveiling of their essence: "they are not notions, but rather pure concepts which, by means of the pure power of imagination, refer essentially to time"; as such "they constitute transcendence" and are determinations of objects in advance. Kant's own result is close in content. The categories are valid because "only by means of them can any object of experience be thought at all" (A 93/B 126), and in the Schematism they are restricted to their temporal schemata (A 145–146/B 185). "They are not notions" contradicts Kant's definition (A 320/B 377), under which the categories are notions. Heidegger's thesis is Kant's Schematism read back into the deduction, and it makes the categories' essence temporal where Kant makes their use temporal.

**¶7, 86–87.** Their "objective reality" is thereby demonstrated; "reality" must not be taken in the sense of modern epistemology as actuality, which Kant calls "Dasein" or "existence"; "as Kant himself aptly translates it, 'realitas' means 'fact-ness' [*Sachheit*]", the content of a being, its essence; objective reality asks how the content of the pure concepts can determine what stands against finite knowledge. The terminological point is correct and has Kant's text behind it: transcendental affirmation "is called reality (thinghood)" (*Sachheit*), "because through it alone, and only so far as it reaches, are objects Something (things)" (A 574–575/B 602–603), and existence is a separate modal category (A 80/B 106). Heidegger's correction of "objective reality" against the Neo-Kantian reading is accurate philology. His gloss of objectivity as "what stands against finite knowledge" is his frame.

**¶8, 87.** If one interprets "objective reality" not from the pure synthesis of the imagination but clings to "objective validity", the term of the introductory juridical formulation, and takes validity as "the logical value of judgment", "the decisive problem will be completely lost from view". The target is the Southwest school's logic of validity (Rickert), which Commentary R names. Kant uses "objective validity" throughout the deduction, in its execution as well as its introduction: the problem is how "**subjective conditions of thinking** should have **objective validity**" (A 89–90/B 122), and the categories "therefore have *a priori* objective validity, which was just what we really wanted to know" (A 111). Heidegger's distinction of an introductory "validity" from a substantive "reality" is not Kant's distribution of the terms. The charge against the logic of validity holds against the Neo-Kantians, not against Kant's word.

**¶9, 87.** The problem of the "origin and the truth" of the categories (A 128) is "the question of the possible manifestness of Being from beings"; the *quaestio juris* is only the formula for "an analytic of transcendence, i.e., of a pure phenomenology of the subjectivity of the subject, namely, as a finite subject". Kant's phrase comes at the close of the A text: the deduction makes comprehensible "the objective validity of its pure *a priori* concepts, and thereby determine[s] their origin and truth" (A 128). Kant derives origin and truth from objective validity, the term Heidegger has just set aside. "Pure phenomenology of the subjectivity of the subject" puts Husserl's title over Kant's chapter, with finitude as the difference (Commentary R). Kant's chapter has its own name for the side of the deduction that concerns the subject: the "subjective deduction", which in the A preface he says "does not belong essentially" to his chief end (A xvi–xvii). Heidegger's formula makes the inessential side the essence of the deduction.

**¶10, 87–88.** If the deduction has solved the problem posed by metaphysica specialis, is the ground-laying finished? Does this not confirm the usual practice of treating the deduction as central? Why another stage? The questions lead to the Schematism. Kant's own judgment of the centre was the deduction (A xvi). Heidegger's question grants Kant's judgment as "the previous usage" and prepares to overrule it.

The Third Stage makes the A-Deduction load-bearing, chiefly A 104–110, A 115–128 and A 99, and it reads Kant's text more closely than any earlier stage. The quotations are again exact, and several readings hold. The order of the A-Deduction's third section is reconstructed faithfully. Kant's A 104 does lead from the object to the unity of consciousness and the concept as rule. The imagination is Kant's mediator in A 119–124. The defence of the "and" at A 124 is a sound reading of the transmitted text. And *realitas* means *Sachheit* in Kant (A 574–575/B 602–603). The take-up departs at four points. First, single words are re-inscribed: the adverb *dawider* becomes a "Being-in-opposition" and a "resistance of Being", *Vermögen* becomes the free "I can", "productive" becomes "formative", and Kant's "something in general = X" becomes "a nothing". Second, quotations stop before Kant's positive claim: representation "is still determinant of the object *a priori*" (A 92/B 125), the categories as the understanding's possession of the imagination's unity (A 119), the understanding as "the legislation for nature" and "source of the laws of nature" (A 126–127). Third, the preposition *vor* at A 118 is read as *coram* on the evidence of an A-only Latin phrase, where *für* and "prior to" are both live readings. Fourth, the decision to read A alone rests on the thesis of §31, against Kant's statement that B altered "nothing" in the propositions or proofs, only the presentation (B xxxvii–xxxviii). Heidegger concludes that "the understanding gives up its preeminence". The A text he chose concludes with the understanding as lawgiver of nature. The stage's verdict is therefore a contestation of Kant's own conclusion, presented as a reading of Kant's text.

#### The Fourth Stage of the Laying of the Ground: The Ground of the Inner Possibility of Ontological Knowledge

**¶1–¶2, 88.** The inner possibility of ontological knowledge has shown itself from the totality of transcendence, whose holding middle is the pure imagination; Kant finds this result "strange" and stresses the obscurity of the deduction; the ground-laying aims at a systematic presentation of transcendence, "(transcendental philosophy=ontology)"; but in the deduction, given finitude and "the dominance of the logical (rational) way of posing the question", the understanding stood in the foreground. "Strange" is Kant's word at A 123, said of the affinity of appearances, not of the imagination's role as such. The equation in parentheses, transcendental philosophy as ontology, has Kant's warrant at A 845/B 873 (above, §3). The description of the deduction as dominated by the understanding is the verdict of §17, against Kant's own A 126–127, and it is now the premise for turning to the Schematism.

**¶3, 89.** If all knowledge is primarily intuition, the relation of the imagination and the understanding to pure intuition must be discussed explicitly, which means presenting "the self-forming of transcendence and its horizons" in its "innermost occurrence"; Kant does this in the section "On the Schematism of the Pure Concepts of the Understanding" (A 137–147/B 176–187). The order follows Kant's. The Schematism comes after the deduction and concerns the sensible condition of applying the categories. Kant's own title for its place is the "transcendental doctrine of the power of judgment" (A 136/B 175), which Heidegger does not name here. In Kant the Schematism belongs to the doctrine of judgment, which teaches how to apply concepts. Heidegger makes it the self-forming of transcendence, the ground of the whole.

**¶4, 89.** This placement "betrays the fact that these eleven pages of the *Critique of Pure Reason* must constitute the central core of the whole voluminous work". This is the book's best-known claim, and it is made on structural grounds before any exegesis. Kant's own ranking was different. Kant knew of "no investigations more important for getting to the bottom of that faculty we call the understanding" than those of the deduction, which "have cost me the most, but I hope not unrewarded, effort" (A xvi). A late note that Heidegger quotes at the end of §23 calls the Schematism "one of the most important" chapters, one among several. The claim of centrality is Heidegger's.

**¶5, 89.** Kant introduces the problem "in a more superficial form" as a question of subsumption; its justification, as with the *quaestio juris*, will follow the working out of the inner dynamic. The strategy repeats §18: Kant's stated framing is treated as a preliminary formula and reinterpreted from the execution. Kant's framing is not marginal here. Subsumption is what the "transcendental doctrine of the power of judgment" is about: judgment is "the faculty of **subsuming** under rules" (A 132/B 171), and the Schematism answers "how is the **subsumption** of the latter under the former ... possible?" (A 137–138/B 176–177).

##### §19. Transcendence and Making-Sensible

**¶1–¶2, 90.** Taking-in-stride requires a turning-toward that makes encounter possible; for the being to offer itself, "the horizon of its possible encountering must itself have the character of an offering"; this offering needs "a certain perceivability", "immediately capable of being taken in stride in intuition", so the horizon must present itself "as a pure look"; hence "the pure understanding must be grounded in a pure intuition which guides and sustains it". Heidegger's vocabulary (offer, perceivability, pure look) has no Kantian counterpart. The conclusion, that the pure understanding must be grounded in pure intuition, contradicts Kant's ordering in the B-Deduction, where the unity of space and time as intuitions "presupposes a synthesis, which does not belong to the senses", given "as the understanding determines the sensibility" (B 160–161 n.). In Kant the dependence runs both ways but the determining runs from the understanding. "Grounded in" is the claim of the Third Section, stated here.

**¶3, 90.** The finite creature must "'form' the look of the offering from out of itself"; since pure intuition stands in essential relation to pure synthesis, "the pure power of imagination carries out the forming of the look of the horizon"; it "forms" in a double sense, creating the horizon as free turning-toward and providing "something like an 'image' ['*Bild*']". The pun on *bilden* and *Bild* inside *Einbildungskraft* is Heidegger's engine, and Kant's text gives it something. Kant's imagination does form images: it is "the faculty for representing an object even without its presence in intuition" (B 151), and in A it brings the manifold "into an image" (A 120). What it does not do in Kant is form the "look of the horizon". The pure imagination's products in Kant are schemata, and a schema "is to be distinguished from an image" (A 140/B 179).

**¶4, 90–91.** "Image" is taken "in its most original sense", the look a landscape presents; and Kant says, on the second way of the deduction, that the imagination "must bring ... into an image" (A 120). The ellipsis matters, and Taft's note says so: "The passage makes much more sense, however, if the words Heidegger omitted are restored", namely "the power of imagination must bring the manifold of intuition into an image" (A 120). Kant's sentence concerns apprehension, the imagination's action "exercised immediately upon perceptions" (A 120), the most empirical of its tasks. Heidegger's cut removes the object of the verb, so that the sentence appears to say the imagination makes images as such, and it is enlisted for the pure image of the horizon. This is selection by ellipsis, flagged by his own translator.

**¶5, 91.** In this double forming "the ground for the possibility of transcendence is first visible"; transcendence is "finitude itself, so to speak"; since finite intuition is sensibility, the offering of the look "can only be a making-sensible [*Versinnlichung*] of the horizon". Kant has the operation and not the object. He requires "to make the mind's concepts sensible (i.e., to add an object to them in intuition)" (A 51/B 75), and the schemata "first realize the categories" by "sensibility, which realizes the understanding at the same time as it restricts it" (A 146–147/B 185–187). Kant makes concepts sensible. Heidegger makes the horizon sensible. Kant's making-sensible is also a restricting of the categories to appearances, and the word "restricts" plays no part in Heidegger's account.

**¶6–¶7, 91.** Seen from the understanding, letting-stand-against is representing unities that regulate unification (pure concepts); so "transcendence is formed in the making-sensible of pure concepts", which must be pure; "The pure making-sensible occurs as a 'Schematism.' The pure power of imagination gives schema-forming in advance the look ('image') of the horizon of transcendence." Kant's definition of schematism names another agent: "we will call this formal and pure condition of the sensibility, to which the use of the concept of the understanding is restricted, the **schema** of this concept of the understanding, and we will call the procedure of the understanding with these schemata the **schematism** of the pure understanding" (A 140/B 179). In Kant the schema is a product of the imagination (A 140/B 179; A 142/B 181) and the schematism is a procedure of the understanding. Heidegger assigns both to the imagination here, and only in §29 (151) does he quote Kant's "procedure of the understanding" and argue that this procedure is itself grounded in the imagination. The schema in Kant is also defined as the condition "to which the use of the concept of the understanding is restricted", and that limiting function does not appear in "the look of the horizon".

**¶8–¶9, 91–92.** Pure sensibility takes in stride in advance, yet finite intuition cannot produce a being; so pure making-sensible must be "the taking of something in stride which indeed is formed first of all in the taking-in-stride itself", a look that does not offer a being; what then is an image, how is the schema distinguished from it, and in what sense can a schema be an image? The questions are Heidegger's and the paradox of §7 returns: a receptivity that forms what it receives. Kant's pure intuition does not form what it receives. It is a form in which a manifold is given (A 20/B 34), and the a priori synthesis of that manifold is the work of the imagination under the understanding (B 151). Heidegger's "taking-in-stride that forms" unites in one act what Kant divides between sensibility and imagination. §28 will name the union.

##### §20. Image and Schema

**¶1–¶3, 92–93.** Making-sensible means creating a look (image) of something; image can mean the look of a determinate being at hand, a likeness (copy, after-image, model), or "look in general", whether of a being or a non-being. The analysis is Heidegger's phenomenology of the image, with a marginal note pointing to Linke's 1925/26 essay (later). Kant has no such typology. His distinction runs between the image as "a product of the empirical faculty of productive imagination" and the schema of sensible concepts as "a product and as it were a monogram of pure *a priori* imagination, through which and in accordance with which the images first become possible" (A 141–142/B 181). Kant's "monogram", the schema as a figure through which images are first possible, is close to Heidegger's schema-image and goes unmentioned in the Kantbook.

**¶4, 93.** "Kant used the expression 'image' in all three senses", without setting them against each other, and it is "questionable" whether these senses suffice for the Schematism. The claim about Kant's usage is broadly right. Kant's "image" ranges from the product of apprehension (A 120) to the drawn five points (A 140/B 179) to "the pure image ... time" (A 142/B 182). The doubt whether they suffice sets up a fourth sense, the schema-image, which Commentary R describes as "Heidegger's coinage, not Kant's". Kant has a word nearer to it than any of the three, the "monogram" of the previous unit.

**¶5–¶7, 93–94.** Empirical intuition gives a look of a "this-here", even a landscape as a whole, *species*; a photograph is a likeness that wants to show what it copies; a photograph of a death mask shows the "image" of the dead person; making-sensible thus means immediate empirical intuiting and contemplating a likeness. No Kant locus. The examples are Heidegger's. The death mask prepares the move from a likeness of one individual to a look of something "in general".

**¶8–¶9, 94.** Photograph, mask and corpse can also show "how something in general appears", the "appearance (*εἶδος*, *ἰδέα*)"; this unity for many is what a concept represents; making a concept sensible cannot mean getting an intuition of it, since intuition is *repraesentatio singularis*; "the concept is also essentially not capable of having a likeness taken". The Greek words place Kant's problem inside Plato's. The step from intuition as singular representation to the impossibility of an image of a concept is Kant's in substance: "No image of a triangle would ever be adequate to the concept of it. For it would not attain the generality of the concept" (A 140–141/B 180).

**¶10–¶12, 94–95.** What is the making-sensible of a concept? A perceived house shows how a house in general looks, not by our absorption in this look but because it shows "only" the "as ..." in which a house can appear; what we have in view is "the range of possible appearing as such", what "regulates and marks out how something in general must appear in order to be able, as a house, to offer the appropriate look"; this sketching-out "is no list ... of the 'features'". The house is Heidegger's example, as the dog is Kant's. The point, that the schema is a rule of possible looks and not a list of marks, has Kant's support: the concept of a dog "signifies a rule in accordance with which my imagination can specify the shape of a four-footed animal in general" (A 141/B 180). Heidegger's "range of possibilities" is his phenomenological gloss on Kant's "rule". The contrast with a list of marks is aimed at the logical theory of the concept as a sum of features (A 320/B 377: "by means of a mark, which can be common to several things"). The Kantbook sets Kant's Schematism against Kant's own logical definition of the concept, and the Schematism wins.

**¶13, 95–96.** What is meant can be meant only as what regulates its belonging in an empirical look; "If the concept in general is that which is in service to the rule, then conceptual representing means the giving of the rule for the possible attainment of a look in advance in the manner of its regulation"; such representing is "in itself a particular kind of making-sensible". "In service to the rule" echoes A 106 ("something that serves as a rule"). Heidegger draws the conclusion that the concept is essentially a making-sensible. Kant keeps concept and schema apart: the schema is the "representation of a general procedure of the imagination for providing a concept with its image" (A 140/B 179–180), and the concept is what the procedure serves. Heidegger merges them: conceptual representing is the giving of the rule of a look. The merger is the next section's thesis that "what logic refers to as a concept is grounded in the schema".

**¶14–¶16, 96.** This making-sensible gives no immediate look of the concept; the unity is not grasped but "has in view precisely the unity as regulative", by looking away from it to its regulating; "The representing of the process of regulation as such a [representing] is properly conceptual representing"; the representing of unity for many "was only an isolated element of the concept". No Kant locus. Heidegger's correction of his own §11 (the concept as unity) is also a correction of the Logic's definition he started from there. The structure of looking through a rule without looking at it is the structure of the ready-to-hand in *Being and Time*. Commentary R notes the parallel. In Kant's text the schema is "a rule of the synthesis of the imagination" (A 141/B 180), and nothing is said about whether it is thematic or not.

**¶17, 96–97.** The rule is represented in the "how" of its regulating, which is "the free 'imaging' [*Bilden*]" of a making-sensible, unbound to anything at hand; it occurs primarily in the imagination: "This representation of a general procedure of the power of imagination in providing an image for a concept I entitle the schema of this concept" (A 140/B 179–180); schema-formation is schematism; the image-character belongs to the schema and is neither a plain look nor a likeness: "the schema-image". The Kant definition is quoted exactly and is the strongest support in the chapter for Heidegger's reading: the schema is the imagination's procedure for providing a concept with its image. The word "free" is Heidegger's. Kant's schema of a sensible concept is a rule "in accordance with a certain general concept" (A 141/B 180) and is bound to it. The schema-image names Kant's own claim that images are possible only "through" and "in accordance with" the schema (A 142/B 181), the monogram passage, which the Kantbook does not cite.

##### §21. Schema and Schema-Image

**¶1, 97.** The schema-image will clarify the relation of schema to concept and image, on two kinds of concepts: the empirical (dog) and the pure sensible, mathematical (triangle, number). The two kinds are Kant's (A 140–141/B 180). Kant has a third kind, the pure concept of the understanding, which "can never be brought to an image at all" (A 142/B 181). Heidegger treats it in §22, where he will argue that it can after all be brought into a kind of image.

**¶2, 97–98.** Kant stresses that an "object of experience" "or an image of the same" never "attains" the empirical concept (A 141/B 180); this "not-attaining" is not a failure to copy, since an empirical look cannot copy a concept at all; the inadequacy belongs to the schema-image; the particular is "a possible example" of the one that regulates. Kant's sentence: "Even less does an object of experience or an image of it ever reach the empirical concept, rather the latter is always related immediately to the schema of the imagination, as a rule for the determination of our intuition in accordance with a certain general concept" (A 141/B 180). Heidegger's reading of the not-attaining as a structural, not a deficient, relation is faithful to the sentence's second half. The "example" comes from a marginal note pointing to the *Critique of Judgment* (§59); in the *Critique of Pure Reason* "examples are the leading-strings of the power of judgment" (A 134/B 173–174).

**¶3, 98.** "The representing of the rule is the schema", related to many possible schema-images; "The concept of dog signifies a rule according to which my power of imagination can specify the form of a four-footed animal in general, without being limited to any particular form which experience offers to me, or also to any possible image which I can present *in concreto*" (A 141/B 180). Exact, and faithful. The Kantbook here follows Kant closely.

**¶4, 98.** The not-attaining expresses the positive relation of schema-image to schema; "beyond the representation of this regulative unity of the rule, the concept is nothing. What logic refers to as a concept is grounded in the schema"; the concept "always refers immediately to the schema" (A 141/B 180). The last quotation is cut. Kant writes that the empirical concept "is always related immediately to the schema of the imagination, as a rule for the determination of our intuition in accordance with a certain general concept" (A 141/B 180). In Kant's sentence the schema determines intuition "in accordance with" a concept, so the concept is what the schema conforms to. Heidegger's "the concept is nothing beyond" the rule and "grounded in the schema" inverts the dependence. The marginal note, "here we can already see how matters stand with 'the' logic!", draws the polemical conclusion against formal logic. It is later than the text, though the inversion is already in the text.

**¶5–¶6, 99.** Kant says the empirical object reaches its concept "even less" than the image of a pure sensible concept reaches its own; mathematical schema-images are not more adequate as copies; a drawn triangle must be acute, right or obtuse, so its range is narrower and its scope wider; but the image has the face of a particular, while the schema has "in view" the unity of the rule. The reading of Kant's "even less" by way of the triangle follows Kant: an image "would always be limited to one part of this sphere", "right or acute, etc." (A 141/B 180). The phrase "in view" (*zur Absicht*) is Kant's (A 140/B 179: the imagination's synthesis "has as its aim ... only the unity in the determination of sensibility"). Faithful. The note about rough drawings cites the reply to Eberhard (not in the corpus).

**¶7–¶8, 99–100.** Only in the sense of the schema-image can five points be called "an image of the number five" (A 140/B 179); the number never looks like the points or like "5"; we have every number in "the representation of a method for representing a multitude (e.g., a thousand) in an image in accordance with a certain concept" (A 140/B 179); "In fact it is not images of objects but schemata that ground our pure sensible concepts" (A 140–141/B 180). The quotations are exact. Heidegger's gloss, that the five points show the number because they coincide "with the representation of the rule of the possible presentability of this number", is Kant's distinction between image and schema restated. Kant's thousand, which no one can survey, is where the method is available and the image is not. On the mathematical concepts the reading reconstructs Kant.

**¶9, 100–101.** Making concepts sensible is "a quite peculiar providing of peculiar images", irreducible to "pictorial presentation"; conversely, immediate empirical looking and the making of copies "are possible only on the grounds of the possible making-sensible of concepts in the manner of the Schematism". The second claim extends Kant. Kant says images "first become possible" through the schema (A 142/B 181), for sensible concepts. Heidegger makes all empirical seeing depend on schematism. The extension has a basis in Kant's view that perception without concepts is "blind" (A 51/B 75), and the B-Deduction's view that all apprehension stands under the categories (B 160–161). Heidegger does not appeal to either text. The schematism of concepts now grounds perception as such.

**¶10, 101.** "All conceptual representing is, in its essence, Schematism"; every perception of this house includes "the schematizing preliminary view" of house in general; "Hence Kant must say: 'This Schematism ... is a hidden art in the depths of the human soul ...'" (A 141/B 180); and if schematism belongs to finite knowledge, which centres in transcendence, transcendence must at its core be "a transcendental Schematism". The quotation has an ellipsis that matters. Kant's sentence is "This schematism of our understanding with regard to appearances and their mere form is a hidden art in the depths of the human soul, whose true operations we can divine from nature and lay unveiled before our eyes only with difficulty" (A 141/B 180). The GA 3 ellipsis removes "of our understanding with regard to appearances and their mere form". *Being and Time* quoted the full sentence, "Dieser Schematismus unseres Verstandes, in Ansehung der Erscheinungen und ihrer bloßen Form, ist eine verborgene Kunst ..." (SZ 23). In the Kantbook, where the schematism has become the imagination's, the words that make it the understanding's are gone. The cut is small and decisive at this point in the argument. Heidegger does face the phrase later, in §29 (151), where he quotes "the schematism of our understanding" and "the procedure of the understanding with these schemata" and argues that the understanding only "proceeds with" schemata that the imagination produces. Here, where the schematism is first claimed for finite knowing as such, the words are absent. "Hence Kant must say" turns Kant's statement of difficulty into a consequence of Heidegger's analysis.

##### §22. The Transcendental Schematism

**¶1, 101–102.** Schematism belongs necessarily to transcendence, and transcendence requires a pure making-sensible; it must now be shown that the pure making-sensible of the pure concepts (notions) "occurs in a transcendental Schematism". A transition. "Notions" here names the categories before their schematization, which agrees with Kant's own notion, a pure concept with its origin "solely in the understanding" (A 320/B 377).

**¶2, 102.** Schema-forming making-sensible aims to provide the concept with an image; "The schema brings itself, i.e., the concept, into an image"; the pure concepts thought in the "I think" need "an essentially pure visibility" if what stands against is to be perceived "as a Being-in-opposition"; so they must be grounded in pure schemata "that procure an image for them". The agent shifts again, as at §14 ("pure synthesis brings itself to the concept"). Kant's schema is "the representation of a general procedure of the imagination for providing a concept with its image" (A 140/B 179–180), and the imagination does the providing. Heidegger's "brings itself" makes the schema its own agent. The concepts' need of "pure visibility" is Heidegger's. In Kant the pure concepts need schemata to have "significance" and "relation to objects" (A 145–146/B 185), which is a need for application, not for being seen.

**¶3, 102–103.** But Kant says expressly: "the schema of a pure concept of the understanding is something that can never be brought to any image at all ..." (A 142/B 181); if bringing itself into an image belongs to the schema, "image" here can only mean certain kinds of image, the empirical and the mathematical; the notions cannot be brought into such images, since they represent the rules in which objectivity in general forms itself; "'Image' therefore means ... the kinds of schema-images which belong to the schemata of empirical and mathematical concepts". Heidegger meets the sentence that denies his thesis and restricts it. The ellipsis cuts what Kant says the pure schema is instead: "but is rather only the pure synthesis, in accord with a rule of unity according to concepts in general, which the category expresses, and is a transcendental product of the imagination, which concerns the determination of the inner sense in general, in accordance with conditions of its form (time) in regard to all representations, insofar as these are to be connected together *a priori* in one concept in accord with the unity of apperception" (A 142/B 181). Kant offers a positive alternative to the image: the pure synthesis as a determination of inner sense under the unity of apperception. Heidegger's restriction of "image" has some textual support, which follows in the next unit, but it overrides Kant's "never ... at all" (*in gar kein Bild*). Commentary R calls it violent and undisguised, and it is both. The continuation that names apperception as the measure of the determination is the part of the sentence not quoted.

**¶4, 103.** The deduction showed that the notions are related to time through the pure synthesis; "the innermost structure of this relationship as the innermost joining of transcendence" has not yet been clarified. A transition. "Innermost joining" restates the joint (*Fuge*) image of §14.

**¶5, 103.** Time, as pure intuition, provides a look before all experience; its pure look ("for Kant the pure succession of the sequence of nows") must be called "pure image"; Kant says so in the Schematism, "The pure image ... of all objects of the senses in general [is] time" (A 142/B 182), and at A 320/B 377 the notion is "the pure concept, insofar as it has its origin solely in the understanding (not in the pure image of sensibility)". The two citations are Kant's, and they give Heidegger's reading real support. Kant does call time a "pure image" and does speak of "a pure image of sensibility". The ellipsis in the first again removes the sentence's frame: "The pure image of all magnitudes (*quantorum*) for outer sense is space; for all objects of the senses in general, it is time" (A 142/B 182). Kant's pure images are images of magnitudes, and the next sentence gives the schema of magnitude, number. In Kant time is the pure image for the category of quantity. Heidegger takes it as the pure image for all categories. The sentence also settles a philological point from §17. Kant's German here reads *vor dem äußeren Sinne*, "for outer sense": Kant's *vor* means "for", which bears on Heidegger's *coram* reading of *vor der Apperzeption* (A 118). The "sequence of nows" is the ordinary concept of time from *Being and Time* §81, put into Kant's mouth with "for Kant". A marginal note glosses "of all objects" as "of their objectivity"; it is later than the text.

**¶6, 104.** So the schema of a pure concept can be brought into an image if "image" means "pure image"; "Time as 'pure image' is the schema-image and not just the form of intuition standing over against the pure concepts of the understanding"; time is the representation of a "single object" (A 31–32/B 47), so it is not only the necessary pure image of the schemata but "their only pure possibility of a look", showing "always only time and the temporal". The A 31–32/B 47 quotation is Kant's argument from the Aesthetic that time is an intuition, not a concept: "Different times are only parts of one and the same time. The representation that can be given only through a single object, however, is intuition" (A 31–32/B 47). Heidegger turns Kant's proof that time is intuition into the claim that time is the one schema-image of all categories. Kant does treat time as the common medium of the schemata ("the schemata are therefore nothing but *a priori* time-determinations", A 145/B 184). "Schema-image" is Heidegger's. And "not just the form of intuition" rejects the Aesthetic's account as insufficient, as promised in §10.

**¶7, 104–105.** For the manifold of pure concepts to have its image in this single look, the one pure image must be formable in many ways; the schemata "determine" time; "The schemata are therefore nothing but *a priori* time-determinations in accordance with rules" (A 145/B 184), or "transcendental time-determinations" (A 138/B 177), and as such "a transcendental product of the power of imagination" (A 142/B 181); this schematism forms transcendence a priori and is called "transcendental schematism". The three quotations are exact and well chosen. The articulation of one pure image into many reads Kant's list of the time-characters (series, content, order, sum total) as a differentiation of one look. Kant's own word is "determination", an act on inner sense, not the formation of images. Heidegger's account agrees with Kant on the facts and moves them into the vocabulary of looks.

**¶8, 105.** In transcendence the letting-stand-against occurs because ontological knowledge, as schematizing intuition, makes "the transcendental affinity of the unity of the rule a priori visible in the image of time and thereby capable of being taken in stride"; the transcendental schema has "an a priori corresponding character". "Visible" and "taken in stride" are Heidegger's terms. Kant's affinity (A 113–114, A 123) and schematism (A 145–146/B 185) are rules of synthesis. In Kant nothing is seen in "the image of time". Kant's "corresponding" is the A 143–144/B 183 formula for substance, used below.

**¶9, 105.** Kant takes the unity of the pure concepts from the table of judgments and gives the schemata accordingly; the four time-characters, "time-series, content of time, order of time, and the sum total of time" (A 145/B 184), are "not so much developed systematically from an analysis of time itself", but fixed in it "according to the order of the categories"; the interpretation grows briefer and ends in definitions. The criticism has Kant's own words behind it: the schemata "concern, according to the order of the categories, the time-series, the content of time, the order of time, and finally the sum total of time" (A 145/B 184–185). Kant derives the time-characters from the categories, and he does not claim to derive them from time. Heidegger's complaint, that time is articulated by an inherited table, is the Third Section's charge in germ. It is fair to the text.

**¶10, 105–106.** Kant has a right to his brevity, since the systematic working out of ontological knowledge in the system of principles must come upon the schematism, and "this does happen, though only within certain limits" (A 158 ff./B 197 ff.). A reconstruction. Kant's principles are formulated for the schematized categories: "we must not subsume the appearances under the categories *per se*, but only under their schemata" (A 181/B 223–224). The phrase "within certain limits" anticipates §35's judgment that the principles do not take the analysis of time further.

**¶11, 106.** The way into the obscurity of these structures "in the depths of our soul" lies open further, as Kant betrays by remarking: "Rather than pausing now for a dry and boring analysis of what is required for transcendental schemata of pure concepts of the understanding in general, we would rather present them according to the order of the categories" (A 142/B 181); is it only dryness and tedium that stop Kant? The answer is deferred (§35); a rough interpretation of the schema of substance will show that the doctrine is not "baroque" but drawn from the phenomena. The quotation is exact. The suspicion that more than tedium held Kant back is the first open statement of the thesis of shrinking back, here attached to a stylistic remark. Commentary R calls it a promissory note. Kant's sentence gives a reason of exposition; Heidegger's question makes it a symptom.

**¶12, 106–107.** "The schema of substance is the persistence of the real in time ..."
