(A 143/B 183); the First Analogy must be drawn in; substance as notion means "underlying" (subsistence); its schema represents underlying in the pure image of time; time as the pure succession of nows is "now" in every now and so "unchangeable and lasting", it "does not elapse" (A 143/B 183); time is not one lasting thing among others but gives "the pure look of something like remaining in general". Kant's sentence continues: "i.e., the representation of the real as a substratum of empirical time-determination in general, which therefore endures while everything else changes" (A 143/B 183), and its parenthesis adds: "Time itself does not elapse, but the existence of that which is changeable elapses in it" (A 143–144/B 183). Heidegger's reading of time as being "now" in every now is his interpretation of "does not elapse". In Kant the point is that time cannot itself be perceived (B 225) and needs a persistent substratum in appearance. Heidegger has time itself give the look of persistence, where Kant's First Analogy requires something persistent in perception because "time itself cannot be perceived" (B 225). Heidegger's time supplies the image Kant says it cannot supply by itself.

**¶13–¶14, 107.** What Kant "omits here" is the full content of the notion: substance is a category of relation, subsistence and inherence, so time is its pure image only if it presents that relation; and time as now-sequence is in each now also another now, so as the look of remaining it offers "at the same time the image of pure change in remaining". Kant does not omit the relation altogether: his schema contains "which therefore endures while everything else changes" (A 143/B 183), and the First Analogy speaks of substance "in all change of appearances" (B 224). Heidegger's addition makes explicit what Kant's clause contains. The reading of change and persistence as two aspects of the now-sequence is his. In Kant, change belongs to "that which is changeable", in time, not to time.

**¶15, 107–108.** Even this rough interpretation shows that the notion of substance can procure a pure image a priori in time; objectivity is made visible in advance; "To time, therefore, which is itself unchangeable and lasting, there corresponds in appearance that which is unchangeable in existence" (A 143–144/B 183), existence here meaning being-at-hand. The quotation is exact, and Heidegger's gloss of *Dasein* in Kant's sense as "being-at-hand" (*Vorhandensein*) is correct for Kant's usage. Heidegger cut the next words, "i.e., substance, and in it alone can the succession and simultaneity of appearances be determined in regard to time". In Kant, substance is what time-determination requires. In Heidegger, time is what makes substance visible. The two directions meet in the word "corresponds".

**¶16, 108.** "The transcendental Schematism is thus the ground of the inner possibility of ontological knowledge"; time gives the horizon of transcendence the character of a perceivable offer, and as "the only pure universal image" it gives it "a preliminary enclosedness" and offers "something like a halt [*Einhalt*]", making the "Being-in-opposition" of objectivity perceivable for a finite being. The first sentence is the Fourth Stage's thesis. Kant's corresponding sentence says that the schemata are "the true and sole conditions for providing them [the categories] with a relation to objects, thus with **significance**" (A 145–146/B 185). Kant names a condition of significance, Heidegger a ground of possibility. Kant's sentence continues: "hence the categories are in the end of none but a possible empirical use". "Halt" and "enclosedness" are Heidegger's. The resistance he found in A 104's *dawider* now rests in time.

##### §23. Schematism and Subsumption

**¶1, 108–109.** The Schematism has been interpreted "with the sole orientation toward the innermost occurrence of transcendence", but Kant introduces his decisive doctrines through familiar formulations; the deduction started from the legal action and ended by fixing the conditions of the "use" of the categories. Heidegger concedes that his orientation is deliberate, and the concession is accurate. Kant's chapter is oriented toward use: "the possibility of applying **pure concepts of the understanding** to appearances in general" (A 138/B 177).

**¶2, 109.** Using concepts means applying them to objects, bringing objects "under" concepts; traditional logic calls this subsumption; using the pure concepts as transcendental time-determinations, performing pure knowledge, "means: the occurrence of the Schematism"; so the problem can be explained by subsumption, if one notes that an ontological "subsumption" is meant. A reconstruction, with "ontological" for Kant's "transcendental" as usual. Kant's doctrine of judgment is a "transcendental doctrine of the power of judgment" because it shows "the possibility of applying pure concepts of the understanding to appearances in general" (A 138/B 177).

**¶3, 109–110.** Kant already distinguished between bringing "under concepts" (what concerns objects) and bringing "to concepts" (what concerns the pure synthesis of the imagination) (A 78 ff./B 104 ff.); the bringing-to-concepts of the pure synthesis occurs in the transcendental schematism, which "forms" the unity represented in the notion into an essential element of objectivity; "In the transcendental Schematism the categories are first formed as categories", and if they are the true "original concepts" (*Urbegriffe*), "the transcendental Schematism is the original and authentic concept-formation in general". The distinction is in Kant's German and is real: representations are brought "under a concept" analytically by general logic, while "not the representations but the pure synthesis of the representations" is brought "to concepts" by transcendental logic (A 78/B 104). Guyer and Wood render both as "bring under concepts", and Heidegger's German is the better guide here. Kant also says who does the bringing: "to bring this synthesis to concepts is a function that pertains to the understanding" (A 78/B 103). Heidegger places it in the schematism, and so in the imagination. "Original concepts" is Kant's word for the categories (A 81/B 107). The claim that the schematism is the original concept-formation reverses Kant's order, in which the categories are given in the understanding and schematized in their application (A 140/B 179; A 147/B 187).

**¶4, 110.** By introducing the Schematism through subsumption, Kant wants to lead to "transcendental subsumption as the central problem" and show that "the question of the inner possibility of original conceptuality in general" breaks open. The intention ascribed to Kant is Heidegger's construction. Kant's stated intention is the question "how is ... the **application** of the category to appearances possible?" (A 138/B 177), and he calls it "so natural and important" that it "makes a transcendental doctrine of the power of judgment necessary". On Kant's account the problem is application. On Heidegger's it is the conceptuality of concepts. The texts agree that the categories are unlike all other concepts, and Heidegger draws from this the consequence that their being as concepts is in question.

**¶5, 110.** Empirical concepts are "homogeneous" with what they determine; but "pure concepts of the understanding, however, in comparison with empirical (indeed in general sensible) intuitions, are entirely un-homogeneous, and can never be encountered in any intuition. Now how is the subsumption of the latter under the former ... possible ...?" (A 137–138/B 176–177); in the question of their use, the categories' essence becomes a problem, the question of their "formation"; talk of subsumption "under categories" contains the question in what sense one may still speak of subsumption "under concepts". The quotation is exact. Heidegger's inference, that heterogeneity puts the categories' conceptuality in question, goes beyond Kant's, which concludes that "there must be a third thing" (A 138/B 177). For Kant the heterogeneity calls for mediation. Heidegger takes it to show that the categories are not concepts in the ordinary sense.

**¶6–¶7, 111.** Taken as an introduction, the subsumption formula even points to the core: conceptual representing is representing "in general"; since the categories are not homogeneous with empirical objects, their generality cannot be that of a highest genus; what then is the "generalis" of metaphysica generalis? "The problem of the Schematism of the pure concepts of the understanding is the question concerning the innermost essence of ontological knowledge." The question about the generality of ontological concepts is Heidegger's, and it is the Aristotelian question of the generality of being, which is no genus. Kant's own answer to how the categories are general is that they are "concepts of an object in general" (B 128), forms of thought for objects of any intuition, which through their schemata are restricted to ours (A 146–147/B 186–187). Heidegger's question takes Kant's Schematism to a problem of Aristotle's *Metaphysics* that Kant does not raise.

**¶8, 111.** If the Schematism chapter poses the problem of the conceptuality of the original concepts and solves it by determining them as transcendental schemata, "then the doctrine of the Schematism of the pure concepts of the understanding is the decisive stage of the laying of the ground for *Metaphysica Generalis*". The conclusion follows from Heidegger's premises. On Kant's premises, as the chapter states them, the Schematism is the condition of the categories' application and the beginning of the doctrine of judgment (A 136–137/B 175–176).

**¶9, 111–112.** The orientation by subsumption also prefigures the solution: if the pure concept and the appearances are wholly heterogeneous, there must be a mediating term: "This mediating representation must be pure (without anything empirical) and yet *intellectual* on the one hand and *sensible* on the other. Such a representation is the *transcendental schema*" (A 138/B 177); and "an application of the category to appearances becomes possible by means of the transcendental time-determination which, as the schema of the concept of the understanding, mediates the subsumption" (A 139/B 178). Both quotations are exact. Here Heidegger lets Kant's formula of mediation stand, and in it the schema is a third thing between concept and intuition. On his own reading the schema is the ground of both, and the two pictures are not reconciled in the paragraph.

**¶10, 112–113.** There is no reason to complain of the chapter's disunity; "If anything in the *Critique of Pure Reason* is articulated with the utmost rigor and measured in every word, then it is this core of the whole work"; its seven parts are listed (from the introduction by subsumption, A 137/B 176, to the "critical application" at the end); the chapter "is not 'confused'" but built with "unprecedented assurance", which becomes visible only if "the finitude of transcendence" is grasped as the ground. The defence of the chapter's construction is accurate as a description of its parts. Kant's text does run from subsumption through the general character of schemata to the individual schemata and the restriction of the categories (A 137–147/B 176–187). The defence is aimed at Kant's critics, who called the chapter confused. That Heidegger defends Kant's construction and in §§22–23 cuts two of Kant's sentences short is the measure of how much the reading needs the chapter. The condition he names, the finitude of transcendence, is the reading's premise, not the chapter's.

**¶11, 113.** Kant wrote in 1797: "In general the schematism is one of the most difficult points. Even Herr Beck cannot find his way in it. — I hold this chapter to be one of the most important" (Reflexion 6359, not in the corpus). The quotation supports importance, not primacy: "one of the most important", among others. The note dates from the period of Kant's correspondence with Beck, the period of the *Opus postumum*, and it is the late Kant once more fixing the sense of the *Critique*.

The Fourth Stage is the centre of the book's take-up, and its reading of the Schematism holds against Kant's text in several places. The concept of a sensible object is a rule for the imagination's synthesis (A 141/B 180), schemata are transcendental time-determinations (A 138/B 177; A 145/B 184), time is a "pure image" (A 142/B 182), the notion is defined against "the pure image of sensibility" (A 320/B 377), Kant distinguishes bringing "under" from bringing "to" concepts (A 78/B 104), and the time-characters follow the order of the categories (A 145/B 184–185). These are Kant's, and some of them were underread before Heidegger. The departures are concentrated and consistent. Three quotations are cut where Kant names a different agent or a narrower frame. "This schematism ... is a hidden art" omits "of our understanding with regard to appearances and their mere form" (A 141/B 180), which *Being and Time* had quoted whole and which the Kantbook confronts only fifty pages later (§29, 151). "The pure image ... is time" omits "of all magnitudes" and space (A 142/B 182). "Can never be brought to any image at all ..." omits Kant's positive definition of the pure schema as a pure synthesis under the unity of apperception (A 142/B 181). A fourth, at A 120, omits the object of the imagination's image-making, which Taft's note restores. Kant's agents are moved. Kant calls the schematism "the procedure of the understanding" (A 140/B 179), and bringing to concepts "a function that pertains to the understanding" (A 78/B 103). Heidegger has "the schema brings itself into an image" and makes the schematism the imagination's own act. Kant's limit becomes ground: the schema is the condition "to which the use of the concept of the understanding is restricted" (A 140/B 179), and the schematism makes the categories of "none but a possible empirical use" (A 146/B 185). Heidegger calls it "the ground of the inner possibility of ontological knowledge" and leaves out the restriction. Kant's verdict on the chapter, "one of the most important", becomes "the central core of the whole voluminous work". What the stage establishes against Kant's text is a reading of real power. Kant's account of the schema as rule and of time as the medium of all schemata is presented with a precision earlier commentary lacked. And on four occasions the reading cuts the sentence that tells against it.

#### The Fifth Stage of the Laying of the Ground: The Full Essential Determination of Ontological Knowledge

**¶1–¶2, 113–114.** With the transcendental schematism the goal of the ground-laying was attained; a fifth stage "should take possession explicitly of the ground which has been won", appropriating the stages in their unity; Kant lays down this determination in "the highest principle of all synthetic judgments" (A 154–158/B 193–197), which must therefore contain "the most central determination of the essence of transcendence". Heidegger's order (Schematism, then highest principle) is Kant's (A 137–147/B 176–187, then A 148–158/B 187–197). In Kant the highest principle opens the system of principles, which goes on to the Axioms, Anticipations, Analogies and Postulates (A 158–235/B 197–294). Heidegger makes the highest principle the end of the ground-laying and stops there. The system of principles, where Kant's categories do their work on nature, lies outside the five stages. The 1965 note named the lecture course that later supplied it.

##### §24. The Highest Synthetic Principle as the Full Determination of the Essence of Transcendence

**¶1, 114–115.** Kant introduces this doctrine against traditional metaphysics, which wants to know beings "from mere concepts"; mere thinking joins subject and predicate and "must be merely explanatory, 'analytic,' because it has 'played merely with representations'" (A 155/B 195); its highest principle is the "Principle of Contradiction" (A 150 ff./B 189 ff.); mere thinking is no knowledge but a necessary element of finite knowledge. The quotation is placed in another context. Kant's phrase belongs to the sentence on objective reality: if an object cannot be given, "the concepts are empty, and through them one has, to be sure, thought but not in fact cognized anything through this thinking, but rather merely played with representations" (A 155/B 195). Kant says this of concepts without an object, not of analytic judgments. Analytic judgments are for Kant legitimate cognitions, and the principle of contradiction counts "as the universal and completely sufficient **principle of all analytic cognition**" (A 151/B 191). The step from "analytic" to "mere play" is Heidegger's. It assimilates Kant's analytic judgment to empty thought, so that the synthetic judgment alone counts as knowledge. A marginal note asks for a distinction between the subject-object relation and the analytic judgment; it is later than the text and corrects the conflation.

**¶2, 115.** If the predicate belongs to knowledge, what matters is the relation of the whole subject-predicate relation to "something wholly other" (A 154/B 193–194), the being itself, with which knowing must be "in accord"; knowing must "go beyond"; Kant calls this relation synthesis, "the Veritative Synthesis". The quotation is turned. Kant: "In synthetic judgments, however, I am to go beyond the given concept in order to consider something entirely different from what is thought in it as in a relation to it" (A 154/B 193–194). Kant's "something entirely different" is another predicate, a content not thought in the subject concept. Heidegger's "wholly other" is the being itself. Kant's sentence is about the relation between two concepts, which a third thing must mediate (A 155/B 194). Heidegger's reading is about the relation between knowing and beings. The words "going beyond" and "the wholly other" pass from logic to ontology without a change of letter. "Kant calls this ... synthesis (the Veritative Synthesis)" attaches Heidegger's term to Kant's word.

**¶3, 115–116.** This going-beyond requires "a Being-in-there [*Darinnensein*], in a 'medium'" (A 155/B 194), which Kant names: "It is but one quintessence [*Inbegriff*] in which all our representations are contained, namely, the inner sense and its a priori form, time. The synthesis of the representations rests on the power of imagination, but their synthetic unity (which is required for judgment) [rests] on the unity of apperception" (A 155/B 194). The quotation is exact (Guyer and Wood: "There is only one totality"). Kant's next sentence draws the consequence: "Herein therefore is to be sought the possibility of synthetic judgments, and, since all three contain the sources of *a priori* representations, also the possibility of pure synthetic judgments" (A 155/B 194–195). "All three ... the sources" is A 94's doctrine again, the three sources side by side. The "Being-in-there" is Heidegger's gloss on "medium", and it makes Kant's medium of judgment the place where a finite being stands.

**¶4, 116.** The triad recurs; the third and fourth stages showed that the three form a structural unity "whose formative center is the transcendental power of imagination"; Kant's recollection of the triad must now be read "in the transparency of its structure, which is finally revealed in the Transcendental Schematism". The instruction tells the reader to read Kant's list as something other than a list. Kant's sentence presents three sources each grounding something: time contains all representations, imagination their synthesis, apperception their unity (A 155/B 194). Heidegger's "formative center" is the result of his reading of A 124 and A 142. Kant's text at A 155 offers no centre, and Heidegger's instruction supplies it.

**¶5–¶6, 116–117.** Kant brings the whole problem into the formula "possibility of experience" (A 156 ff./B 195 ff.); "possible" here is not opposed to actual but means what makes experience possible, the *possibilitas* of traditional metaphysics, "synonymous with *essentia* or *realitas*"; real definitions are taken "from the essence of the matter, from the initial ground of possibility" (Jäsche *Logic* §106, not in the corpus; B 302 n., A 596/B 624 n.). The equation of possibility with essence is the school metaphysics that Kant inherited, and the *Critique*'s note on real definitions supports it: a real definition "does not merely supply other and more intelligible words for the name of a thing, but rather contains in itself a clear **mark** by means of which the **object** ... can always be securely cognized" (A 241 n.). Kant adds, in the same chapter, that "we cannot even give a real definition of a single one of them [the categories], i.e., make intelligible the possibility of their object, without immediately descending to conditions of sensibility" (A 241/B 300). That sentence supports Heidegger's identification of the categories' possibility with their schematized sense, and he does not quote it. The marginal note "incomplete—but important here" is later.

**¶7, 117.** "The possibility of experience, then, is that which a priori gives objective reality to all our cognitions" (A 156/B 195); "Possibility of experience is therefore synonymous with transcendence." The quotation is exact. The equation follows from Heidegger's earlier definitions and not from Kant's sentence. Kant's next sentence says what the possibility of experience rests on: "Now experience rests on the synthetic unity of appearances, i.e., on a synthesis according to concepts of the object of appearances in general" (A 156/B 195), "thus ... the transcendental and necessary unity of apperception" (A 156/B 196). In Kant, the possibility of experience is the synthetic unity of appearances under apperception. Heidegger's "transcendence" takes over Kant's phrase and leaves its ground, apperception, unnamed.

**¶8, 117–118.** "That an object is given" means "it 'is presented immediately in intuition'", which means "to relate the representation {of the object} to experience (be it actual or still possible)" (A 156/B 195); this requires a prior turning-toward what can be "summoned", which occurs in the ontological synthesis. The quotation is exact. The word "summoned" (*herbeigerufen*) comes from the next sentence of Kant's, unquoted and unattributed. There the representation of space and time "is a mere schema, which is always related to the reproductive imagination that calls forth the objects of experience" (A 156/B 195). In Kant it is the reproductive imagination that summons. Heidegger takes Kant's word for an empirical function and gives it to the pure turning-toward. The same sentence calls the pure representation of space and time "a mere schema", which supports Heidegger's link between pure intuition and the schema (§28).

**¶9, 118.** A second condition: truth means "accordance with the Object" (A 157/B 196); so a "with-what" of possible accordance must be encountered in advance, a regulating standard; the horizon of standing-against must be open and perceivable. Kant's sentence: experience "as *a priori* cognition ... also possesses truth (agreement with the object) only insofar as it contains nothing more than what is necessary for the synthetic unity of experience in general" (A 157/B 196–197). Kant limits a priori cognition's truth to what experience requires. Heidegger reads the same clause as the requirement of a standard given in advance. The marginal note pointing to A 237, the principles as "source of all truth", is later.

**¶10–¶11, 118.** The two conditions together delimit transcendence in one proposition; its "conclusive formulation" is "the conditions for the possibility of experience in general are at the same time conditions for the possibility of the objects of experience" (A 158/B 197). Heidegger quotes the formula that follows Kant's statement of the principle, not the statement itself, and he cuts the formula's end. Kant's principle is stated first: "The supreme principle of all synthetic judgments is, therefore: Every object stands under the necessary conditions of the synthetic unity of the manifold of intuition in a possible experience" (A 158/B 197). The formula Heidegger quotes then ends: "and on this account have objective validity in a synthetic judgment *a priori*" (A 158/B 197). Kant's statement makes the principle a claim about objects standing under conditions of synthetic unity. Its last clause makes it a claim about objective validity, the term §18 dismissed as introductory. Heidegger quotes the middle and calls it the conclusion.

**¶12, 118–119.** The decisive content lies not in what Kant spaced but in the "are at the same time" (*sind zugleich*), which expresses the unity of transcendence: the letting-stand-against "forms the horizon of objectivity in general"; going out-to is "a constant standing-out-from ... (*Ecstasis*)", which forms and holds before itself a horizon; "In itself, transcendence is ecstatic-horizontal." Heidegger states openly that he moves the emphasis from Kant's spacing ("**possibility of experience**", "**possibility of the objects of experience**") to an unemphasized "at the same time". The terms "ecstatic" and "horizontal" are those of *Being and Time* §69, where temporality is ecstatic and has horizontal schemata. The highest principle becomes the formula of temporality's structure before time has been mentioned in the reading of it. Kant's "at the same time" says that one set of conditions does double duty. Heidegger hears in it the unity of two moments of one structure, going out and holding a horizon. The double duty supports the unity. The ecstatic-horizontal structure is Heidegger's.

**¶13, 119.** The "at the same time" does not mean co-occurrence, joint thinkability or identity; the principle "is no principle that is arrived at in the drawing of a conclusion that we must put forth as valid if experience is to hold true", but "the expression of the most original phenomenological knowledge of the innermost, unified structure of transcendence"; a note calls it "the properly understood, metaphysical Principle of Sufficient Reason" (with *Vom Wesen des Grundes*). Kant's text describes the principle's status otherwise: synthetic a priori judgments "are possible, if we relate the formal conditions of *a priori* intuition, the synthesis of the imagination, and its necessary unity in a transcendental apperception to a possible cognition of experience in general" (A 158/B 197). The principle sums up the result of the deduction and the Schematism, a conditional to which the proofs of the principles appeal. Heidegger's denial that it is reached by inference is aimed at the Neo-Kantian reading of transcendental argument as regress from the fact of experience. Kant's "therefore" (*also*) and "In this way ... are possible, if" give the principle the form of a conclusion. Heidegger's "phenomenological knowledge" renames it.

##### §25. Transcendence and the Laying of the Ground for Metaphysica Generalis

**¶1–¶2, 120.** With the full structure of transcendence in view, the character of ontological knowledge, its knowing and its known, can be surveyed; the knowing is "a pure schematism", and the unity of the three elements is expressed in the transcendental schema as "transcendental determination of time". Kant's term is exact (A 138/B 177). In Kant, the three elements meet in the schema, which is their product (the imagination's), under their unity (apperception), in their medium (time). Heidegger's summary is fair to Kant here.

**¶3–¶4, 120.** If ontological knowledge forms the pure look, is it not "creative", and does the finite creature not become infinite? No: it is not creative as the *intuitus originarius* is; "Ontological knowledge not only does not create beings, but also it does not relate itself at all, thematically or directly, to the being." The question and answer settle the paradox of §7. The answer has a Kantian counterpart: the categories "do not represent any special object given to the understanding alone" (A 251). But Kant says "do not represent any special object", where Heidegger says the knowledge "does not relate itself at all ... to the being". In Kant the categories relate to beings, as appearances, through their schemata. They relate to no special object of their own.

**¶5, 121.** What is known in ontological knowledge? "A Nothing. Kant calls it the 'X' and speaks of an 'object.'" Two passages are interpreted: one "in the introduction to the Transcendental Deduction" (A 108 f.), the other in the chapter on phenomena and noumena (A 235 ff./B 294 ff.), which "concludes the positive laying of the ground for *Metaphysica Generalis*". Heidegger's own sentence registers Kant's words and departs from them: Kant calls it "X" and "object"; Heidegger calls it "a Nothing". The first passage is not in the introduction to the deduction but in the A text's second section, under the recognition in the concept (A 104–110). The second, as the next units show, is from the part of the phenomena and noumena chapter that Kant removed in B.

**¶6–¶7, 121–122.** The first passage: "Now we are also able to determine more correctly our concept of an *object* in general. All representations, as representations, have their object ... these appearances, however, are not things in themselves; rather, they are themselves only representations which in turn have their object—an object which can no longer be intuited by us and which may therefore be named the nonempirical, i.e., transcendental object = X" (A 108–109); the "terminus" of the prior turning-toward cannot be intuited empirically, which "includes ... the necessity of its immediate distinguishability in a pure intuition". The quotation is exact. The inference, that what cannot be intuited empirically must be perceivable in pure intuition, is Heidegger's. Kant's next sentence says how this object is thought: "The pure concept of this transcendental object (which in all of our cognitions is really always one and the same = X) is that which in all of our empirical concepts in general can provide relation to an object, i.e., objective reality" (A 109). In Kant the transcendental object is a concept. Heidegger makes it a "terminus" seen in pure intuition.

**¶8, 122.** The second passage: "All our representations are in fact referred to some Object through the understanding ... the understanding refers them to a *Something* as the object of the sensible intuition: but this *Something* as object of an intuition in general is to that extent only the transcendental Object. But this means a Something = X, of which we know nothing ... but rather which, as just a correlatum of the unity of apperception, can serve only for the unity of the manifold in sensible intuition. By means of this, the understanding unifies them in the concept of an object" (A 250; "the text corrected by Kant himself", Erdmann's *Nachträge*). The quotation is exact. Two facts about it bear on the take-up. The passage is A-only. Kant replaced A 249–253 in the second edition with a new discussion of noumena in a negative and a positive sense (B 306–309). So Heidegger's second "main passage" for the transcendental object is one Kant withdrew. And the quotation stops two sentences before Kant says what the X is: "This transcendental object cannot even be separated from the sensible *data,* for then nothing would remain through which it would be thought. It is therefore no object of cognition in itself, but only the representation of appearances under the concept of an object in general, which is determinable through the manifold of those appearances" (A 250–251). Kant's X cannot be separated from sensible data. Heidegger's next sentence makes it a "Nothing" that is pure horizon.

**¶9–¶10, 122–123.** The X is unknowable not because it hides behind appearances but because "it is a Nothing"; "Nothing means: not a being, but nevertheless 'Something'"; it "serves only as *correlatum*", i.e., "it is pure horizon"; Kant calls it the "transcendental object", i.e., "the Being-in-opposition [*das Dawider*] which is discernable in and through transcendence as its horizon"; the horizon must be unthematic yet in view. Kant's sentences use "nothing" once, and differently: if the transcendental object were separated from the sensible data, "nothing would remain through which it would be thought" (A 250). In Kant, nothing is what remains if the X is cut off from sensibility. In Heidegger, the X itself is a nothing because it is not a being. "Correlatum" in Kant is the correlate "of the unity of apperception". Heidegger's "pure horizon" makes the correlate of apperception into the horizon of transcendence, and apperception drops out of the sentence. The "Being-in-opposition" returns from A 104 and identifies Kant's adverb with Kant's X. Of all the book's readings this is the most interpretive. It has a Kantian basis in "something = X, of which we know nothing", and it departs from Kant in deciding what the X is without his continuation.

**¶11, 123.** "The X is 'object in general'", meaning not an indeterminate being but "the horizon of a standing-against"; this horizon is "a Nothing" if object means a being apprehended thematically, and ontological knowledge "is no knowledge if knowledge means: apprehending of beings". Kant's "object in general" is a concept: "the concept of something in general" (A 251), "the representation of appearances under the concept of an object in general" (A 251). Heidegger's horizon is not a concept. The final clause concedes that "ontological knowledge" is knowledge only in an extended sense. Kant's own name for it is "transcendental cognition" (B 25), cognition of our mode of cognition. In Heidegger it becomes a holding-open that knows no being.

**¶12, 123.** Ontological knowledge is knowledge if it attains truth; it "is the original truth, which Kant therefore terms 'transcendental truth'", elucidated by the schematism: "In the whole of all possible experience ... lies all our knowledge, and transcendental truth consists in the general relation to the same, which precedes all empirical truth and makes it possible" (A 146/B 185). The quotation is now complete, unlike in §3, and its clause of location is present: transcendental truth "consists in the general relation to" possible experience. Heidegger reads the relation as transcendence. Kant's previous sentence, which leads into it, says that "the categories are in the end of none but a possible empirical use" (A 146/B 185), and Heidegger uses that clause in the next unit.

**¶13, 123–124.** Ontological knowledge "forms" transcendence by "the holding-open of the horizon"; "If truth indeed means: unconcealment of ..., then transcendence is original truth"; truth "must bifurcate into the unveiledness of Being and the openness of beings"; "Kant says: ontological knowledge only has 'empirical use,'" serving finite experience. The definition of truth as unconcealment is Heidegger's, from *Being and Time* §44, and it enters as a conditional ("If truth indeed means"). Kant's definition, "agreement of cognition with its object" (A 58/B 82), stands against it, and §2 had granted that Kant presupposes that concept. The attribution "Kant says: ontological knowledge only has 'empirical use'" puts Kant's words about the categories (A 146/B 185) into a sentence about ontological knowledge. Kant does mean the a priori principles to have only empirical use. In Kant, though, the clause is a restriction: "none but a possible empirical use". Heidegger turns it into a service the horizon performs for finite experience.

**¶14, 124.** It must at least remain open whether this knowledge, "always only ontological and never ontic" in its creativity, bursts finitude or plants the finite subject in its proper finitude. No Kant locus. The question is answered in §§34–35 and the Fourth Section, where the formative imagination is shown to be time and so finitude itself. In Kant's text the question does not arise, because the a priori is the form of a receptivity and the spontaneity of a discursive understanding, neither of them creative.

**¶15–¶16, 124–125.** Ontology is "the explicit unveiling of the systematic whole of pure knowledge, to the extent that it forms transcendence"; "Kant wants to replace 'the proud name of an ontology' with that of a 'Transcendental Philosophy,' i.e., with an essential unveiling of transcendence", justified while "ontology" bears its traditional sense, which "presumes to give synthetic a priori knowledge of things in general"; once ontology grasps itself in its finitude, the word is justified, and Kant himself uses it so "in the decisive passage ... which sets forth the outline of metaphysics as a whole" (A 845/B 873). The report of A 247/B 303 is wrong in the one word that matters. Kant's sentence reads: "the proud name of an ontology, which presumes to offer synthetic *a priori* cognitions of things in general in a systematic doctrine (e.g., the principle of causality), must give way to the modest one of a mere analytic of pure understanding" (A 247/B 303). Kant replaces "ontology" with "analytic of pure understanding", not with "transcendental philosophy". The replacement name Heidegger reports comes from A 845/B 873, where transcendental philosophy is glossed "(*Ontologia*)". Joining the two passages lets Kant appear to rename ontology upward into transcendental philosophy. In fact A 247 renames it downward, to an analytic of the understanding whose principles "are merely principles of the exposition of appearances" (A 247/B 303). This is a misattribution, small in wording and large in effect. The sentence Kant wrote is the plainest statement in the *Critique* that the Analytic is not ontology in the sense Heidegger claims for it. The gloss "which can only come to an infinite creature" is Heidegger's. Kant's ground for the demotion is not infinity but appearance: the understanding "can never overstep the limits of sensibility, within which alone objects are given to us" (A 246/B 303).

**¶17, 125.** With the transformation of metaphysica generalis "the proper edifice of *Metaphysica Specialis* begins to totter"; this is not treated here, since it needs a more original appropriation of what Kant achieved "in the unity of the Transcendental Aesthetic and Logic". The Dialectic, where Kant tries the claims of metaphysica specialis and finds them illusory (A 293–704/B 349–732), is set aside. In the *Critique* the tottering of metaphysica specialis is not a consequence left for later. It is half the book, and it is Kant's verdict on the "final end" of metaphysics that §1 said Kant held to. The Kantbook's Second Section ends where Kant's treatment of the final end begins.

The Second Section is the book's sustained reading of Kant's text, and its pattern is now clear across five stages. Heidegger makes a sequence of Kantian texts load-bearing: the first sentence of the Aesthetic, the contrast of original and derived intuition, the root of the two stems, the metaphysical exposition of space and time, §10 of the clue, the A-Deduction's third section, the Schematism and the highest principle. He reads them in an order he constructs, the five stages, against the architectonic Kant gave them. Where the reading reconstructs, it holds against the text, and several of its points are now standard. Sensibility is defined by receptivity, not organs. Appearance and thing in itself are one object in two relations (B xxvii, B 69). The functions of judgment have no further ground (B 145–146). The category without schema has no object (A 147/B 187). The A-Deduction's two ways meet in the imagination (A 119–124). A 124's "and" can be defended. Kant's schema is a rule for the imagination's synthesis and time the medium of all schemata (A 138–145/B 177–184). The take-up departs in three repeated ways. First, Kant's illustrations and limits become grounds. The intuitus originarius, an "illustration ... not a ground of proof" (B 72), becomes the measure of finitude. The "unknown" root (A 15/B 29) becomes a direction. The schema, the condition "to which the use of the concept ... is restricted" (A 140/B 179), becomes the ground of possibility. Second, the agents of Kant's sentences shift: from the understanding to the imagination (A 78/B 103 and its correction, A 79/B 105, A 119, A 140/B 179, A 141/B 180), from the imagination to the synthesis or the schema itself ("brings itself"), from apperception to a horizon (A 250). Third, quotations stop before the clause that tells against the reading, and the quotations themselves remain exact. The clauses omitted are these: the separation of the two sources (A 51–52/B 76), the three underivable sources (A 94, quoted only in §27), the understanding as lawgiver of nature (A 126–127), "of our understanding" in the hidden-art sentence (A 141/B 180), the space and magnitude in "pure image" (A 142/B 182), Kant's positive definition of the pure schema (A 142/B 181), the formal statement and the "objective validity" clause of the highest principle (A 158/B 197), and the transcendental object's inseparability from sensible data (A 250–251). Once the report is wrong, at A 247/B 303. The reading is often defensible as extrapolation and sometimes as productive misreading. It is announced as a reading of Kant's "inner movement" against his "external architectonic". Measured against Kant's text, the inner movement is reached by keeping the A edition's words and dropping the clauses in which Kant, in either edition, gave the understanding the last word.

## Third Section: The Laying of the Ground of Metaphysics in Its Originality

**¶1–¶2, 126.** Can the ground-laying be grasped more originally, or is the urge for originality empty curiosity that ends in a critique "from outside", always unjust? To remain interpretation, the idea of originality "must be taken from the Kantian ground-laying itself"; first it must be fixed what the laid ground is. The principle is Kant's own, from the Architectonic: a science "must not be explained and determined in accordance with the description given by their founder, but rather in accordance with the idea, grounded in reason itself" (A 834/B 862). Heidegger does not cite it here either. The promise to take the measure from Kant is the Section's standard, and each of its steps can be checked against it. Kant's own measure of a ground, in the A preface, is certainty and completeness. "Opining" is not permitted (A xv), and the subjective deduction is subordinated because it resembles "a hypothesis" (A xvii). Heidegger's measure is originality, and it comes from the Introduction (GA 3, 2), not from Kant.

### A. The Explicit Characterization of the Ground Laid in the Laying of the Ground

#### §26. The Forming Middle of Ontological Knowledge as Transcendental Imagination

**¶1–¶2, 127.** Ontological knowledge "forms" transcendence, the holding-open of the horizon visible through the pure schemata, which "spring forth" as a "transcendental product" (A 142/B 181) of the transcendental imagination; it forms the unity of pure intuition (time) and pure thinking (apperception); it was central already in the deduction and named in the second stage; so it is "the ground upon which the inner possibility of ontological knowledge, and with it that of *Metaphysica Generalis*, is built". The phrase "transcendental product of the imagination" is Kant's (A 142/B 181). "Spring forth" is Heidegger's verb. The conclusion, that the imagination is the ground, compresses the readings of §§14, 17 and 22 that the earlier units examined. Kant's own statement of where the ground lies, in both editions, is the unity of apperception, "the highest point to which one must affix all use of the understanding" (B 133–134 n.), and "the ground of the possibility of all cognitions" (A 118). Heidegger names pure thinking "(apperception)" as one of the two things the imagination unites. In Kant, apperception is what the imagination's synthesis must be related to in order to be intellectual at all (A 124).

**¶3, 127–128.** Kant introduces the imagination as "an indispensable function of the soul" (A 78/B 103); laying bare the ground thus means determining "a faculty of the soul of man", which is "self-evident" if metaphysics belongs to the "nature of man"; so the *Anthropology*, which Kant lectured on for years, should tell us about the ground (Mörchen's dissertation is named for a full account). The *Anthropology* is not in the corpus. The argument tests a route in order to close it. The paragraph quotes A 78/B 103 in its printed form, "of the soul", and §31 will set Kant's own correction against it. "Nature of man" again has no locus in the *Critique* (Introduction ¶2).

**¶4–¶5, 128–129.** The *Anthropology* defines the imagination as "a faculty of intuitions even without the presence of the object"; it belongs to sensibility, the "lower" faculty; it is free in taking in looks and so in a sense gives them to itself; it is "formative" in a double sense, providing an image and making it, "at once a receptive (receptive) and a creating (spontaneous) 'forming'"; it falls between sensibility and understanding, and Kant classes it with sensibility despite its spontaneity (Aristotle's *phantasia* between *aisthesis* and *noesis* is noted). The double character Heidegger draws from the *Anthropology* is stated in the *Critique*'s second edition, at the place he cites only in the next paragraphs: "Imagination is the faculty for representing an object even **without its presence** in intuition. Now since all of our intuition is sensible, the imagination ... belongs to **sensibility**; but insofar as its synthesis is still an exercise of spontaneity, which is determining and not, like sense, merely determinable ... the imagination is to this extent a faculty for determining the sensibility *a priori*" (B 151–152). The B-Deduction contains the definition and the double character, receptive by belonging to sensibility and spontaneous by determining it. On Heidegger's §31 the B edition is the edition of retreat, and it is the B edition that gives the formula.

**¶6–¶7, 129–131.** The imagination is a faculty of comparing, shaping, combining (from the *Nachlass* and the metaphysics lectures, not in the corpus); it can present a past perception (*exhibitio derivativa*) or freely invent a look (*exhibitio originaria*), and is then "productive"; but it is not creative like the *intuitus originarius*, and cannot produce "a sense representation which was never given before" (*Anthropology* §28). The distinction of reproductive and productive imagination is also the *Critique*'s: "insofar as the imagination is spontaneity, I also occasionally call it the **productive** imagination, and thereby distinguish it from the **reproductive** imagination, whose synthesis is subject solely to empirical laws" (B 152). The point that the productive imagination is not creative of beings agrees with Kant's B 72. The *Anthropology*'s "original presentation" (*exhibitio originaria*) is a third Kantian use of "original", beside the underived representation of A 32/B 48 and the creative intuition of B 72, and Heidegger will join all three in §28.

**¶8–¶9, 131.** The *Anthropology* adds nothing the *Critique* had not shown more originally; but its definition, imagining "without the presence of the object", seems absent from the ground-laying, though it occurs in the deduction "only in the second edition" (B 151), and the Schematism showed the same character. Heidegger concedes that the definition he needs is a B text. The concession sits awkwardly with §31's account of B as the edition in which the imagination is "pushed aside". B 151–152 is where Kant introduces "the transcendental synthesis of the **imagination**", the "productive imagination" and the definition. On this point the B text says more about the imagination than A.

**¶10–¶11, 131–132.** The imagination forms in advance "the look of the horizon of objectivity" before all experience, never dependent on the presence of a being; its pre-forming of the schema of substance (persistence) first brings into view "something like constant presence" (*ständige Anwesenheit*), within whose horizon "this or that 'presence of an object'" can show itself; so the Schematism grasps the imagination's power to intuit without presence more originally, and shows its "creative" essence as "a free forming of images". "Constant presence" is the first appearance in the book of the formula that *Being and Time* §§6 and 81 and the later Heidegger use for the meaning of being in Western metaphysics. Kant's schema of substance is "the persistence of the real in time" (A 143/B 183), the persistence of something real in appearance. Heidegger reads Kant's persistence as presence. The re-inscription quietly sets up a later verdict (§35): Kant understood being as constant presence, which is a thesis about Kant that this sentence states as a reading of the schema. "Free forming" is again Heidegger's word, against Kant's rule-bound schema (A 141/B 180).

**¶12–¶14, 132–133.** The attempt to learn more from the *Anthropology* fails and is a mistake, since the *Anthropology* is empirical twice over, in its sources and in considering faculties only as they relate to experienceable beings; the productive imagination of the *Critique* concerns "the pure look of objectivity in general" and is rightly called transcendental; yet the *Anthropology*'s empirical account contains hints of transcendental structures. The distinction between the empirical and the transcendental use of faculties is Kant's at A 94: "In addition to their empirical use, all of these faculties have a transcendental one, which is concerned solely with form, and which is possible *a priori*." Heidegger quotes the sentence in §27. On the *Anthropology*'s status he agrees with Kant, who excludes empirical psychology from metaphysics (A 848–849/B 876–877).

**¶15, 133–134.** What knowing unveils transcendence? "Transcendental" says only that it has transcendence as theme; the method must be clarified, and the direction of regress read from the laid ground itself; the way into the *Anthropology* was a detour; the interpretation must keep to the imagination. "It has transcendence as theme" is Heidegger's gloss on Kant's "transcendental", the slide noted from §3 on. Kant's own account of the method of the deduction, "transcendental" because it concerns the possibility of a priori cognition (A 11–12/B 25; A 85/B 117), does not mention transcendence.

#### §27. The Transcendental Imagination as Third Basic Faculty

**¶1, 134.** Understanding the faculties "of our mind" as "transcendental faculties" means asking how they make transcendence possible; "faculty" (*Vermögen*) does not mean a "basic power" (*Grundkraft*) present in the soul but what a phenomenon "is able" to do (*vermag*), "possibility" in the sense of §24; so the imagination is a "basic faculty" with pure intuition and pure thinking. The reinterpretation of *Vermögen* as *vermögen*, "being able", is the same move as §17's "I can". Kant's text names the faculties otherwise: "three original sources (capacities or faculties of the soul)" (A 94), which Heidegger quotes four paragraphs later. Kant's "faculties of the soul" are what Heidegger says *Vermögen* does not mean.

**¶2, 134–135.** "Basic faculty" means irreducible to the pure elements with which it forms transcendence; so Kant lists three elements, pure intuition, the pure synthesis of the imagination, and the pure concepts of apperception (A 78–79/B 104); the Schematism lists them again; and the highest principle is introduced with the three "as the three sources" (A 155/B 194). The textual record is accurate. Kant lists three in each place, and the highest principle's "since all three contain the sources of *a priori* representations" (A 155/B 194–195) is in both editions. In these texts the imagination is one of three, irreducible and not a root.

**¶3, 135.** Against this stands Kant's explicit statement at the beginning and end of the *Critique*: only "two basic sources of the mind, sensibility and understanding", "two stems of our cognitive power", "other than these two sources of knowledge ... no others" (A 50/B 74; A 15/B 29, A 835/B 863; A 294/B 350); the division into Aesthetic and Logic corresponds to it. Heidegger sets two sets of Kant passages against each other and states the tension openly. Both sets are in both editions except A 94 and A 115, which follow.

**¶4, 136.** "The transcendental power of imagination is homeless": not treated in the Aesthetic, where it belongs as a faculty of intuition, but in the Logic, where strictly it should not be; since Aesthetic and Logic are oriented on transcendence, their result must lead beyond them. As in §15, the claim that the imagination has no home overlooks that Kant gives its central act a home, the "transcendental doctrine of the power of judgment" (A 136/B 175), and treats its synthesis in the Logic because the Logic is the doctrine of "the rules of the understanding in general" (A 52/B 76) and the imagination's synthesis stands under the unity of apperception (A 118–119; B 151–152). "Homeless" registers that Kant has no separate doctrine of the imagination. It does not register that Kant considered one unnecessary.

**¶5, 136–137.** Did Kant suppress the triad in favour of the duality of stems? No: in the deduction he speaks expressly of "three original sources": "There are, however, three original sources (capacities or faculties of the soul), which contain the conditions of the possibility of all experience, and cannot themselves be derived from any other faculty of the mind, namely sense, imagination, and apperception ... In addition to their empirical use, all of these faculties have a transcendental one" (A 94); and "The possibility of an experience in general and cognition of its objects rest on three subjective sources of cognition: sense, imagination, and apperception" (A 115). Both quotations are exact and both are A-only, as §31 will stress. The first contains a clause the next unit has to answer: the three sources "cannot themselves be derived from any other faculty of the mind". Heidegger quotes it to make the imagination irreducible. The same clause makes sense and apperception irreducible to the imagination, and §§28–29 will "lead back" pure intuition and pure thinking to the imagination.

**¶6–¶7, 137.** Triad and duality stand hard against each other; but does Kant use the image of two "stems" by chance, or to indicate that they grow from a "common root"? The interpretation showed that the imagination is no external bond but "originally unifying", forming the unity of the other two, which have "an essential, structural relation" to it. Kant's answer to the question about the image is in the Introduction: the stems "perhaps" arise from a common root, "unknown to us" (A 15/B 29). Kant chose the image to suggest a root and declined to name it. A 94 rules out the candidate Heidegger is about to name, if "root" means a source from which the stems derive. Heidegger avoids the conflict by refusing derivation in favour of "structural" rootedness in the next division. On the textual record, though, Kant in A names the three sources underivable, and names the root unknown, in the same edition.

**¶8, 137.** "What if this original, formative center was that 'unknown common root' of both stems? Is it an accident that with the first introduction of the power of imagination Kant says that 'we ourselves, however, are seldom conscious [of it] even once'?" (A 78/B 103). A note says that Fichte, Schelling and Jacobi gave the imagination an essential role, and that this interpretation moves "in the opposite direction" from German Idealism. The thesis enters as a question, which is the book's way with its boldest claims. The evidence offered is a clause about consciousness, not about roots: we are "seldom even conscious" of the synthesis. Kant has a text closer to Heidegger's question that Heidegger does not use. In the appendix to the Dialectic, reason's logical maxim bids us test "if imagination combined with consciousness may not be memory, wit, the power to distinguish, or perhaps even understanding and reason", and "the idea of a **fundamental power** – though logic does not at all ascertain whether there is such a thing – is at least the problem set by a systematic representation of the manifoldness of powers" (A 649/B 677). Kant entertains the derivation of understanding and reason from the imagination. He treats it as a regulative problem of reason, an idea of a fundamental power whose existence logic cannot ascertain. Heidegger's next division rejects "fundamental power" by name.

### B. The Transcendental Imagination as the Root of Both Stems

**¶1–¶2, 138.** If the laid ground has the character of a root, it must let the stems grow from it; the ground-laying becomes more original if it unveils how the root is root, which means "nothing less than leading pure intuition and pure thinking back to the transcendental power of imagination"; but does this not reduce all knowledge to mere imagination, the human essence to illusion? The programme is announced plainly, and it is the book's central extension of Kant: a ground Kant called unknown is to be unveiled as root. The worry Heidegger raises against himself is Kant's worry too. In the B preface Kant fears that the transcendental ideality of space and time might be taken for illusion (B 69–70) and that his idealism might be taken for Berkeley's (B 274–275). Kant's answer to the worry is the distinction between appearance and illusion, a different safeguard from the one Heidegger now gives.

**¶3, 138–139.** Leading the two back does not mean they are products of imagination and so "merely imaginary"; it means their structure "is rooted in the structure of the transcendental power of imagination", which can "imagine" only in structural unity with them; what the transcendental imagination forms is not something at hand, so it cannot be "mere imagination"; the horizon it forms, "the understanding of Being", first makes possible the distinction of ontic truth and ontic semblance. The clarification removes the crude reading and keeps the thesis. The claim that the horizon makes the distinction of truth and illusion possible has a Kantian analogue: for Kant, without the rule-governed connection the categories supply, perceptions would be "nothing but a blind play of representations, i.e., less than a dream" (A 112), and an objective succession would otherwise be "a mere dream" (A 202/B 247). "The understanding of Being" names the imagination's product in the vocabulary of *Being and Time*, and with it Kant's imagination becomes the faculty of the understanding of being.

**¶4, 139.** Does ontological knowledge, as finite, not have "a corresponding untruth"? "The idea of transcendental untruth conceals one of the foremost problems of finitude", not yet even posed. Kant has a doctrine that comes close and is not cited: transcendental illusion, "a natural and unavoidable illusion" (A 298/B 354; A 422/B 450), which arises from reason's own principles and "can be rendered harmless but never destroyed". Kant's transcendental illusion is an untruth native to pure reason, the subject of the whole Dialectic. Heidegger's "transcendental untruth" belongs to the finitude of the understanding of being and is not the same. His claim that the problem is "not even posed" stands only if Kant's Dialectic is set aside, as §25 set it aside.

**¶5, 139–140.** Nor may the imagination be thought as a "basic power" (*Grundkraft*) in the soul: "Nothing lies further from this going-back ... than the monistic-empirical explanation of the remaining faculties of the soul based on the power of imagination"; what "soul" and "mind" can mean is first decided by the unveiling of transcendence. Kant's appendix to the Dialectic is the place where such a monistic explanation appears in the *Critique*, and Kant classifies it. The search for a fundamental power, testing whether the imagination might be "perhaps even understanding and reason", is a logical maxim of reason. Its object, the idea of a fundamental power, is "at least the problem" of a systematic representation, not a cognition (A 649–650/B 677–678). Kant and Heidegger agree in refusing a dogmatic fundamental power, and they refuse it for different reasons. Kant refuses because the idea is regulative. Heidegger refuses because "soul" is not yet an ontologically clarified concept. The difference matters: on Kant's account Heidegger's root would be an idea of reason, regulative for the investigation and not cognized.

**¶6, 140.** The regress moves into the dimension of "possibilities"; what is known as the transcendental imagination may dissolve into "more original 'possibilities'", so that "the designation 'power of imagination' itself becomes inappropriate". This is the most candid sentence in the Section about the relation to Kant. The root is named with Kant's word and the word is expected to fail. The imagination's role as root is meant to lead past Kant's faculty to temporality (§§32–34), and Kant's term is a ladder.

**¶7–¶8, 140–141.** The strangeness of the ground cannot vanish but grows, since man's metaphysical nature is "the most unknown and the most real"; if the imagination is the root, the problem of the deduction and the Schematism becomes transparent, since only a unifying that lets the unified spring forth can be an original unity; the interpretation keeps to the course of the ground-laying and goes only as far as Kant's ground-laying contains indications. Kant's "strange" (A 123) returns, now enlarged into the strangeness of man's metaphysical nature. The final promise, to go only as far as Kant's text gives indications, is the standard by which §§28–30 should be read.

#### §28. The Transcendental Power of Imagination and Pure Intuition

**¶1, 141.** Kant calls space and time "original representations"; "original" does not mean innate but the way they represent; "The expression 'original' corresponds to the '*originarius*' in the title *intuitus originarius* and means: letting spring forth" (working rendering); as finite, pure intuitions cannot let a being spring forth. Here the equation of §9 becomes explicit, and Kant's text contradicts it in so many words. At B 72 Kant calls our intuition, pure intuition included, "derived (*intuitus derivativus*), not original (*intuitius originarius*)". Kant's "original representation" of time (A 32/B 48) and of space (B 40) means a representation not obtained by limitation of a prior one. Heidegger says the word "corresponds" to the *originarius* that Kant denies to our intuition. The restriction that pure intuition cannot let a being spring forth keeps the creative sense at arm's length. The equation remains a re-inscription against Kant's explicit distinction.

**¶2, 141–142.** Pure intuitions are nevertheless formative: they pre-present the look of space and time as manifold wholes; their taking-in is "the formative self-giving of what gives itself"; they are *exhibitio originaria*, and "In this presenting, however, lies the essence of the pure power of imagination. Pure intuition can only be 'original' because it itself is, according to its essence, pure imagination which, forming looks (images), gives from out of itself" (working rendering). *Exhibitio originaria* is the *Anthropology*'s term for the productive imagination's free invention (§26). Heidegger now applies it to the pure intuitions of the Aesthetic. The step from "space and time are original representations" to "pure intuition is pure imagination" goes through the word "original" alone, in the joined sense of ¶1. Kant's Aesthetic says that space and time "lie ready" in the mind as forms (A 20/B 34) and does not ascribe their representation to the imagination.

**¶3, 142.** What does pure intuition intuit? Interpreters deny it intuits anything, since it is only "form of intuition"; but it intuits a unified, non-empty whole whose parts are limitations; this unity must be seen in advance, which is why Kant speaks not of synthesis but of "Synopsis" (A 94 f., where Kant says he treated the transcendental synopsis in the Aesthetic); the unity is not that of a concept and cannot spring from "the synthesis of the understanding"; it is seen in advance in image-giving imagination, the origin of everything "synthetic" (A 78/B 103). A footnote adds that the difference between "form of intuition" and "formal intuition" that Kant introduces at B 160 n. can be clarified only by distinguishing the synopsis of pure intuition from the synthesis of the understanding. The A 94 reference is accurate: Kant says of the transcendental use of sense, "We have discussed this **with regard to the senses** in the first part above". The B 160 note says the opposite of what the paragraph claims, and the footnote does not report it. Kant writes there that space represented as object "contains more than the mere form of intuition, namely the comprehension of the manifold given in accordance with the form of sensibility in an **intuitive** representation, so that the **form of intuition** merely gives the manifold, but the **formal intuition** gives unity of the representation. In the Aesthetic I ascribed this unity merely to sensibility, only in order to note that it precedes all concepts, though to be sure it presupposes a synthesis, which does not belong to the senses but through which all concepts of space and time first become possible. For since through it (as the understanding determines the sensibility) space or time are first **given** as intuitions, the unity of this *a priori* intuition belongs to space and time, and not to the concept of the understanding" (B 160–161 n.). Kant grants the unity is intuitive and not conceptual, as Heidegger says. He then derives it from a synthesis "as the understanding determines the sensibility". Heidegger's "cannot spring from the synthesis of the understanding" contradicts the note he cites in his footnote. The note is B-only, and it is the text on which the Marburg reading he rejects in ¶11 relied.

**¶4–¶5, 142–143.** Kant says in a note: "Space and time are the forms of pre-formation [*Vorbildung*] in intuition" (Reflexion 5934; Adickes reads "connection", *Verbindung*, "wrongly in my opinion"); so what is pre-formed in pure intuition is imaginative, which is not strange if pure intuition and pure imagination are at issue, since what is formed in imagination is not necessarily ontic illusion. The Reflexion is outside the corpus. Heidegger reports, honestly, that the editor of the Akademie *Nachlass* reads the word otherwise. On Adickes's reading the note says space and time are forms of connection in intuition, which is the B 160 n. doctrine. The most direct textual support for "pre-formation" rests on a disputed transcription that Heidegger himself flags.

**¶6, 143.** Kant "must have seen little of the essential structure of pure intuition ... had not the imaginative character of what is intuited in it become visible to him": "The mere form of intuition, without substance, is in itself no object, but is rather the merely formal condition of the same (as appearance), as pure space and pure time. ... these are indeed Something, but they are not themselves objects which can be intuited (*ens imaginarium*)" (A 291/B 347); "Hence on the grounds of its essence, pure intuiting is pure imagination." The quotation is exact. Its weight rests on one Latin word. Kant's *ens imaginarium* is the third entry in his scholastic table of the concept of nothing, beside *ens rationis*, *nihil privativum* and *nihil negativum*. It names a status, "empty intuition without object", the mere form as "empty *data* for concepts" (A 291–292/B 347–349). In the school vocabulary "imaginary" means not real. It does not ascribe the form to an act of the imagination. Heidegger's "Hence" turns the classificatory adjective into a claim about the faculty. That is a re-inscription by etymology, and it is the only direct textual evidence the section gives that Kant saw the "imaginative character" of pure intuition.

**¶7, 143–144.** The *ens imaginarium* belongs to the forms of "Nothing", what is not a being at hand; pure space and time are "Something", not "objects"; to say "nothing" is intuited is negative and ambiguous until one sees that "object" here means the being that shows itself in appearance; pure intuitions are "intuitions without things" (Reflexion 5315, not in the corpus), and space is "the representation of a mere possibility of coexistence" (A 374). The reading of Kant's table is accurate: pure space and time are, in Kant's words, "to be sure something" and "not in themselves objects that are intuited" (A 291/B 347). The A 374 quotation is from the first edition's fourth paralogism, a passage cut in B, and it serves Kant there to show that space is "nothing other than a mere representation" against transcendental realism. Heidegger uses it for the non-objective givenness of pure intuition. The use is compatible with Kant's purpose.

**¶8–¶9, 144–145.** The tendency to deny pure intuition an intuited is strengthened by a genuine phenomenon: in dealing with things we are directed at them, not at space and time; but they are there "in the way of an originally forming giving"; the interpretation of pure intuition as pure imagination lets the intuited be clarified positively, as "a preliminary forming of a pure, unthematic, and in the Kantian sense nonobjective look", so that empirical intuition need not intuit space and time. The phenomenology is Heidegger's and is persuasive in its own terms. Kant's account of the same phenomenon is that space and time are forms in which objects are given, and that they can be "represented as object", as in geometry, only through a synthesis that yields formal intuition (B 160 n.). Kant's unthematic form and thematic formal intuition correspond to Heidegger's unthematic look and to nothing on Heidegger's side. Heidegger's "forming giving" fills the place Kant left for the form of intuition with an activity Kant assigns to the synthesis under the understanding.

**¶10, 145.** With this interpretation "the transcendental character of transcendental intuition is first clarified"; "standing as it does at the beginning of the *Critique of Pure Reason*, the Transcendental Aesthetic is fundamentally unintelligible. It has only a preparatory character and can be read properly for the first time [only] from the perspective of the Transcendental Schematism." This is contestation of Kant's architectonic, stated without hedging. Kant's reason for placing the Aesthetic first is in the Introduction: the conditions under which objects are given "precede those under which those objects are thought" (A 15–16/B 30). The B-Deduction's note at B 160–161, which says the Aesthetic ascribed the unity of space and time to sensibility "only in order to note that it precedes all concepts", concedes that the Aesthetic's account is provisional. To that extent Kant agrees with Heidegger that the Aesthetic is not the last word. Kant completes it in the B-Deduction by the understanding's synthesis. Heidegger completes it from the Schematism by the imagination.

**¶11, 145–146.** The Marburg attempt to take space and time as "categories" in the logical sense and dissolve the Aesthetic into the Logic is untenable, yet its motive is genuine: the Aesthetic alone cannot be the whole; but the "syn"-character of pure intuition leads not to the understanding's synthesis but to the origin of pure intuition in the imagination; and the dissolution becomes still more questionable if pure thinking too is rooted in the imagination. Heidegger and the Marburg school read the same textual problem, the unity of pure intuition, and give it opposite answers. The Kantian text both parties need is B 160–161 n., and on its wording the Marburg answer is closer: Kant derives the unity "as the understanding determines the sensibility". Heidegger's answer has A 94's "synopsis through sense" and A 78's "synthesis in general ... the mere effect of the imagination". The dispute is between Kant's A and Kant's B, and §31 will decide it for A by argument rather than text.

#### §29. The Transcendental Power of Imagination and Theoretical Reason

**¶1–¶2, 146.** To derive pure thinking from the imagination seems absurd, since Kant says the imagination is "always sensible" (A 124): how can a lower faculty be the origin of the higher? But no empirical derivation of a higher from a lower faculty is at issue, and the rank of "lower" and "higher" cannot guide; what does "sensible" mean? Kant's sentence at A 124 is stronger than the clause Heidegger quotes, and it is aimed at exactly his thesis: "It is this apperception that must be added to the pure imagination in order to make its function intellectual. For in itself the synthesis of the imagination, although exercised *a priori,* is nevertheless always sensible, for it combines the manifold only as it **appears** in intuition, e.g., the shape of a triangle" (A 124). Kant does not rank the faculties as lower and higher here. He says that the imagination's synthesis becomes intellectual only by the addition of apperception. The objection Heidegger dissolves, a rank of lower and higher faculties, is not the one A 124 makes. Kant's objection is that the imagination's synthesis, without apperception, combines only appearances.

**¶3, 147.** Sensibility means finite intuition, taking in what gives itself (§5); not every sensible intuition is empirical; the "lower" bodily affection is not essential; so the transcendental imagination as pure finite intuition "must be 'sensible'"; this cannot assign it to the lower faculties, and the weightiest objection falls. The reply rests on §5's definition, which is sound as a reading of A 19/B 33 and B 146–147. It answers a rank objection and leaves A 124's objection, that the imagination's synthesis is not intellectual by itself, where it was.

**¶4–¶6, 147–148.** But thinking is sharply distinguished from intuition; yet both are kinds of representing (the genus of A 320/B 376); the understanding's essence is its dependence on intuition, and this "Being" is "in the pure synthesis of the pure power of imagination"; one might reply that the understanding is related to intuition "through" the imagination without being it, and that logic, which need not treat the imagination, attests its independence, and Kant always introduces the understanding as logic determines it. Heidegger states the objection in Kant's favour and concedes its textual basis: Kant introduces the understanding "in the shape" given it by logic. The claim that the understanding's being is "in" the imagination's synthesis is the step to be argued, and the next paragraphs argue it from Kant's B text.

**¶7, 148–149.** Traditional logic does not treat the imagination; whether logic "need not" treat it is open; does Kant's treatment of pure thinking in the deduction and Schematism not show that the functions of judgment and the notions are "artificially isolated elements of the pure synthesis", itself a "presupposition" of "the synthetic unity of apperception"? Does Kant not dissolve formal logic into transcendental logic, whose central theme is the imagination, so that he must say, "characteristically only in the second edition": "And thus the synthetic unity of apperception is the highest point to which one must affix all use of the understanding, even the whole of logic and, after it, transcendental philosophy; indeed this faculty is the understanding itself" (B 133–134 n.)? The quotation is exact, and it says the opposite of the inference drawn from it. Kant's note makes the synthetic unity of apperception the highest point of logic and transcendental philosophy and identifies that faculty with "the understanding itself". It does not mention the imagination. Heidegger takes a B text in which the understanding is made the summit of all logic as evidence that Kant dissolved formal logic into a transcendental logic whose theme is the imagination. "Characteristically only in the second edition" gives the B note the role of a symptom, but what it states is the primacy of apperception, which A already asserted (A 117 n., A 118). The "presupposition" between quotation marks is A 118's "presupposes a synthesis", examined in §17.

**¶8–¶9, 149.** Preconceptions about the independence of thinking drawn from formal logic must not decide; the essence of pure thinking must be sought in what the ground-laying showed; thinking as judging is a "remoter" determination, the "faculty of rules" "comes nearer" (A 126), because it leads to the understanding as "pure apperception". A 126 is quoted as in §16. Kant's sentence at A 126 adds that the explanations of the understanding "come down to the same thing", and Kant does not rank judging as remoter. That ranking is Heidegger's, with the A 126 comparative "more fruitful" as its support.

**¶10, 150.** "Faculty of rules" means holding before oneself the unities that guide all unification, their affinity encompassed by an enduring unity; representing this unity is the "basic feature of letting-stand-against"; in this turning-toward the "self" is "taken out" into the turning, and the "I" of this "self" is manifest; so the "I represent" "accompanies" all representing, not as a separate act of knowing but as going "along"; the essence of pure thinking and of the I lies in "pure self-consciousness", whose "consciousness" can be clarified only from the being of the self. "Accompanies" is Kant's word from the B-Deduction: "The **I think** must **be able** to accompany all my representations" (B 131), a B-only sentence, used here without the B label. The thesis that the consciousness of the self is to be clarified "from the being of the self, not the reverse" reverses the order of the Paralogisms. There Kant warns against inferring the being of the soul from the "I think", which is "the mere form of consciousness" (A 382; B 406–411). Heidegger's "being of the self" is fundamental ontology, and it begins where Kant's critique of rational psychology ends.

**¶11, 150.** The "I think" is always "I think substance", "I think causality"; the I is the "vehicle" of the categories (A 343/B 401). Kant: "this concept is the vehicle of all concepts whatever, and hence also of transcendental concepts, and is thus always comprehended among them" (A 341/B 399). Kant's point is that the "I think" belongs among the transcendental concepts without a title of its own. Heidegger reads "vehicle" as the I's carrying the categories to where they unify. The image fits Kant's word.

**¶12, 150–151.** The pure understanding is a forming spontaneity "whose occurrence lies in the 'transcendental Schematism'", which Kant calls "the procedure of the understanding with these schemata" (A 140/B 179) and "the schematism of our understanding" (A 141/B 180); but the schemata are "a transcendental product of the power of imagination" (A 142/B 181); how to reconcile this? "The understanding does not produce the schemata, but rather 'proceeds with them'"; this procedure, grounded in the imagination, makes up "the original being-understanding [*Verstandsein*]"; "The apparently independent achievement of pure understanding in the thinking of unities is, as spontaneously forming representing, a pure basic act of the transcendental power of imagination."
