Here Heidegger quotes the two phrases the Fourth Stage omitted and argues with them, which deserves acknowledgment. The three quotations are exact and the tension he states is Kant's: the schemata are the imagination's products and the schematism is the understanding's procedure. Kant's own resolution, in A and B, is the identity of function: the understanding is "the unity of apperception in relation to the synthesis of the imagination" (A 119), and "It is one and the same spontaneity that, there under the name of imagination and here under the name of understanding, brings combination into the manifold of intuition" (B 162 n.). Heidegger resolves it by subordinating one side: the understanding's procedure is an act of the imagination. Kant's texts allow the identity and do not decide which name is primary. In the Typic of the *Critique of Practical Reason* Kant decides it for practical reason, as the §30 units note.

**¶13, 151.** When Kant calls this turning-toward "our thought" (A 104), "thinking" means not judging but "the freely forming and projecting, although not arbitrary 'thinking-for-oneself' [*Sichdenken*] of something. This original 'thinking' is pure imagining." Kant's "our thought of the relation of all cognition to its object" (A 104) is ordinary usage, and the gloss is Heidegger's. The same paragraph of Kant's text defines the unity this thought requires as "the formal unity of consciousness in the synthesis of the manifold of representations" (A 105). The identification of thinking with imagining is the section's thesis restated, not a reading of A 104.

**¶14, 151–152.** The pure understanding is a "closed unity"; the whole of affinity gets its wholeness from a representing, the forming of the idea; so the understanding must be a "faculty of ideas", reason, for without reason we have "no coherent use of the understanding" (A 651/B 679); ideas "contain a certain completeness" (A 567–568/B 595–596) and represent "the form of a whole" (A 832/B 860), more originally rule-giving. The three quotations are exact, and all come from the Dialectic and the Doctrine of Method, where reason's ideas are regulative. Kant's sentence at A 651/B 679 continues: "and, lacking that, no sufficient mark of empirical truth; thus in regard to the latter we simply have to presuppose the systematic unity of nature as objectively valid and necessary". The ideas' role is to guide the understanding's use toward systematic unity. Heidegger makes the idea the source of the understanding's own wholeness. Kant's understanding has its own unity, the closed table of categories (A 64–65/B 89–90), and reason's ideas give the understanding's cognitions their systematic unity, not their categorial completeness.

**¶15, 152.** Against this, Kant says of the transcendental ideal, which "must serve as a rule and archetype", that it is "entirely different ... with the creatures of the imagination" of which "painters and physiognomists" claim to have images (A 570/B 598); but this says only that the ideal rests on "determinate concepts" and is not a "wavering sketch" of the empirical imagination, and does not exclude that those concepts are possible "only in the transcendental imagination". Kant's passage says more than Heidegger grants, and in Kant's own vocabulary of the imagination. The creatures of imagination are "as it were, **monograms**, individual traits, though not determined through any assignable rule, constituting more a wavering sketch ... than a determinate image", which "can, though only improperly, be called ideals of sensibility" (A 570/B 598). Kant's "monogram", the word the Schematism used for the pure imagination's schemata of sensible concepts (A 141–142/B 181), here names the imagination's creatures in contrast to the ideal of reason. Kant separates reason's ideal from the imagination's products by their relation to rules. Heidegger's reply, that the determinate concepts might be possible only in the transcendental imagination, is an extrapolation without textual support, and it is offered as one.

**¶16–¶17, 152–154.** One might grant the free forming in pure thinking but deny full coincidence with the imagination, since the imagination is also receptivity, the original unity of receptivity and spontaneity; pure intuition was shown to be spontaneous; so pure thinking would have to show receptivity; Kant identifies understanding with spontaneity, but that excludes receptivity no more than identifying sensibility with receptivity excluded spontaneity; the exclusive characterizations are owed to empirical cognition; in pure knowledge the "self-giving taking-in" cannot stay hidden. The symmetry argument is Heidegger's. Kant's definitions are not owed to a view of empirical cognition. They are stated for both: "intuition can never be other than **sensible**", and the understanding is "the faculty for bringing forth representations itself, or the **spontaneity** of cognition" (A 51/B 75). In B Kant adds of our understanding that it "is not itself a faculty of intuitions" (B 153). Heidegger's receptive understanding has a basis in A 104 (the object's necessity "against" arbitrariness) and none in Kant's definitions.

**¶18, 154.** The unity of apperception is "against all haphazardness", so in the turning-toward this "Being-in-opposition" is received; "The freely forming projecting of affinity is in itself a representing, taking-in-stride submitting to it"; rules are represented as binding; so pure thinking is "in itself ... taking-in-stride, i.e., pure intuition"; this receptive spontaneity must spring from the imagination. The conclusion, that pure thinking is pure intuition, contradicts Kant's B 135 and B 153 outright: "ours can only **think** and must seek the intuition in the senses" (B 135); the understanding "is not itself a faculty of intuitions" (B 153). The step from self-binding to receptivity is Heidegger's. It has a practical analogue in Kant's autonomy, where the will is subject to a law it gives itself, and §30 will draw on it. In Kant's theoretical philosophy the understanding's legislation is not described as a self-subjection that receives (A 126–127).

**¶19, 154–155.** The understanding has "the 'ground for its possibility'" in a "faculty" that "looks out into an infinity of self-made representations and concepts" (essay on the progress of metaphysics, not in the corpus); the imagination projects the whole of possibilities into which it "looks out", forming the horizon within which the knowing self acts; hence Kant can say, "Human reason is by nature architectonic, i.e., it considers all cognitions as belonging to a possible system" (A 474/B 502). The A 474/B 502 quotation is exact, and in Kant it introduces reason's "interest" in the thesis side of the antinomies. Kant's claim is about reason's systematic interest, not about the imagination. "Only for this reason can Kant say" makes Kant's sentence depend on the imagination thesis, which is Heidegger's construction. The *Fortschritte* phrase cannot be checked here. On Heidegger's reading it names the imagination, and that identification is his too.

**¶20, 155.** The intuition-character of pure thinking is less strange, since pure space and time are as "unintuitable" as the properly understood categories, if "intuitable" means perceivable by a sense organ. The point echoes Kant's *ens imaginarium*: pure space and time are not objects intuited (A 291/B 347). Heidegger's symmetry, categories and pure intuitions equally unintuitable, holds only for "intuitable" in the sensory sense. Kant's distinction between them is not sensory. Space and time are intuitions, singular and immediate (A 24–25/B 39; A 31–32/B 47), and the categories are concepts, general and mediate.

**¶21, 155.** The necessity in the horizon's standing-against is possible only as meeting "a being-free for it"; "Freedom already lies in the essence of pure understanding, i.e., of pure theoretical reason, insofar as this means placing oneself under a self-given necessity"; understanding and reason are free not because spontaneous but because their spontaneity is "a receptive spontaneity, i.e., transcendental power of imagination". The definition of freedom as placing oneself under a self-given necessity is Kant's definition of autonomy of the will, transferred to theoretical reason. Kant does call the understanding's act spontaneity and the "I think" an act of spontaneity (B 132), and he links theoretical spontaneity to our existence as "an intelligence that is merely conscious of its faculty for combination" (B 158). He does not call theoretical reason free in the sense of autonomy, and in the Dialectic he denies that theoretical reason can cognize freedom at all (B xxviii–xxix; A 557/B 585). The thesis of *Vom Wesen des Grundes*, freedom as ground of grounds, is here attributed to Kant's understanding.

**¶22, 155.** In reducing pure intuition and pure thinking to the imagination, the imagination reveals itself as structural possibility, transcendence as the essence of the finite self; it loses the character of an empirical faculty and the limitation to the theoretical; the last step must be taken. A transition. The "reduction" here means rooting, as B ¶3 defined it, and the root has now become "transcendence as the essence of the finite self", which is no longer a Kantian faculty at all, as B ¶6 foresaw.

#### §30. The Transcendental Power of Imagination and Practical Reason

**¶1, 156.** "Everything is practical that is possible through freedom" (A 800/B 828); since freedom belongs to theoretical reason, it is in itself practical; and if finite reason as receptive spontaneity springs from the imagination, practical reason too is grounded in it; this must be shown from the essence of the "practical self". The quotation is exact. Its context is the Canon, and Kant's next sentence restricts it: "But if the conditions for the exercise of our free choice are empirical, then in that case reason can have none but a regulative use". Kant's "practical" concerns free choice and its laws. The inference "theoretical reason is in itself practical" rests on §29's transfer of autonomy to the understanding, not on Kant's sentence, which is about what is possible through freedom of choice.

**¶2, 156.** The self lies in "self-consciousness", which must be determined from the being of the self; the moral self-consciousness that makes the practical self a self must be examined and traced to the imagination. The procedure again begins from the being of the self, the order reversed against the Paralogisms (§29 ¶10).

**¶3, 156–157.** The moral I, the person: personality is "the idea of the moral law" "with the respect inseparable from it" (*Religion*, not in the corpus); respect is "receptivity" for the moral law; a "feeling", yet a pure, "self-produced" feeling (*Groundwork*, not in the corpus); so the essence of feeling must be clarified. The *Religion* and the *Groundwork* are outside the corpus. The *Critique of Practical Reason* supports the claim that respect is a pure feeling: "respect for the moral law is a feeling which is produced by an intellectual cause, and this feeling is the only one that we know quite a priori and the necessity of which we can perceive" (CPrR, chapter "Of the Motives of Pure Practical Reason"). Kant's "produced by an intellectual cause" makes respect an effect of the law on feeling. Heidegger's "receptivity" for the law makes it the openness through which the law is received. The two descriptions differ in direction, as the next units show.

**¶4, 157.** Every feeling has a structure: a "having-a-feeling for ..." that is at once a "feeling-oneself" of the one who feels; even pleasure is pleasure in something and being pleased. The analysis is Heidegger's phenomenology of feeling and mood (*Being and Time* §29). Kant's *Critique of Practical Reason* distinguishes pathological feeling, "founded on the susceptibility of the subject", from the one feeling produced by reason, and offers no general structure of feeling of this kind.

**¶5–¶6, 157–158.** Kant analyses respect in the *Critique of Practical Reason*; respect is respect for the law; it does not judge actions after the fact but "first constitutes the possibility for action. The respect for ... is the way in which the law first becomes accessible to us"; it does not serve "for the 'grounding' of the law"; the respecting feeling is the way the law can come to meet us; in respect the respecting I is manifest to itself; respect for the law is respect for oneself as the self not determined by self-conceit; "respect always applies only to persons, never to things". Several claims have Kant's text behind them. Kant writes that "the respect for the law is not a motive to morality, but is morality itself subjectively considered as a motive", and that "Respect applies always to persons only — not to things" (CPrR, same chapter). Respect strikes down "self-conceit" and so is a positive feeling "known a priori". Heidegger's formula, "the way in which the law first becomes accessible to us", re-describes Kant's order. In Kant the law determines the will directly, and respect is the effect of that determination on a sensible being: "as respect is an effect on feeling, and therefore on the sensibility, of a rational being, it presupposes this sensibility". Heidegger makes respect the disclosure of the law. Kant makes it the law's effect on feeling. The same Kantian sentence gives Heidegger his strongest support, and he does not cite it: respect "presupposes this sensibility, and therefore also the finiteness of such beings on whom the moral law imposes respect; and ... respect for the law cannot be attributed to a supreme being". Kant himself ties respect to finitude.

**¶7–¶8, 158–159.** In respect I submit to the law, which reason as free gives itself; "In respect before the law, I submit to myself"; submitting I raise myself to myself as a free, self-determining being; respect reveals my "dignity"; it is the way of the self's "not throwing away the hero in its soul", "the way of being-responsible of the self to itself, the authentic being-a-self"; "The submitting self-projecting onto the whole basic possibility of authentic existing, which the law gives, is the essence of acting being-a-self, i.e., of practical reason." The analysis of submission and elevation is Kant's: the law "humiliates" self-conceit, and "the lowering of the pretensions of moral self-esteem, that is, humiliation on the sensible side, is an elevation of the moral, i.e., practical, esteem for the law itself on the intellectual side" (CPrR, chapter on motives). Kant's elevation is of esteem for the law. Heidegger's is the self's elevation to itself. The last sentence is a re-inscription in the vocabulary of *Being and Time* (self-projection, authentic existence, being-responsible), and the definition of practical reason as "the submitting self-projecting onto ... authentic existing" is not Kant's.

**¶9, 159.** The concept of feeling as an empirical faculty has disappeared and been replaced by "a transcendental, basic structure of the transcendence of the moral self"; so "feeling" must be understood ontologically if Kant's "moral feeling" and "feeling of my existence" are to be exhausted. "Moral feeling" is Kant's term (CPrR: respect for duty "is the only true moral feeling"). "Feeling of my existence" is not located in the corpus. Kant insists that the moral feeling is "produced by an intellectual cause" and is not the ground of the law (CPrR), which agrees with Heidegger's refusal of an empirical concept of feeling.

**¶10, 159–160.** No further step is needed to see that respect lets "the original constitution of the transcendental power of imagination" stand out: "The submitting, immediate surrender to ... is pure receptivity, the free self-giving of the law, however, is pure spontaneity; both are originally unified in themselves"; and only this origin of practical reason in the imagination explains why, in respect, law and acting self are manifest non-objectively. The section's conclusion meets a Kantian text it does not cite, in the *Critique of Practical Reason* itself. In the Typic of pure practical judgment Kant writes: "the moral law has no faculty but the understanding to aid its application to physical objects (not the imagination)". In German: *kein anderes ... vermittelndes Erkenntnisvermögen, als den Verstand (nicht die Einbildungskraft)*. Kant excludes the imagination from mediating practical reason by name, and gives the understanding's form of a law of nature, not a schema of sensibility, as the "type". Heidegger's claim that "no further step is needed" passes over the one text in which Kant decides the question. The unity of receptivity and spontaneity in respect is a structure Heidegger rightly finds in Kant's analysis of respect. Its attribution to the imagination is Heidegger's, against the Typic.

#### §31. The Originality of the Laid Ground and Kant's Shrinking-Back from the Transcendental Power of Imagination

**¶1, 160.** The highest principle delimited transcendence, the imagination proved its essential ground, and the principle therefore "speaks of the essential constitution of the human creature in general, insofar as it is determined as finite pure reason". Kant's principle speaks of "the conditions of the **possibility of experience**" and of "every object" (A 158/B 197). The human essence enters through Heidegger's identification of possibility of experience with transcendence (§24).

**¶2, 160.** This constitution, rooted in the imagination, is "the 'unknown'" into which Kant "must have looked" when he spoke of "the root unknown to us"; the unknown is "not that of which we know absolutely nothing, but rather what pushes against us as the disquieting element in what is known"; Kant did not carry out the more original interpretation; "Kant shrank back from this unknown root." A marginal note adds: this holds only for one who grants that Kant went *toward* the imagination, since only then is there a "back", and the *Critique of Judgment* §59 confirms the interpretation and the shrinking-back. Kant's "unknown to us" (A 15/B 29) is reinterpreted as the disquieting known. On Kant's usage, "unknown" means not cognized (as with the thing in itself, "uncognized by us", B xx). The marginal note is later than the text and concedes the hypothetical structure of the thesis: shrinking back presupposes an approach, and the approach is the book's reading of A. *Being and Time* had said the same of the schematism (SZ 23). The Kantbook relocates the shrinking-back from the schematism to the imagination and from a gesture to an event between editions.

**¶3, 161.** "In the second edition ... the transcendental power of imagination as it came to light in the impassioned course of its first projection is thrust aside and given a new interpretation — one favoring the understanding" (working rendering); all that was its grounding achievement had to be kept, or the ground-laying would collapse; the return in the *Critique of Judgment* is not discussed. Kant's own account of the second edition contradicts the claim of reinterpretation. In the B preface he says that he found "nothing to alter either in the propositions themselves or in their grounds of proof, or in the form and completeness of the book's plan", and that his changes concern "the mode of presentation", made to remove "difficulties and obscurities" (B xxxvii–xxxviii). Heidegger's thesis requires that Kant's account of his own revision be mistaken or disingenuous. The paragraph's second sentence, that the imagination's achievement had to be kept, explains in advance why B still names the transcendental synthesis of the imagination (B 151–152).

**¶4, 161.** In B Kant struck "the two main passages" naming the imagination as a third basic faculty: the first (A 94) is replaced by a critique of Locke's and Hume's analysis of the understanding, "as if Kant saw his own procedure in the first edition—although unjustly—still close to empiricism"; the second (A 115) falls with the rewriting of the deduction. The textual facts are right: Guyer and Wood note that the A 94–95 paragraph "is omitted in the second and replaced by three that here follow it" (B 127–129, on Locke and Hume), and A 115 belongs to the A text replaced by §§15–27. The interpretation of the replacement is a conjecture about motive, and Heidegger marks it as one ("as if").

**¶5, 161–162.** Even where Kant first introduces the imagination as "an indispensable function of the soul" (A 78/B 103) he later changed it, "certainly only in his personal copy", to "function of the understanding" (Erdmann's *Nachträge* XLI); "With that, pure synthesis is assigned to the pure understanding", and the imagination becomes dispensable, although the Schematism chapter, "which also remained unchanged in the second edition", shows it to be the ground. The note in Kant's copy is real and recorded by Guyer and Wood. Two facts qualify its use. The second edition itself printed the sentence unchanged: B 103 reads, like A 78, *einer blinden, obgleich unentbehrlichen Funktion der Seele*. The correction is a marginal note in Kant's copy of the first edition, of unknown date, and it did not enter the edition Heidegger reads as the retreat. And the correction's sense is disputed by Heidegger's own later note: "the understanding, however, conceived 'transcendentally'" (marginal note *b*). Heidegger concedes that the Schematism, his central text, stands unchanged in B. On his own account the retreat left the chapter he calls "the central core of the whole voluminous work" untouched.

**¶6, 162–163.** The imagination showed itself in the deduction already, so if it was to be removed as basic faculty, the deduction had to be wholly rewritten; "The transcendental power of imagination is the disquieting unknown which became the motive for the new version of the Transcendental Deduction"; a later marginal note adds that with its exclusion sensibility and understanding are more sharply distinguished, logos gains its right, certainty becomes the basic trait of metaphysics, and the two "lie there like two blocks". The motive asserted is not documented in Kant's text. Kant's stated motive for rewriting the deduction is obscurity of presentation (B xxxvii–xxxviii), and his B preface devotes its new pages to the refutation of idealism (B xxxix–xli n.), a response to the charge, in the Göttingen review, that the *Critique* was Berkeley's idealism. Heidegger does not mention that charge. It is the best-documented external motive for the B changes, and it bears on the deduction's emphasis on objective validity and the understanding. The marginal note belongs to the 1930s Heidegger and his history of certainty.

**¶7–¶8, 163–164.** The change marks Kant's new position: the imagination is now only a "function" of the understanding; in A all synthesis sprang from the imagination, irreducible to sensibility or understanding; in B the understanding alone is the origin of all synthesis: synthesis "is an act of the spontaneity of the power of representation", which "one must call understanding" (B 130; note the "indifferent expression" *Vorstellungskraft*); synthesis is "an action of the understanding" (B 130); "the faculty of combining *a priori*" is the understanding (B 135); "pure synthesis of the understanding" (B 140, 153); "the transcendental synthesis of the imagination" is "an effect of the understanding on sensibility" (B 152) and "synthetic influence of the understanding on the inner sense" (B 154). The quotations are exact, and this is the paragraph where the B text supports Heidegger best. B does make combination an act of the understanding: "since one must call the latter understanding, in distinction from sensibility, all combination ... is an action of the understanding" (B 130). The opposition with A is weaker than the paragraph says. A 78 already gave "to bring this synthesis to concepts" to the understanding. A 119 already defined the understanding as "the unity of apperception in relation to the synthesis of the imagination". A 124 already made apperception what renders the imagination's function "intellectual". The B text shifts the name of the act. In A the synthesis is the imagination's, under apperception. In B it is the understanding's, exercised as imagination on sensibility. The doctrine that the understanding's unity governs the synthesis is in both.

**¶9, 164.** Is the imagination not still there? Yes, but "the transcendental power of imagination is still there in name only": "It is one and the same spontaneity which, there, under the name of the power of imagination, and here, under the name of the understanding, brings combination into the manifold of intuition" (B 162 n.); imagination is now only the name for the synthesis related to intuition, which "in itself" belongs to the understanding (B 151). The B 162 note is quoted exactly and read in one direction. It says one spontaneity has two names. It does not say which name is the real one, and it is the B form of A's own identification at A 119. B 151 does not say that the synthesis "is at bottom understanding". It says the imagination "belongs to sensibility" as a condition and is "spontaneity" as determining sensibility, "an effect of the understanding on sensibility" (B 151–152). Heidegger's "in name only" decides what the note leaves open.

**¶10, 164–165.** The imagination no longer mediates as an independent basic faculty but "falls through" between the two sources; its office is transferred to the understanding; the B term "synthesis speciosa" proves its loss of independence, since it is so called only because the understanding there relates to sensibility, and without that relation it is "synthesis intellectualis". Kant's B 151 distinguishes "figurative" synthesis (*synthesis speciosa*) from the "combination of the understanding" (*synthesis intellectualis*) and calls both "transcendental". The new name marks the imagination's synthesis as the one that relates the categories to our intuition. That is the role Heidegger assigned it in A. That the name "proves" a loss of independence is Heidegger's reading. Its opposite is equally available: B coins a technical term for the imagination's synthesis where A had none.

**¶11, 165–166.** Why did Kant shrink back? Not from failing to see a more original ground-laying: the A preface distinguishes an "objective" and a "subjective" side of the deduction; the objective "belongs essentially to my ends", the subjective, concerning "the pure understanding itself ... and the cognitive powers on which it itself rests", "does not belong essentially to it; because the chief question always remains: 'What and how much can understanding and reason cognize free of all experience?' and not: 'How is the faculty of thinking itself possible?'" (A xvi–xvii). The quotation is exact and stops before Kant's reason for the subordination. Kant goes on: "Since the latter question is something like the search for the cause of a given effect, and is therefore something like a hypothesis ... it appears as if I am taking the liberty in this case of expressing an **opinion**". And: "even in case my subjective deduction does not produce the complete conviction that I expect, the objective deduction that is my primary concern would come into its full strength, on which what is said at pages [A] 92–3 should even be sufficient by itself" (A xvii). Kant subordinates the subjective deduction on grounds of certainty. He did not want the objective proof to rest on an inquiry that might look hypothetical, and he says A 92–93, a passage common to both editions, suffices by itself. Kant's reason is a methodological decision, stated in the first edition, six years before the alleged retreat.

**¶12–¶13, 166–167.** The deduction is "necessarily objective-subjective at the same time"; the subjective side cannot be absent, but its explicit working-out could be deferred, and Kant did so from insight into its nature; the subjective deduction must go back to "the cognitive powers on which the understanding itself rests", not by an empirical, "hypothetical" explanation; Kant already speaks of this "wholly untrodden path" with its "obscurity" (A 98) and of the need to "penetrate deeply into the first grounds of the possibility of our cognition in general". Heidegger reports Kant's deferral as a decision and reads it as insight. Kant's A 97–98 does say the deduction must assess "the transcendental constitution of the subjective sources that comprise the *a priori* foundations for the possibility of experience", that these "direct us toward three subjective sources of cognition, which make possible even the understanding" (A 97–98), and that the obscurity is "initially unavoidable in a path that is thus far entirely unexplored" (A 98). The phrase "which make possible even the understanding" is real support for Heidegger's reading of A, and he does not quote it. It is A-only. Kant's A text places sources beneath the understanding, and the B text drops the sentence.

**¶14–¶15, 167.** Kant knew the possibility of a more original ground-laying, but it was not his immediate aim; that cannot be why he struck the imagination; the imagination itself must have given the occasion; since he did not carry out the subjective deduction, the subjectivity of the subject remained guided by traditional anthropology and psychology, for which imagination was a lower faculty; the insight of the deduction and the Schematism "was not strong enough" to show subjectivity in a new light. The diagnosis is a reconstruction of motive without textual evidence beyond the A/B differences. Kant's own psychology of faculties is not traditional in the way the diagnosis requires. In both editions Kant treats the imagination's synthesis as transcendental and productive (A 118, A 123; B 151–152), not as a lower faculty in the school sense. The claim that tradition reasserted itself is Heidegger's explanation of a change Kant explained otherwise.

**¶16–¶17, 167–168.** How can the lower faculty constitute reason? Must the primacy of logic fall, the architectonic of Aesthetic and Logic collapse, the *Critique of Pure Reason* lose its theme "if pure reason reverts to the transcendental power of imagination"? "Does not this ground-laying lead us to an abyss? In the radicalism of his questions, Kant brought the 'possibility' of metaphysics to this abyss. He saw the unknown. He had to shrink back. It was not just that the transcendental power of imagination frightened him, but rather that in between [the two editions] pure reason as reason drew him increasingly under its spell." The rhetoric is the book's most famous, and the claim it makes is biographical and unverifiable. "He saw the unknown" turns Kant's "unknown to us" (A 15/B 29) into a sight. "Abyss" (*Abgrund*) is the word of *Vom Wesen des Grundes*, where the ground of grounds is freedom as abyss. What can be checked is the sequence of Kant's works. The *Groundwork* (1785) falls between the editions, and the B preface gives the practical use of reason new prominence (B xxv–xxx). The causal connection is Heidegger's.

**¶18, 168–169.** Through the ground-laying Kant first saw the generality of ontological knowledge and could now "critically traverse" moral philosophy, replacing the empirical generality of popular morals with ontological analysis; the sharp division of pure a priori from empirical gained weight; since subjectivity lies in personality, which is moral reason, the rational character of knowledge and action had to harden; "All pure synthesis and synthesis in general must, as spontaneity, fall to the faculty which in the authentic sense is free, the acting reason"; finitude now had to be sought in the pure rational being as such, not in sensibility; so "this personal-ontological problem of a finite pure reason in general could tolerate nothing" reminding of a specific realization, as the imagination, "specifically human" and "sensible", did. The account has a textual hinge in the *Groundwork*'s method, which Kant describes as applying to "all rational beings" and not deriving morality from "the special constitution of human nature" (not in the corpus). In the *Critique*, the B-only passage at B 72 already extends sensible intuition hypothetically to "all finite thinking beings". Heidegger's thesis that the imagination was dropped because it was specifically human has no direct text. Kant's B 151–152 keeps the imagination as the faculty through which the categories apply to "the intuition that is possible for us".

**¶19, 169.** The problem of finite rational being in general versus its human realization comes forward in the B-Deduction; the first "improvement" in B shows it: "at least for us humans" is inserted into the characterization of finite intuition (B 33), to show that finite intuition is receptive but need not be mediated by sense organs as with us. In §5 (26) the same insertion was said to "make it more obvious that in the first edition finite knowledge is the theme from the outset". There it was a clarification of A, here it is a symptom of B's turn from the human to the finite rational being in general. The two readings are compatible only if the insertion both continues A's theme and widens it. Heidegger does not reconcile them. The text supports the second reading better: Kant's insertion restricts the dependence on affection to humans "at least", leaving open other finite beings, as B 72 does.

**¶20–¶21, 169–170.** The obscurity and "strangeness" of the imagination and the bright power of pure reason worked together to veil again the perspective that had opened "for a moment"; this is the core of the old observation that Kant moved from the "psychological" A to the more "logical" B; but A was never psychological nor B logical, both are transcendental, objective and subjective; B decided "for the pure understanding against the pure imagination, in order to save the rule of reason"; in B the subjective deduction is not withdrawn but "sharpened in the direction of the pure understanding as the faculty of synthesis". The correction of the received view is sound. A's deduction is not psychology, as Kant himself insists (A xvi–xvii; A 86–87/B 118–119), and B's deduction has a subjective component in §§24–25 (self-affection, the I's knowledge of itself). The claim that B decided "against" the imagination "to save the rule of reason" is the thesis of the section restated as a motive. The textual change it rests on is the change of name examined in ¶¶7–10.

**¶22, 170.** Since B, by widening the concept of finite rational being beyond the human, poses finitude more comprehensively, should one not hold to B? B is not "better" for being "more logical"; it is, rightly understood, "still more 'psychological'", in the exclusive direction of pure reason. Heidegger states the strongest objection to his choice of A and answers it with a paradox. On Kant's account, B would be the better text by its author's judgment, since it improves the presentation (B xxxviii). Heidegger's answer does not engage that judgment.

**¶23, 170–171.** Why was finitude the problem from the start? Because metaphysics belongs to "human nature"; the specific finitude of human nature is decisive; the apparently external question whether A or B deserves preference is "only the pale reflection" of the decisive question: is the imagination "strong enough" to determine the finite human subject's essence originally, as a whole, or does excluding it make the problem of human pure reason more tractable? While undecided, the interpretation remains incomplete. The section ends by turning the philological question into a systematic one and leaving the systematic one open. On the philological question, the units of this section and the Third Stage show the evidence for A's priority and for B's retreat to be thinner than the rhetoric of ¶17. A's own text contains the clauses that limit the imagination: A 94's three underivable sources, A 119's understanding as the unity of the imagination's synthesis, A 124's apperception added to make the function intellectual, and A 126–127's understanding as lawgiver. B adds the definition, the term *synthesis speciosa* and the productive imagination, as well as removing A 94 and A 115.

The first two parts of the Third Section make five groups of Kantian text load-bearing. The first is the imagination as third source (A 94, A 115, A 124, A 78–79/B 104, A 155/B 194). The second is the root of the two stems (A 15/B 29). The third is the original representation of space and time (A 32/B 48) with *ens imaginarium* (A 291/B 347). The fourth is the schematism of "our understanding" (A 140–142/B 179–181). The fifth is the B-Deduction's language of the understanding as the source of combination (B 130–162). Where the section reports the texts, it reports them exactly, and on two matters it reads them better than its critics allow. It quotes the phrases it had earlier cut and argues with them (§29, 151). And it rightly distinguishes A's transcendental deduction from psychology. Where it builds, it builds on words and against clauses. "Original" is joined to the *originarius* Kant denies us (B 72). *Ens imaginarium* is read as an act of the imagination. "Faculty" is read as "being able". B 133–134 n., which makes apperception and "the understanding itself" the highest point, is read as dissolving logic into the imagination. A 124's "always sensible" is answered while the sentence's first clause, that apperception must be added to make the imagination's function intellectual, is left aside. B 160 n., which derives the unity of pure intuition "as the understanding determines the sensibility", is cited in a footnote and not reported. Two Kantian texts that bear directly on the root thesis are not cited at all. A 649/B 677 entertains imagination as "perhaps even understanding and reason" and classes the idea as a regulative problem. The Typic of the *Critique of Practical Reason* says the moral law has "no faculty but the understanding ... (not the imagination)". The thesis of shrinking back rests on a marginal correction in Kant's copy of A that the B edition did not print (B 103 reads "of the soul"), on the removal of A 94 and A 115, and on the B-Deduction's new attribution of combination to the understanding. It sets aside Kant's own account of the B revision (B xxxvii–xxxviii) and his A-edition reason for subordinating the subjective deduction (A xvi–xvii). It explains the change by a motive, the spell of pure reason, for which there is no text. The verdict of this part is therefore mixed. Heidegger's reading of A as giving the imagination a stronger role than B is supported, and B's renaming is real. His reading of the imagination as the root that Kant saw and fled extends A beyond its own limiting clauses and contests B against its author's account, and the book itself presents it as an interpretation that must "use violence" (§35, 202).

### C. The Transcendental Imagination and the Problem of Human Pure Reason

**¶1–¶2, 171–172.** That the *Critique* concerns from the outset only human pure reason is shown by a "characteristic piece of evidence": the task "How are synthetic judgments a priori possible?" cannot be solved except by considering "the faculties of man" by which he extends his knowledge a priori, since for a reason "of a being in general" (God, a higher spirit) the task is "indeterminate", whereas "as regards man, every knowledge of his consists of concept and intuition"; the sentences come from the essay on the *Progress of Metaphysics*, where Kant had the problem of metaphysics "as a whole" before him; specific human finitude is the problem, not a "case". The *Progress* essay (the Berlin prize question of 1791, published posthumously in 1804) is not in the corpus, so the quotation is reported, not verified. Its date matters to §31. As a text later than B, it counts against the claim that B gave up the human being for finite reason in general. The *Critique* itself says the same in both editions and is not cited: "We can accordingly speak of space, extended beings, and so on, only from the human standpoint" (A 26/B 42), and our way of perceiving "does not necessarily pertain to every being, though to be sure it pertains to every human being" (A 42/B 59). The evidence holds for human finitude. It does not hold for the imagination. By Heidegger's own quotation, human knowledge "consists of concept and intuition", two elements and no third.

**¶3, 172.** Sensibility as receptive intuition belongs to human finitude; as pure sensibility it is an element of the structure of transcendence; "Human pure reason is necessarily a pure sensible reason", sensible in itself and not because it is tied to a body; rather man can "have" a body in a transcendental sense only because transcendence is a priori sensible. "Pure sensible reason" is Heidegger's formula, and it joins what Kant's doctrine of two sources keeps apart. Kant says the two faculties "cannot exchange their functions. The understanding is not capable of intuiting anything, and the senses are not capable of thinking anything. Only from their unification can cognition arise" (A 51/B 75–76). Kant's "unification" is a joining of two. Heidegger's single "sensible reason" presupposes the root he is about to argue for. The order of grounding between sensibility and the body is Heidegger's own; the *Critique* has no transcendental doctrine of the body to set beside it.

**¶4–¶5, 172–173.** If the imagination is the ground of human subjectivity in its unity, it must make possible a pure sensible reason; pure sensibility in its universal meaning is time; is time to stand in original unity with the "I think", the pure I that on the "generally prevailing interpretation" Kant sets against all time? "How in general is this related to time?" (Taft) The universal meaning is Kant's: time is "the *a priori* formal condition of all appearances in general", space only of outer ones (A 33–34/B 50). The question names the obstacle fairly. The "prevailing interpretation" is not only the interpreters'. It is Kant's, and the texts that state it are A 107, A 350, A 381–382 and B 278, which the following sections cite only in part (§33c, §34).

#### §32. The Transcendental Power of Imagination and Its Relation to Time

**¶1, 173.** The imagination was shown to be the origin of pure sensible intuition (§28); in principle it is thereby proved that time "springs from" the transcendental imagination; how time is grounded in it needs an analysis of its own. The section opens on §28's result, which rested on the joined sense of "original" (B 72 against A 32/B 48) and on *ens imaginarium* read as an act of the imagination (A 291/B 347). Whatever §32 adds, the step from imagination to time is only as strong as that reading.

**¶2, 173.** Time "constantly flows" (B 291) as the pure succession of the sequence of nows; pure intuition intuits it non-objectively; intuiting is taking-in-stride of what gives itself, and "pure intuition gives itself what can be taken in stride in the very act of taking-in-stride" (working rendering). The B 291 phrase comes from a sentence with its own argument. Kant writes that for the category of substance "we need an intuition in **space** (of matter), since space alone persistently determines, while time, however, and thus everything that is in inner sense, constantly flows" (B 291). Kant says time flows in order to show that what persists must be found in outer intuition. Heidegger takes the clause as the ordinary concept of time and leaves the argument aside. He returns to the same page in §35 as Kant's "new insight" about space. "Sequence of nows" is Heidegger's term from *Being and Time* (§81). Kant's own wording is that "different times are not simultaneous, but successive" (A 31/B 47). The last clause makes pure intuition a receiving that supplies what it receives. Kant's form of intuition is the condition of receptivity, which "necessarily precedes all intuitions" (A 26/B 42) and lies "ready for it in the mind *a priori*" (A 20/B 34). It is not itself a reception. The re-inscription here prepares §34's thesis that pure receiving is self-affection.

**¶3–¶4, 173–174.** Taking-in-stride is first understood as receiving something present; but pure intuition of the succession of nows cannot be that, since it would "intuit" only the current now, never the sequence and its horizon; strictly not even one now, which has "an essentially unbroken stretch into its just-now and its right-away" (working rendering); pure intuition must give the look of the now so that it looks ahead and back; so the pure intuition of the Aesthetic "cannot from the outset be the taking-in-stride of something 'present'". Kant has an uncited counterpart for the denial of the punctual now: "Points and instants are only boundaries, i.e., mere places of their limitation; ... from mere places, as components that could be given prior to space or time, neither space nor time can be composed" (A 169–170/B 211). Kant's ground is the continuity of magnitudes. Heidegger's ground is a phenomenology of the now with its "just-now" and "right-away", the vocabulary of Husserl's lectures on inner time-consciousness, which Heidegger had edited for publication in 1928. That is a mediation: Kant's Aesthetic is read through Husserl's retention and protention. Kant's continuous time and Heidegger's stretched now agree in denying the point. They disagree on what does the stretching.

**¶5–¶6, 174.** Does it follow that pure intuition is "at bottom" pure imagination? Only insofar as pure intuition itself forms what it takes in; but that this forming should be at once looking, looking-ahead and looking-back "has nothing to do with the transcendental power of imagination!"; "If only Kant himself had not expressly brought out this threefold-unified character of forming in the imagining of the imagination!" (working rendering). Heidegger states the objection in full and answers it with a text from outside the *Critique*. The objection stands as far as the *Critique* goes. No passage of the *Critique* divides the imagination by present, past and future.

**¶7–¶8, 174–175.** In the metaphysics lectures (Pölitz), Kant divides the "forming power" into the faculties of *Abbildung* (representations of present time, *facultas formandi*), *Nachbildung* (past time, *facultas imaginandi*) and *Vorbildung* (future time, *facultas praevidendi*); *Abbildung* means not making a copy but the look taken immediately off the present object. Pölitz's edition (1821) is not in the corpus; the division is reported, not verified. As reported, it classifies representations by the time they represent, and it belongs to empirical psychology's survey of the faculties. Heidegger concedes in the next paragraph that Kant does not speak here of the transcendental imagination. The *Critique*'s definition of the imagination matches only the second member: "the faculty for representing an object even without its presence in intuition" (B 151). A 120 n. adds a present-time role: the imagination is "a necessary ingredient of perception itself". Nothing in the *Critique* gives the imagination the future. Kant's own word for cognizing in advance is "anticipation", and he gives it to a principle of the pure understanding (A 166–167/B 208).

**¶9, 175.** Without Kant's speaking of the transcendental imagination here, it becomes clear that the forming of "imagining" is related to time; pure imagining, which forms its formation from itself, "as in itself relative to time, must first of all form time"; time as pure intuition is "the forming intuiting of what it intuits in one. This gives the full concept of time for the first time" (Taft). The inference runs from an empirical faculty whose products are dated to a pure faculty that produces the dates. That is an extension, and its premise is that the pure imagination is related to time as the empirical one is. The "full concept" joins intuiting and intuited. Kant draws the same distinction differently at B 160–161 n.: the "form of intuition" gives the manifold and the "formal intuition" gives the unity, through a synthesis "as the understanding determines the sensibility". §28 cited that note without reporting its last clause, and §32 again leaves it aside.

**¶10, 175–176.** Pure intuition can form the succession of nows only if it is off-forming, fore-forming and after-forming imagination; time is not a field into which the imagination has strayed; within the horizon where we "reckon with time" it is the sequence of nows, but that is "in no way time in its originality"; "the transcendental power of imagination allows time as sequence of nows to spring forth, and as this letting-spring-forth it is therefore original time" (Taft). Kant has one time, of which "different times are only parts of one and the same time" (A 31–32/B 47). He has no "original time" behind the time of the Aesthetic. The two levels, an original temporality and a derived sequence of nows we reckon with, are *Being and Time*'s (§§65, 78–81). "Letting spring forth" is the gloss §28 gave to *originarius*, the very character Kant denies to our intuition (B 72). §28 restricted it to the claim that pure intuition does not let beings spring forth. Here it returns for time, which is not a being, and the restriction does not apply. The thesis that the imagination is original time is a re-inscription, carried by Kant's word "original" in Heidegger's sense (guard 11).

**¶11, 176.** Can so far-reaching an interpretation be "picked out" of Kant's "few hints"? Given its consequences, it must be grounded more concretely and securely. The concession is exact. The hints so far are a lecture transcript, the word "original" and the word *imaginarium*. §33 is offered as the Kantian ground the claim still lacks.

#### §33. The Inner Temporal Character of the Transcendental Imagination

**¶1, 176–177.** In the first edition the imagination is called the faculty of "synthesis in general"; to show its temporal character we must look where Kant treats synthesis expressly, in the section preparing the deduction, "On the *a priori* grounds for the possibility of experience" (A 95 ff.); its "preliminary reminder" is no dispensable remark but must be kept in view for the deduction and the Schematism. The sentence that makes synthesis in general "the mere effect of the imagination" (A 78/B 103) stands in both editions. "First edition" is true of A 95–130 and not of that sentence, which B kept along with "of the soul" (guard 16). Kant describes the "preliminary reminder" more modestly. He has "found it more advisable to prepare than to instruct the reader" in the following four numbers and to give the systematic exposition only in the third section (A 98). Heidegger's weight on the preparatory text reverses Kant's own ranking of it, as the decision for A's subjective deduction did in §17 (A xvi–xvii).

**¶2–¶4, 177–178.** The three elements of pure knowledge are pure intuition, pure imagination and pure understanding, and their unity is the problem; Kant divides the reminder into the syntheses of apprehension in intuition, reproduction in imagination, recognition in the concept; are they three because there are three elements, or because time appears in them as present, having-been and future, so that the imagination proves to be original time? Kant answers the first question in the same passage: the threefold synthesis "direct[s] us toward three subjective sources of cognition, which make possible even the understanding" (A 97), and later he names them "**sense, imagination,** and **apperception**" (A 115). Kant gives the three syntheses the first reason. Nowhere does he relate them to three tenses. Heidegger's second reason is a proposal, and its terms, "having-been" for the past, are *Being and Time*'s.

**¶5–¶6, 178.** By naming the second mode "reproduction in imagination", Kant already makes the imagination one element among others and not the root: "That is correct"; but the deduction shows it as mediating middle; the root thesis came only from the more original interpretation and "no use may be made of that result here"; the temporal character of the three modes is to give "the last decisive proof". The concession is accurate, and the method is right: the argument from the three syntheses is meant to stand on Kant's text alone. The analysis does not keep this rule. In ¶15 the claim that the pure imagination is an "ingredient" of pure intuition is §28's root result, and the argument uses it.

**¶7–¶10, 178–179.** Four preliminary points. "Synthesis of" apprehension means synthesis in the mode of apprehending. Each part begins from the empirical, to show a corresponding pure synthesis as its condition. The goal, not always clearly formulated, is the modes' inner belonging-together. Kant demands that it be laid "at the ground of what follows" that "our representations ... are subject to time". The first three points read Kant fairly. Kant does pass from each empirical synthesis to an *a priori* one for apprehension (A 99–100) and for reproduction (A 101–102). In the third part he passes from empirical recognition to "transcendental apperception" (A 106–107), not to a pure "recognition". The fourth quotation is compressed. Kant writes that our representations may arise where they will, yet "as modifications of the mind they nevertheless belong to inner sense, and as such all of our cognitions are in the end subjected to the formal condition of inner sense, namely time"; then: "This is a general remark on which one must ground everything that follows" (A 98–99). The ellipsis drops "as modifications of the mind" and "the formal condition of inner sense". Kant subjects representations to time in their status as states of the mind. Heidegger's next question, whether thinking itself is subject to time, needs the unqualified version.

##### a) The Pure Synthesis as Pure Apprehension

**¶11–¶12, 179–180.** In empirical intuition a manifold always shows itself, which could not "be represented as such if the mind did not distinguish the time in the succession of impressions on one another" (A 99); the mind must say in advance "now and now and now" so that impressions can be "run through" and taken together; intuition, as *repraesentatio singularis*, is in itself synthetic and takes a look off the offer of impressions: an immediate off-forming. The quotation is exact. Kant's reason follows it: "for as contained in one moment no representation can ever be anything other than absolute unity" (A 99). The "now and now and now" adds the sequence of nows to Kant's "distinguishing the time" without changing his point. Taking the look "off" the impressions is the *Abbildung* of the lecture (¶7–¶8), now placed in the *Critique*. This is a reconstruction with a gloss.

**¶13–¶14, 180.** There must be a pure synthesis of apprehension, without which we could not have the representation of time; it does not occur within the horizon of time but first forms the now and the sequence of nows; pure intuition is "original receptivity", a taking-in of what it releases from itself; its "offering" is "generative"; pure apprehension is time-forming. Kant's sentence has two subjects, and Heidegger makes them one. Kant: without the synthesis of apprehension "we could have *a priori* neither the representations of space nor of time, since these can be generated only through the synthesis of the manifold that sensibility in its original receptivity provides" (A 100). Sensibility provides; the synthesis generates. Heidegger gives both verbs to pure intuition, so that the receptivity is itself productive. That is §34's self-affection in small, built from Kant's sentence by merging its subjects. The same sentence gives his claim real support. Kant says here, in A, that the representation of time is generated by synthesis, which is the doctrine of B 160–161 n. Kant also says "space" as well as time. The mapping of apprehension onto the present uses only half of Kant's sentence.

**¶15, 180–181.** Kant says: "There is thus an active faculty of the synthesis of this manifold in us, which we call imagination, and whose action exercised immediately upon perceptions I call apprehension" (A 120); so pure apprehension is a mode of the transcendental imagination, which therefore has pure temporal character; but since the pure imagination is an "ingredient" of pure intuition, what Kant next calls "imagination" cannot be the transcendental imagination. The quotation is exact (A-only) and concerns the action "upon perceptions", in the deduction from below. The note Heidegger points to says that "the imagination is a necessary ingredient of perception itself" (A 120 n.). Heidegger's "ingredient of pure intuition" moves the note from perception to pure intuition, and it rests on the §28 result that ¶6 set aside. The distinction between the imagination of the second synthesis and the transcendental imagination is contradicted by A 102. It is in the second synthesis, on account of reproduction, that Kant first introduces the name: "we will also call this faculty the transcendental faculty of the imagination" (A 102).

##### b) The Pure Synthesis as Pure Reproduction

**¶16–¶17, 181–182.** Empirical reproduction represents beings "even without the presence of the object"; this making-present presupposes that the mind can bring back what was earlier represented, a kind of unifying; it can unify only if the mind does not "lose from its thoughts" what is brought back; retaining requires that the mind "distinguish the time" and have the "earlier" and "back then" in view; so the no-longer-now must be capable of being brought back before all experience, in pure reproduction. Kant's empirical reproduction is association, by which "even without the presence of the object, one of these representations brings about a transition of the mind to the other in accordance with a constant rule" (A 100). "Making-present" (*Vergegenwärtigung*) is Husserl's word for it, and the mediation of ¶3–¶4 continues. Kant's pure reproduction has a definite task. If I lost "the preceding representations (the first parts of the line, the preceding parts of time, or the successively represented units) from my thoughts" and did not reproduce them, "no whole representation ... could ever arise" (A 102). Kant's pure reproduction brings back parts of a pure manifold, a line, a stretch of time or a number, so that the whole can be represented. Heidegger's pure reproduction forms the "earlier" as such. The first is a condition of the representation of magnitudes. The second is a horizon of having-been, a re-inscription.

**¶18–¶19, 182.** But is the pure imagination not productive, so that pure reproduction is "a wooden iron"? No: it forms the possibility of reproduction by holding open the horizon of the earlier; it forms having-been, and is pure "after-forming"; a footnote defends Kant's "the reproductive synthesis of the imagination belongs among the transcendental actions of the mind" (A 102) against Riehl's emendation to "productive", and proposes "pure reproductive synthesis" instead. Kant's text is divided on this point, and Heidegger takes one side. For him: Kant asks for "a pure transcendental synthesis of this power, which grounds even the possibility of all experience (as that which the reproducibility of the appearances necessarily presupposes)" (A 101), and calls the reproductive synthesis transcendental (A 102). Against him: "only the **productive synthesis of the imagination** can take place *a priori*; for the **reproductive** synthesis rests on conditions of experience" (A 118). B adds that the reproductive imagination "belongs not in transcendental philosophy but in psychology" (B 152). The footnote says that Kant "usually" calls the empirical imagination reproductive and does not cite A 118, the A-Deduction sentence that motivated Riehl. Heidegger's reading of A 102 is defensible and has A 101 behind it. Within A it contests A 118 without reporting it.

**¶20–¶21, 182–183.** The pure retaining of the "back then" unites itself with the now; "The synthesis of apprehension is therefore inseparably combined with the synthesis of reproduction" (A 102), "for every now is now already just-now"; the imagination, as the faculty of "synthesis in general" in both modes, can be the origin of time as the unity of present and having-been; otherwise "not even the purest and most fundamental representations of space and time could arise" (A 102). Both quotations are exact. Kant's reason for "inseparably" is the generation of a whole representation, which needs both running through and retaining (A 102). "Every now is already just-now" is Heidegger's reason, from the phenomenology of retention. In the second quotation Kant again names space with time.

**¶22, 183.** If time is the threefold-unified whole of present, having-been and future, and Kant adds a third mode, and if all representing, thinking included, is subject to time, the third mode must "form" the future. The inference has two premises that are not Kant's. One is the unqualified "subject to time" of ¶7–¶10. The other is a time with three dimensions. Kant says of time: "It has only one dimension" (A 31/B 47). The threefold-unified whole is the ecstatic temporality of *Being and Time* §65, and the future it requires for the third synthesis is fixed in advance by that structure, not by Kant's text.

##### c) The Pure Synthesis as Pure Recognition

**¶23–¶25, 183–184.** The analysis of the third synthesis is longer than the first two, yet one looks in vain for the future; how can pure thinking, the I of pure apperception, have temporal character, when Kant says "Pure reason, as a merely intelligible faculty, is not subject to the form of time, and hence not to the conditions of temporal succession" (A 551/B 579), and excludes the "at the same time" from the principle of contradiction lest "the proposition [be] affected by the condition of time", since as "a merely logical principle" it "must not limit its claims to temporal relations" (A 152–153/B 191–192)? No wonder nothing temporal is found; but neither conjecture nor a first reading decides. The quotations are exact. The first comes from the resolution of the Third Antinomy, where reason is considered as the cause of actions in its intelligible character. It is a practical locus, used here for the theoretical "I think". Kant's statements about the I of apperception itself are elsewhere and are stronger. "This pure, original, unchanging consciousness I will now name **transcendental apperception**" (A 107). "This I is no more an intuition than it is a concept of any object; rather, it is the mere form of consciousness" (A 382). The I "does not have the least predicate of intuition that, as **persistent**, could serve as the correlate for time-determination in inner sense" (B 278). None of the three is cited.

**¶26–¶28, 184–185.** Kant begins with empirical recognition: "without consciousness that that which we think is the very same as what we thought a moment before, all reproduction in the series of representations would be in vain" (A 103); when the mind returns from the past to the present being, who tells it that this is the same being it left? Does perception not fall apart into single representations, so that what apprehension and reproduction give as one has no place? Is this place made after the fact, or are both syntheses oriented in advance to the being as something present in sameness? The quotation is exact. Kant's next sentence states his problem: the representation "would be a new representation in our current state, which would not belong at all to the act through which it had been gradually generated" (A 103), and his example is counting. Kant's sameness is first that of the act of consciousness across its successive steps. Heidegger moves it to the sameness of the being encountered. Kant reaches the object only in the next step, as the "something in general = X" that makes the unity necessary (A 104–105). The move toward the being is a re-inscription. It anticipates a step Kant takes, but in a different order.

**¶29–¶30, 185–186.** Evidently both syntheses are guided in advance by a uniting of the being with a view to its sameness, which Kant rightly calls synthesis "in the concept": "For this one consciousness [representing of these unities as conceptual representing] is what unifies the manifold that has been successively intuited, and then also reproduced, into one representation" (A 103; the brackets are Heidegger's); so what appears third is the first, which "springs ahead" of the others; Kant names it aptly, for its uniting is a reconnoitring (*Rekognoszieren*); it explores in advance and is "watching out for" (A 126; Taft) what must be held before us as the same. A marginal note qualifies the order: it is the order of the empirical genesis of concepts, which is "still not Kant's goal". Four moves need separating. First, the bracket glosses Kant's "one consciousness" as conceptual representing, where Kant goes on to ground it in transcendental apperception (A 106–107). Second, the priority of the third synthesis is Kant's. "A transcendental ground must therefore be found for the unity of the consciousness in the synthesis of the manifold of all our intuitions" (A 106), and that ground is transcendental apperception (A 107). In Kant, though, that priority belongs to apperception, the faculty the Third Section has been subordinating to the imagination. Heidegger finds Kant's primacy and gives its bearer a new name. Third, Kant's word is *Rekognition*, "recognition". The military sense of *rekognoszieren* is Heidegger's. Fourth, the A 126 phrase (Kant's *durchspähen*, Guyer-Wood "poring through") is said by Kant of the understanding as "the **faculty of rules**": "It is always busy poring through the appearances with the aim of finding some sort of rule in them" (A 126). The paragraph it comes from ends with the understanding as "the lawgiver for nature" (A 126–127; guard 13). Heidegger borrows the understanding's activity to describe the imagination's fore-forming.

**¶31, 186.** Empirical identification presupposes a pure one; as pure reproduction forms the possibility of bringing back, pure recognition must offer the possibility of identifying; it explores not a being but the horizon of "holding-before" as such, and its exploring is the original forming of this holding-before, "that is, of the *future*"; since Kant assigns off-, after- and fore-forming to the empirical imagination, pure fore-forming is an act of the pure imagination. The pure counterpart of recognition in Kant is transcendental apperception (A 106–107). Kant never calls it imagination, and at A 124 he says it "must be added to the pure imagination in order to make its function intellectual". The last step brings the lecture's empirical *facultas praevidendi* (§32) into the pure synthesis and assigns to the imagination what Kant's text assigns to apperception. That contests A 124 without reporting it.

**¶32, 187.** The third mode not only has temporal character but shows a priority over the other two; "What if in this apparently wholly time-averse Kantian analysis of pure synthesis in the concept the most original essence of time came to light: that it temporalizes itself primarily out of the future?" (working rendering; Taft's sentence is garbled here). The thesis is *Being and Time*'s primacy of the future (§65). Heidegger asks it as a question because Kant's text does not state it. The primacy he found in ¶29–¶30 is real in Kant, but it is the primacy of apperception.

**¶33–¶34, 187.** However that may be, the task is solved: if the imagination forms time, "then we cannot avoid the thesis stated above: the transcendental power of imagination is original time" (Taft); time's universal character has been shown, so the imagination can bear the unity of the finite subject as "pure, sensible reason". The verdict claims more than the three analyses gave. In apprehension, Kant's text supports a pure synthesis that generates the representation of time, and of space (A 99–100). In reproduction, it supports a pure synthesis of the imagination (A 101–102), which A 118 restricts. In recognition, Kant's pure ground is apperception. The mapping onto present, having-been and future is Heidegger's throughout. The third member, on which the primacy of the future depends, has no Kantian support, and it takes its key term, "poring through", from the understanding.

**¶35–¶36, 187–188.** But do pure sensibility and pure reason not remain heterogeneous, and pure sensible reason a "nonconcept"? Perhaps the reverse way succeeds: to show that time has the character of selfhood, since time "apart from the subject is nothing" (A 35/B 51), which "indeed implies that in the subject, it is everything" (Taft); but what does "in the subject" mean? Time is not in the subject like cells in the brain; did Kant see only the negative point? Is what the Aesthetic says about time the last word, or a pointer to "the more original essence of time"? Kant's sentence says more than its last clause. "Time is therefore merely a subjective condition of our (human) intuition (which is always sensible, i.e., insofar as we are affected by objects), and in itself, outside the subject, is nothing" (A 35/B 51). It supports the human finitude of ¶1–¶2. It also ties time to our being affected by objects, which §34 must reinterpret as affection by the self. "In the subject it is everything" is Heidegger's inference. In the same paragraph Kant adds that time is "necessarily objective in regard to all appearances" (A 35/B 51), which is its empirical reality. Calling the Aesthetic a provisional first word anticipates §35. In Kant, the doctrine of time is completed in B 67–69 and B 152–158, and both are B texts.

#### §34. Time as Pure Self-Affection and the Temporal Character of the Self

**¶1–¶2, 188–189.** Where Kant first circumscribes the unity of pure knowledge (second stage), he says that space and time "must always affect" the concept of the representations of objects (A 77/B 102); the "concept" is the universal of representing objects, the letting-stand-against; so far time was the horizon in which affections of sense meet us, but now time itself is to affect, and affection is the announcing of a being already at hand; whence does time come, if it affects? Kant's sentence: space and time "belong nevertheless among the conditions of the receptivity of our mind, under which alone it can receive representations of objects, which thus must always also affect the concept of these objects" (A 77/B 102). The subject of "affect" is the conditions of receptivity (*die*, the *Bedingungen*). *Derselben* can refer either to the objects (Guyer-Wood) or to the representations (Heidegger). Kant's point is that the conditions under which objects are received must also determine the concepts of them. That is the Schematism in outline (A 139–140/B 178–179). Kant uses the verb outside its technical sense, an object's acting on our sensibility (A 19/B 33). Heidegger reads the technical sense and so produces his "dark thesis" and its question: what affects without being at hand? The puzzle comes from his reading, not from Kant's text (guard 11).

**¶3–¶5, 189.** Time is pure intuition only by fore-forming the look of succession from itself and holding it toward itself as forming taking-in; it concerns itself with what it forms, without experience; "time is by its essence pure affection of itself"; it is not an affection striking a self at hand but "forms the essence of something like concerning-oneself-with-oneself" (working rendering; Taft "self-activating"); so it forms the essential structure of subjectivity; only on this ground can the finite being be dependent on taking-in-stride. Kant's self-affection is a B doctrine with a specific job. It explains "the paradox ... namely how this presents even ourselves to consciousness only as we appear to ourselves" (B 152–153). Its parties are distinct: "That which determines the inner sense is the understanding", which "under the designation of a **transcendental synthesis of the imagination**" exercises its action "on the **passive** subject, whose **faculty** it is, about which we rightly say that the inner sense is thereby affected" (B 153–154). Heidegger keeps the structure, affection by oneself, and drops the parties. Self-affection becomes the origin of the self instead of the reason the self appears to itself. That radicalizes Kant (guard 7). It also reverses Kant's order. For Kant receptivity to objects (A 19/B 33) is the basic fact of finitude and self-affection a consequence of the form of inner sense. Heidegger grounds receptivity in self-affection.

**¶6, 189–190.** Now the dark sentence: to affect the letting-stand-against purely is to bring before it a Being-in-opposition, before pure apperception, the I itself; time belongs to its inner possibility and, as pure self-affection, forms finite selfhood "in such a way that the self can be something like self-consciousness" (working rendering). Kant separates self-consciousness from self-cognition in the very passage that introduces self-affection. "Consciousness of itself (apperception) is the simple representation of the I". What needs inner sense is the "inner perception of the manifold" given in the subject "without spontaneity" (B 68). In the synthetic unity of apperception "I am conscious of myself not as I appear to myself, nor as I am in myself, but only that I am. This representation is a thinking, not an intuiting" (B 157). For Kant, time is the form under which I cognize myself. It does not form the consciousness that I am. Heidegger's formula contests B 157 without citing it.

**¶7–¶8, 190.** Finitude of knowledge rests on the finitude of intuiting, taking-in; pure knowledge is grounded in receptive intuition; "to take in stride purely means: to be affected free of experience, i.e., to affect oneself"; time as pure self-affection is the finite pure intuition that bears the pure concept, which stands in service to intuition. The sentence about taking in stride purely is the hinge of the section, and Kant does not pass through it. Kant's pure intuition is a form that "can be given in the mind prior to all actual perceptions" (A 26/B 42) and "lie[s] ready" there (A 20/B 34). Being given *a priori* is not in Kant a case of being affected. Heidegger's step requires that whatever is purely received be received from something. Once the object is excluded, the only remaining source is the self. "In service" repeats §5's primacy of intuition (guard 5).

**¶9, 190.** The idea of pure self-affection, which determines the innermost essence of transcendence, was not first introduced in B; there it is only formulated more explicitly, "characteristically already in the Transcendental Aesthetic"; the passage stays dark without the perspective of the stages, within which it is almost "self-evident". Every Kantian statement of self-affection is B-only: B 67–69, added to the Aesthetic in 1787, and B 152–158 in the new Deduction (guard 2). A has no sentence in which the mind affects itself. "Not first introduced in B" claims the idea for A without an A text. The B text that states it most explicitly, B 153–155, is the one §31 used as evidence of B's retreat. There the "transcendental action of the imagination" is the "synthetic influence of the understanding on the inner sense" (B 154), which Heidegger glosses "that is, time" (§31, 164). The Aesthetic's version, which §34 quotes, names no faculty. The fuller version names the understanding. §34 depends on B where §31 condemns it.

**¶10–¶11, 190–191.** Kant: "Now that which, as representation, can precede any act of thinking something is intuition and, if it contains nothing but relations, it is the form of intuition, which, since it does not represent anything except insofar as something is posited in the mind, can be nothing other than the way in which the mind is affected by its own activity, namely this positing of its representation, thus the way it is affected through itself, i.e., it is an inner sense as far as regards its form" (B 67–68); sense means finite intuition, its form pure taking-in; inner sense receives not "from outside" but from the self; self-affection constitutes the mind as mind; a footnote rejects the proposed emendation of "*ihrer* Vorstellung" to "*seiner*", since "*ihrer*" means that what is posited re-presents the "pure relations" of succession. The quotation is exact. The footnote's reading is grammatically sound. The 1787 text reads *ihrer*, whose antecedent is the feminine "form of intuition" (*welche*), and *seiner* would refer the representation to the mind. Kant's preceding sentence states what is posited: "the representations **of outer sense** make up the proper material with which we occupy our mind", and time is the formal condition of "the way in which we place them in mind" (B 67). The positing is the placing of outer representations in time. Heidegger's "pure relations" of succession is true to "if it contains nothing but relations". His "not from outside" leaves out that the material comes from outer sense. Kant's next sentences end with the manifold of inner sense given "without spontaneity" and called "sensibility" (B 68–69).

**¶12, 191.** "At a stroke" it is manifest that time as pure self-affection does not occur "alongside" pure apperception "in the mind", but lies already in pure apperception as the ground of selfhood, and makes the mind a mind. Kant names this identification and rejects it. "It is customary in the systems of psychology to treat **inner sense** as the same as the faculty of **apperception** (which we carefully distinguish)" (B 153). And: "Apperception and its synthetic unity is so far from being the same as the inner sense that the former, rather, as the source of all combination, applies, prior to all sensible intuition of objects in general, to the manifold of **intuitions in general**" (B 154). Since pure inner sense is pure self-affection for Heidegger (§35, 198), his thesis is the one Kant attributes to the psychologists. It contests the sentence that §31 cited.

**¶13–¶14, 191–192.** The pure finite self has temporal character; "Time and the 'I think' no longer stand incompatibly and incomparably at odds; they are the same" (Taft); by interpreting each transcendentally for the first time, Kant brought them into their original sameness "without, to be sure, expressly seeing it as such" (working rendering); can we read past his giving both the same essential predicates? The claim that Kant did not see the sameness fails in one precise respect. Kant considered the identification of inner sense with apperception and refused it (B 153–154). What Heidegger asserts is not an unseen consequence of Kant's interpretation. It is a position Kant rejected in the text where his doctrine of self-affection is stated.

**¶15–¶16, 192.** The I: "The fixed and perduring I (of pure apperception) constitutes the correlate of all of our representations . . ." (A 123, Taft); time: "Time does not elapse . . .", time "which is itself unchanging and perduring" (A 143–144/B 183), and "Time . . . perdures and does not change" (A 182/B 224–225). The predicates are Kant's, and the German repeats: *das stehende und bleibende Ich*, *die selbst unwandelbar und bleibend ist*, *bleibt und wechselt nicht*.
