Taft's "fixed and perduring" and Guyer-Wood's "standing and lasting" both hide that repetition. Three findings qualify it. First, the A 123 sentence is cut where Kant sets the I and time side by side as two. The sentence goes on: "so far as it is merely possible to become conscious of them, and all consciousness belongs to an all-embracing pure apperception just as all sensible intuition as representation belongs to a pure inner intuition, namely that of time" (A 123). Kant's "just as" is a proportion between two correlates, each with its own domain. Second, "A 182, B 224 f." joins an A page to a B-only sentence. "The time ... lasts and does not change" is in the proof added in 1787 (the 1781 text lacks it), and its next sentence is "Now time cannot be perceived by itself", so the substratum must be found in appearances as substance (B 225; guard 2). Third, Kant uses the same pair of predicates in A twice more, both times to deny. Of the I: "one can ... perceive that this representation continually recurs with every thought, but not that it is a standing and abiding intuition, in which thoughts (as variable) would change" (A 350). Of time: "the appearance before outer sense has something standing and abiding in it ... whereas time, which is the only form of our inner intuition, has in it nothing abiding" (A 381). Heidegger cites the Paralogism of substantiality (GA 3, 193 n.: A 348 ff., B 406 ff.; the corpus reads "A 548") and not these two sentences.

**¶17, 192.** One will reply that the coincidence is unsurprising, since Kant means only that neither the I nor time is "in time"; granted, but does it follow that the I is not temporal, or rather that it is so "temporal" that it is time itself? The reply Heidegger states is Kant's position. The alternative he poses is a dilemma Kant's texts do not face, since A 350 and A 381 deny the I the one feature, an abiding intuition, that would make it time-like in Heidegger's sense.

**¶18–¶20, 192–193.** What does it mean that the "standing and lasting" I is the "correlate"? It carries out the letting-stand-against and forms the Being-in-opposition; does Kant mean a soul-substance, he who worked out the paralogism of substantiality? or an eternal I? No: "standing" and "lasting" are not ontic assertions of unchangeability but transcendental determinations; only because the I holds before itself constancy and lasting in general does it form the horizon of sameness in which the objective can be experienced as the same in change; "As I, it forms the correlate of constancy in general" (working rendering). Against a substantialist reading Heidegger reads Kant correctly. The Paralogism forbids inferring substance from the I (A 349–350). His transcendental reading then runs into B 278. That text uses Kant's word "correlate" to deny the I exactly this office: it has not "the least predicate of intuition that, as **persistent**, could serve as the correlate for time-determination in inner sense, as, say, **impenetrability** in matter, as **empirical** intuition, does" (B 278). For Kant, the horizon of constancy in which the objective is the same in change is the schema of substance (A 143–144/B 183). It requires the persistent in outer intuition (B 291; A 381). It is not the I.

**¶21–¶22, 193–194.** This pure procuring of the look of presence is "the essence of time itself as pure intuition"; the "standing and lasting" I means the I as original time forms the letting-stand-against and its horizon; about the I's timelessness and eternity nothing is decided, nor even asked; the I is "standing and lasting" in this sense so long as it is temporal, a finite self. The identification of the I with time as pure intuition is the point Kant denies most often. The I is no intuition, "rather, it is the mere form of consciousness" (A 382). It is "a merely **intellectual** representation of the self-activity of a thinking subject" (B 278). Heidegger's restraint about eternity matches Kant, for whom "the persistence of the soul, merely as an object of inner sense, remains unproved and even unprovable" (B 415).

**¶23–¶24, 194.** If the same predicates are said of time, then the sequence of nows that springs from time cannot determine its full essence; any decision about the I's "temporality" must take pure self-affection as its guide; wherever Kant "with full right" denies temporal character to pure reason and the I, he says only that reason is "not subject to the form of time". The wording is Kant's at A 551/B 579, "not subject to the form of time", and Heidegger reads it exactly. What follows in Kant is the intelligible causality of reason, which "does not arise or start working at a certain time" (A 551/B 579). Taking "form of time" to mean derived time is the distinction of §32 ¶10, carried into Kant's word "form".

**¶25–¶27, 194–195.** In this sense alone is the deletion of "at the same time" from the principle of contradiction justified: with it the principle would be restricted to beings within time; yet "at the same time" need not mean within-time-ness; it expresses the temporal character that belongs as prior "recognition" ("fore-forming") to all identification, which grounds both the possibility and the impossibility of contradiction; Kant, oriented to the non-original essence of time, had to deny it; "Pure sensibility (time) and pure reason are not only homogeneous, they belong together in the unity of the same essence" (working rendering). A footnote cites the Dissertation of 1770 (§14.5), where the principle of contradiction presupposes time, and Mendelssohn's objection of 25 December 1770. Neither is in the corpus. The footnote's point, that Kant's view of the "at the same time" changed between 1770 and 1781, is fair and is not a reproach. The marginal notes refer to Haering's edition of the Duisburg *Nachlass* and to the course of 1935/36. There Heidegger explains the omission in Kant's own terms: "'At the same time' is a time-determination and thereby characterizes the object as temporal, i.e., as object of experience" (GA 41, 175–176). He does not repeat the 1929 claim that it is pure recognition (retrospective). The last sentence answers the problem of the Schematism by abolishing its premise. Kant began from the fact that pure concepts "in comparison with empirical (indeed in general sensible) intuitions, are entirely un-homogeneous" (A 137/B 176). §23 quoted that premise (GA 3, 110). §34 now denies it at the level of pure sensibility and pure reason, and with it the need for a "third thing" in the sense Kant gave it.

#### §35. The Originality of the Laid Ground and the Problem of Metaphysics

**¶1–¶3, 195–196.** Kant's ground-laying comes upon the transcendental imagination as an in-between faculty pressing forward against the two basic sources; the more original interpretation unveiled it as root, and the root as rooted in original time, "the original, threefold-unifying forming of future, past, and present in general" (Taft); the modes of pure synthesis are three "not because they refer to the three elements of pure knowledge" but because, originally one, they make up the temporalizing of time itself; the in-between faculty is "nothing other than original time". The summary drops the qualifications of §33. Kant gives the first reason, three sources (A 97, A 115). Heidegger's reason, three tenses, is offered as a correction of Kant, not a reading of him. The summary's "unveiled" stands for the three analyses whose third member had no Kantian text.

**¶4, 196–197.** Original time makes possible the imagination as "spontaneous receptivity and receptive spontaneity"; only in this unity can pure sensibility as spontaneous receptivity and pure apperception as receptive spontaneity form the unity of a finite pure sensible reason. The chiasmus puts into one faculty what Kant distributes over two, "the receptivity of impressions" and "spontaneity of concepts" (A 50/B 74). Its nearest Kantian counterpart is in B. The imagination "belongs to **sensibility**; but insofar as its synthesis is still an exercise of spontaneity, which is determining and not, like sense, merely determinable", Kant calls it the productive imagination (B 151–152). So the edition Heidegger is about to dismiss gives the imagination both characters in one sentence.

**¶5, 197.** If the imagination is struck out as a basic faculty, as in B, and its function given to the understanding as mere spontaneity, the unity of sensibility and thinking in a finite human reason cannot even be posed as a problem; because the imagination opens the possibility of a ground-laying, "the first edition remains closer to the innermost thrust of the problematic", and "deserves a fundamental priority over the second" (Taft); every reinterpretation of the pure imagination as a function of pure thinking, which "German Idealism" carried further after B, misjudges its essence. This is the verdict deferred at the end of §31, and the ground given is the one §31 asked for, the unity of human pure reason. B does remove A 94 and A 115, the passages on the three sources. It keeps the imagination as belonging to sensibility (B 151), so "struck out as a basic faculty" is more than B's text says. It names the understanding's action on inner sense "transcendental synthesis of the imagination" (B 153–154). The verdict does not address Kant's statement that B changed only the presentation (B xxxvii–xxxviii). The historical claim about German Idealism lies outside this commentary's material.

**¶6–¶7, 197.** Original time lets the pure forming of transcendence happen; from here the five stages and the Schematism are understood retrospectively; "Because transcendence temporalizes itself in original time, the ontological cognitions are 'transcendental time-determinations'" (working rendering). Kant's phrase belongs to the schema, "the transcendental time-determination which, as the schema of the concept of the understanding, mediates the subsumption" (A 139/B 178), and "The schemata are therefore nothing but *a priori* **time-determinations** in accordance with rules" (A 145/B 184). Heidegger extends the phrase from the schemata to ontological cognitions as a whole. Since Kant's principles are the rules of the schematized categories, the extension is defensible. Its ground, "temporalizes itself in original time", is Heidegger's.

**¶8–¶10, 197–198.** Time's central function shows itself in Kant first as the universal form of representing; but Kant stresses it in the analysis of the second synthesis: the ground of reproduction is found "when we consider that appearances are not things in themselves, but are rather the mere play of our representations, which in the end come down to determinations of inner sense" (A 101); this does not mean that beings dissolve into representing; the pure representations of objectivity have "played into one another" within a "play-space" (*Spiel-Raum*) formed by pure determinations of inner sense, that is, by pure self-affection, original time; the horizon of transcendence is the pure schemata as transcendental time-determinations. The quotation is exact. Kant's sentence serves the transcendental idealist premise: because appearances are representations, an *a priori* rule can govern their reproduction (A 101). Heidegger's denial that beings dissolve agrees with Kant's own defence against Berkeley (B 69–71). "Play-space" is Heidegger's pun on Kant's *Spiel*. It is a re-inscription with no basis in Kant's sentence, which says "mere play". Kant also writes "determinations of inner sense", the modifications of the mind of A 98–99. Heidegger makes them "pure" determinations and identifies pure inner sense with self-affection and original time, which is §34's thesis read back into A 101.

**¶11, 198.** Because Kant saw the problem in this perspective and held fast to time's function, he could forgo an express discussion of time in the two ways of the deduction. The A-Deduction's systematic part (A 115–130) does mention time only in passing, as "a pure inner intuition, namely that of time" (A 123–124). The explanation from Kant's having seen the problem in advance is an interpretation of a silence.

**¶12–¶14, 198–199.** In B Kant seems to take back the primacy of time along with the imagination, disowning the Schematism; B inserts a "General remark on the system of principles" (B 288 ff.), which begins: "It is very remarkable that we cannot have insight into the possibility of any thing in accordance with the mere categories, but we must always have available an intuition in order to display the objective reality of the pure concept of the understanding"; that the pure image is time is not said; the next paragraph says "even more remarkable" that "we do not merely need intuitions, but always **outer intuitions**" (B 291); Kant stands before "a new insight": "Space also enters into the pure schematism"; yet the Schematism chapter was not altered; to infer that time's primacy has fallen would be "a complete misinterpretation". Both quotations are exact. The insight was not new. The Schematism of both editions says: "The pure image of all magnitudes (*quantorum*) for outer sense is space; for all objects of the senses in general, it is time" (A 142/B 182). GA 3, 103 quoted that sentence without its first clause (guard 14). The chapter did not need altering, because space was already in it. A, too, places "something standing and abiding" in the appearance before outer sense alone (A 381). The B 291 paragraph argues that substance requires matter in space, because time "constantly flows". §32 took its definition of time as succession from that sentence. Kant also states the purpose of the whole remark: it is "of great importance, not only in order to confirm our preceding refutation of idealism, but, even more, when we come to talk of **self-cognition** from mere inner consciousness ... to indicate to us the limits of the possibility of such a cognition" (B 293–294). The remark is about the dependence of self-cognition on outer intuition, which is the reverse of §34's self constituted by time alone. Heidegger reports it as a remark on the schematism of space and leaves out the purpose Kant gives it.

**¶15–¶17, 200.** One might reply that if transcendence is not grounded in time alone, striking out the imagination is consistent; but pure space is as rooted in the imagination as time is, so long as time means the sequence of nows; with that time, space indeed stands on a level; time is the ground of transcendence as pure self-affection, which is also the condition of forming pure space; there follows only "the positive problem of showing that, like time, space in a certain sense also belongs to the self as something finite, and that this [self], on the grounds of original time to be sure, is essentially 'spatial'" (Taft); B's recognition shows that the Schematism cannot be grasped while time is only the sequence of nows. The concession is real and the task is left open. *Being and Time* §70, which grounds Dasein's spatiality in temporality, is what Heidegger had to offer for it at the time. In "Time and Being" (1962) he said that this attempt "cannot be maintained" (retrospective; not in the corpus). Kant's B text runs in both directions. We cannot represent time "without, in **drawing** a straight line ... attending merely to the action of the synthesis" (B 154). Time "cannot be made representable to us except under the image of a line" (B 155–156). Heidegger takes the first direction, the condition of space in successive synthesis, and leaves aside Kant's second, the need of time for the figure of space.

**¶18, 200–201.** A peculiarity, not accidental, of Kant's ground-laying: what is unveiled in the regress shows itself as forming transcendence, but the faculties, time included, are not determined from this function and remain "within the provisional composition of the first point of departure" (Taft); because Kant had no worked-out interpretation of original time, the schemata as transcendental time-determinations had to remain "so scanty and opaque", for time as a pure sequence of nows offers no way to a "temporal" interpretation of the notions (n. 275, back to §22, 106 f.). This answers §22's question about why Kant's treatment of the schemata is so short. The answer is Heidegger's. Kant's own reason in §22's locus is that he would rather not pause "for a dry and boring analysis" (A 142/B 181). The diagnosis has a Kantian form. Kant licenses understanding an author "even better than he understood himself, since he may not have determined his concept sufficiently and hence sometimes spoke, or even thought, contrary to his own intention" (A 314/B 370). Heidegger's claim that Kant had not determined the concept of time sufficiently meets that condition. GA 3 does not cite the *Critique*'s version of the maxim (see the 1950 preface, ¶2), though it cites the Eberhard version in the next paragraph.

**¶19–¶20, 201–202.** An interpretation that merely repeats what Kant said is no interpretation; what matters is "what it sets before our eyes as still unsaid, in and through what has been said" (Taft); the intention was to bring out what Kant "had wanted to say"; the interpretation makes its own a maxim Kant laid down at the close of his reply to Eberhard: the *Critique* may be "the proper *apologia* for Leibniz" against his admirers, as for older philosophers whom historians make talk nonsense because, neglecting "the key to all interpretations of the pure products of reason on the basis of mere concepts, the critique of reason itself (as the common source for all)", they cannot see "what they had wanted to say" (Taft; Taft garbles the sentence that introduces the quotation). *On a Discovery* (1790) is not in the corpus. The quotation is reported as GA 3 gives it. The maxim's key is named in its own words: the critique of reason. Kant reads Leibniz by the standard of the *Critique*. Heidegger reads the *Critique* by the "idea which shines forth", fundamental ontology. The form of the maxim is kept and its key is exchanged. That is a re-inscription of Kant's hermeneutic principle, and Heidegger states it openly.

**¶21, 202.** To wring from what the words say what they want to say, "every interpretation must necessarily use violence"; such violence cannot be "roving arbitrariness"; the power of an idea that shines ahead must drive and guide it, so that the interpretation can entrust itself to "the concealed inner passion of a work" and be forced to say the unsaid. Kant's maxim at A 314/B 370 works by "compar[ing] the thoughts that an author expresses", and neither of Kant's versions speaks of violence. The word is Heidegger's, and its only safeguard is the illuminating power of the result. The 1950 preface grants that the charge of violence "can indeed be supported by this text" (GA 3, XVII). On the units of this Section, the violence is concentrated at a few points: the joined sense of "original", the lecture's three tenses, the mapping of recognition onto the future, and self-affection as the essence of the self.

**¶22–¶24, 202–203.** Kant's ground-laying leads to the imagination, root of the two stems, which is rooted in original time; the original ground is time; it begins with *metaphysica generalis* and so becomes the question of ontology, of the being of beings (marginal note: "but it is driven by *metaphysica specialis*: theology"); "The question concerning Being ... is the problem of *Being and Time*" (Taft); fundamental ontology is not something new against the old but the attempt to help the ground-laying "by a retrieval to its own more original possibility" (working rendering; Taft garbles the construction). The marginal note is the later Heidegger's, and Kant's text supports it better than the 1929 sentence it corrects: "Metaphysics has as the proper end of its investigation only three ideas: **God**, **freedom, and immortality**" (B 395 n.; cf. B 7). The claim that the problem of the *Critique* is the problem of *Being and Time* is Heidegger's own thesis, stated here as the result of the interpretation. It is a conclusion about his book, not a report of Kant's.

The Third Section's last part makes time the ground of the ground, and it does so by turning to texts the earlier parts barely used. These are the threefold synthesis of A 97–110, the B doctrine of self-affection (B 67–69), the shared predicates of the I and time (A 123, A 143–144/B 183, B 224–225) and the B General Remark (B 288–294). Where Heidegger reconstructs, he is often exact and sometimes better than his critics allow. He is right that Kant asks for a pure transcendental synthesis of reproduction (A 101), against Riehl's emendation. His reading of *ihrer Vorstellung* (B 68) is grammatically sound. He refuses a substantialist reading of the "standing and lasting" I, as Kant's Paralogism does, and he reads A 551/B 579's "not subject to the form of time" to the letter. Kant's text also generates the representation of time through synthesis (A 100), and it gives the unity of consciousness priority over the first two syntheses (A 106–107). Where Heidegger builds, the construction rests on four transfers. Kant's empirical classification of imaginings by tense (Pölitz) is transferred to the pure synthesis. The primacy Kant gives apperception is transferred to the future, and the understanding's "poring through" (A 126) to the imagination. The B doctrine of self-affection is transferred to A and from the self's appearance to the self's essence. Space's role in the Schematism, already present at A 142/B 182, is treated as B's new insight. Against these transfers stand Kant texts that GA 3 does not report. In A, Kant uses "standing and abiding" to deny that the I is such an intuition (A 350) and that inner sense has anything abiding (A 381). He calls the I "the mere form of consciousness" (A 382) and allows a priori only the productive synthesis of the imagination (A 118). In B, he refuses to identify inner sense with apperception (B 153–154), separates the consciousness "that I am" from self-cognition (B 157), and denies the I any persistent predicate that could serve as the "correlate" of time-determination (B 278). He also says the General Remark exists to show that self-cognition depends on outer intuition (B 293–294). Several of Heidegger's best texts are B texts (B 67–68, B 151–152, B 224–225, B 291), though §31 had found B to be the edition of retreat. The verdict on the Third Section as a whole is therefore divided. The book's thesis that human pure reason is the *Critique*'s problem holds in both editions (A 26/B 42, A 42/B 59). Its thesis that the imagination is the root of sensibility and understanding is a defensible extension of A that A's own limiting clauses resist (A 94, A 118, A 124, A 126–127). Its thesis that the root is original time, and that time and the "I think" are the same, is a radicalization of B's self-affection carried out against the texts in which Kant considers and rejects that sameness. §35 calls this violence, under a Kantian maxim whose key Heidegger has exchanged.
